- **Trap:** convicting everyone inside the structure. Guilt is personal, and fear can reduce it: not every silent docker is guilty.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md), [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Subsidiarity and solidarity

**Each principle guards against the other's typical failure, so they are held together, both ways, and neither outranks the other.** *Caritas in Veritate* 58 (Benedict XVI, 29 June 2009): [subsidiarity](#subsidiarity) without [solidarity](#solidarity) gives way to "social privatism"; solidarity without subsidiarity gives way to "paternalist social assistance" that demeans those in need.

| Text | What it says (paraphrased) |
|---|---|
| CA 15 (1991) | the State supports economic life indirectly, according to subsidiarity (favourable conditions), and directly, according to solidarity (defending the weakest, limiting the autonomy of the parties who set working conditions, minimum support for the unemployed): two modes of *the State's own* action |
| CiV 58 (2009) | the rule above; then applied to aid, which must involve local agents and civil society, since aid can lock people into dependence ([6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)) |
| MH 73 (2026, citing CiV 58) | in its own words: subsidiarity cut off from solidarity becomes mere protection of particular interests; solidarity unsupported by subsidiarity becomes welfare that fosters no responsibility |
| MH 46 (2026) | the five principles are to be taken together ([the five principles](#the-five-principles)) |

- **Level: rung 3**: the pairing is taught in encyclicals (CA 15; CiV 58; MH 73).
- **Boss 3(b)** sets the subsidiarity objection against the solidarity argument in its childcare case and asks for the crux; the card gives the pairing, not that crux.

**Principle or application:** that the two principles correct each other is a principle; CA 15's list of State measures and CiV 58's counsel on aid are applications; that aid can breed dependence is an empirical premise.

- **Trap:** "solidarity belongs to the State, subsidiarity to families and firms": misreads CA 15. SRS 39 lays solidarity's duties on the stronger, the weaker and intermediate groups alike, and subsidiarity binds the higher body, the State included.
- **Trap:** ranking one over the other: neither CiV 58 nor MH 73 does, and reading CiV 58 as subordinating subsidiarity inverts the opposite error.
- **Trap:** "social privatism is simply the market's fault": it is subsidiarity cut off from solidarity, whoever does it.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Social friendship

**The family of names for solidarity, old and new: Leo XIII's friendship between classes, Pius XI's social charity, Paul VI's civilization of love, Francis's fraternity and social friendship.**

| Who | Name | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Leo XIII, 1891 | the Church tries "to bind class to class in friendliness and good feeling", so classes are "united in the bonds of friendship, but also in those of brotherly love" | RN 21, 25 (Benziger 1903) |
| Pius XI, 1931 | social justice and [social charity](#social-charity) direct economic life, social charity as the soul of the juridical order | QA 88 (paraphrased) |
| John Paul II, 1991 | what is now called solidarity is Leo's friendship, Pius XI's social charity and Paul VI's "civilization of love" | CA 10 |
| Francis, 2020 | fraternity and "social friendship"; an order whose soul is social charity | *Fratelli Tutti* 99; FT 180, citing QA |

- **Level: rung 3** (FT, encyclical, 3 October 2020; the older texts are encyclicals too).
- John Paul II *defined* solidarity (SRS 38); he did not invent the thing, which T1 of 3.4 traces to 1891.

**Principle or application:** social friendship is successor vocabulary for a rung-3 principle; every aid target, tariff or wage rule offered in its name is an application.

- **Trap:** hearing "friendship" as a feeling: the vocabulary names the virtue SRS 38 defines *against* feeling, "a firm and persevering determination".
- **Trap:** "John Paul II coined solidarity": he defined it; the principle is older.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md)

### Ownership and use

**Aquinas's hinge: managing a thing may rightly be private; its use stays common.** ST II-II q.66 a.2 (English Dominican Province): as to the "power to procure and dispense", it is lawful, even necessary to human life, that man possess property; as to use, he ought to hold external things "not as his own, but as common", ready to share them with those in need. The reply to the first objection calls the division of possessions "not contrary to the natural law, but an addition thereto devised by human reason".

- *Rerum Novarum* 22 quotes the article; *Quadragesimo Anno* 45 calls the result the [twofold character of ownership](#twofold-character-of-ownership).
- Aquinas's three reasons for private management (people tend their own better, affairs run in better order, quarrels are fewer) are **empirical premises** (3.5).
- **In use** (3.5 Ex 1, the Brightwells' hay): the surplus is theirs to manage and may be sold at a fair price, a prudent reserve kept; it is held as common in use, so it must be made available, and holding it back to exploit the drought is forbidden (CCC 2409). The neighbour may not help himself: no life is at stake.
- 1.4 reads the article ([procuring, dispensing and use](#procuring-dispensing-and-use)); a.7 on extreme necessity is [`history-of-political-thought` 2.4](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-04-aquinas-on-kingship-tyranny-and-property.md)'s.
- **Level.** The article as Aquinas's is theology and common teaching, **rung 4**; as restated by an act (RN 22), its content is **rung 3**.

**Principle or application:** the distinction, as the encyclicals restate it, is a principle; Aquinas's reasons for private management are empirical premises.

- **Trap:** "the destination makes everything everyone's, so the poor may simply take": the use is common, not the title, and extreme necessity is narrow.
- **Trap:** "*Rerum Novarum* repeats Aquinas" or "Leo invented a right Aquinas denied": see [secondary natural right](#secondary-natural-right).
- **Boss parts:** a.7 beside GS 69 is Boss 1(b), and RN 22 beside RN 13 and 45 is Boss 2(a); the card does not read either pair.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)

### Secondary natural right

**Francis's phrase: private property is a natural right, but a secondary one, derived from the universal destination of goods** (*Fratelli Tutti* 120, encyclical, 3 October 2020). The phrase is FT 120's; it is not in the *Compendium*.

The tension it sits in, text by text:

| Text | What it says |
|---|---|
| Aquinas, ST II-II q.66 a.2 ad 1 | the division of possessions is "an addition thereto devised by human reason", not contrary to natural law ([`history-of-political-thought` 2.4](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-04-aquinas-on-kingship-tyranny-and-property.md)) |
| RN 6 (1891) | every man has "by nature" the right to possess property as his own (Benziger 1903) |
| CA 30 (1991) | RN affirmed the natural character of the right, which the Church has always defended; it is not absolute |
| LE 14 (1981); CCC 2403 | the right to private property is subordinated to the right of common use; the destination "remains primordial" |
| FT 120 (2020) | a secondary natural right, derived from the destination |
| MH 66 (2026) | property is always subordinate to the destination |

- **Level of the ordering FT 120 restates** (property legitimate *and* subordinate): **rung 3**.
- **Level of the reconciliation** of RN's "by nature" with Aquinas's "addition devised by human reason", usually through his *ius gentium* (q.57 a.3): the theologians' work, **rung 4** ([1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md); 3.5).
- **Level of the dispute** whether FT 120's "secondary" changes RN or restates the old order: **rung 5**.
- **The floor** for every reading: property is a natural right **and** subordinate. Neither side may drop a half.

**Principle or application:** the ordering is a principle; how "secondary" relates to RN is disputed theology, not an application.

- **Trap:** "*Rerum Novarum* repeats Aquinas" (overstates: Aquinas says an addition by human reason, RN says by nature), or "Aquinas taught a natural right to private property" (never write it).
- **Trap:** "Leo invented a right Aquinas denied": also wrong; the reconciliation is rung 4.
- **Trap:** reading "secondary" as a revocable concession of the state: overstates; the floor keeps both halves.
- **Citation:** cite FT 120 (Fratelli Tutti), not the *Compendium*.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)

### Twofold character of ownership

**Ownership is at once individual, for the owner and his family, and social, so that goods meant for all actually serve all.** *Quadragesimo Anno* (Pius XI, 15 May 1931; paraphrased):

- **45:** nature gives the right of ownership so that persons can provide for themselves and their families, and so that goods destined for all serve all.
- **46:** deny the social side and you drift into individualism; deny the individual side, toward collectivism: the "twin rocks of shipwreck".
- **47:** right and use differ. [Commutative justice](#commutative-justice) protects what is owned; using it well falls under other virtues; misuse or non-use does not forfeit the right.
- **49:** public authority may define owners' duties for the common good where natural law has not, but cannot abolish the natural right to own and bequeath.
- **50:** superfluous income; what remains after one's needs and fitting station goes to the poor (with RN 22).
- [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md) teaches it; 3.5 reloads it beside LE 14, which sets the Church's teaching radically apart from collectivism and denies that ownership is "absolute and untouchable".
- **Level: rung 3** (encyclical), a principle owed religious submission.

**Principle or application:** the twofold character is a principle; the detailed duties public authority fixes are applications ("whether these standards are right is prudence", 2.2).

- **Trap:** treating non-use as forfeiture (QA 47; 3.5 Ex 2's idle Quarran estate keeps its title).
- **Trap:** reading QA 49 as licence to abolish ownership: it puts owning and bequeathing beyond the state's power to abolish.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)

### Social mortgage

**John Paul II's image: every private title carries a mortgage, an intrinsically social function grounded in the universal destination of goods.** From his address opening the Puebla conference (28 January 1979, III.4), restated in *Sollicitudo Rei Socialis* 42 (1987), where the option for the poor, the universal destination and the social mortgage stand in one paragraph ([3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)). *Laudato Si'* 93 recalls it ([6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)). *Magnifica Humanitas* 66 (2026): the social function of property is not "a mere theological opinion" but Church doctrine.

- **Level: rung 3** (SRS 42). MH 66 marks its own weight, LG 25's "manner of speaking": that excludes rung 5 without making a definition. Find the act, not the first use (4.1).
- **What it licenses:** overriding a title needs public authority, a common-good reason and fair compensation (3.5 T5): regulation (QA 49; GS 71; CCC 2406) or [expropriation](#expropriation). 6.1 runs it on water permits: real titles that carry the mortgage and yield to regulation for the common good (LS 93).

**Principle or application:** the mortgage is a principle; regulating or overriding a given title is an application for legislators and courts.

- **Trap:** "coined at Puebla": not verified. Say "from John Paul II's Puebla address, restated in SRS 42".
- **Trap:** "property's social function is the Pope's opinion": MH 66 rules it out in so many words.
- **Trap:** reading the mortgage as a lien any private person may foreclose: the poor may not take on their own authority short of extreme necessity (3.5 Ex 2).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Expropriation

**Where the common good requires it, public authority may take extensive, unused or poorly used estates, with compensation reckoned in equity.**

| Text | What it says (paraphrased) |
|---|---|
| GS 71 (1965) | goods pass to public ownership only by competent authority, within the limits of the common good, with fair compensation; where great estates lie idle beside landless majorities, reform is needed "according to the different cases", with compensation reckoned in equity, and redistribution needs the means, education and cooperative facilities to succeed |
| PP 24 (1967) | estates that are extensive, unused or poorly used and impede the general prosperity: the common good "sometimes" demands their expropriation |
| CA 43 (1991) | ownership of productive goods left unused, or used for speculation that curbs others' work, has no justification |
| QA 47 (1931) | the counterweight: misuse or non-use does not by itself destroy the right |

- **3.5 Ex 2, the Quarran estate:** 9,000 idle hectares held for a road to raise their price, beside two hundred landless families. The owner fails his duty (CA 43; PP 24); the families may not seize it on their own authority (not extreme necessity; QA 47); public authority may regulate or expropriate with equitable compensation (GS 71). Whether to expropriate rather than tax or lease, and what compensation is equitable, the principle does not settle.
- **Level.** That the common good *can* require expropriation with compensation is **rung 3**. GS 71's 1965 call for land reform, PP 24's "sometimes", and every decision about an estate, a vacancy tax, a compulsory lease or a compensation formula are applications.
- **Who judges:** legislators and courts.

**Principle or application:** the possibility, with its conditions, is a principle; every use of it is an application, owed serious, respectful consideration.

- **Trap:** "the Church teaches this land tax": PP 24's "sometimes" and GS 71's "according to the different cases" shut that door.
- **Trap:** private seizure of idle land in the destination's name: non-use voids no title, and poverty short of extreme necessity gives no right to take.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)

### Preferential option for the poor

**Where goods and attention are scarce, the poor's claims come first: in each Christian's charity, in the Church's use of goods, and in judging social arrangements. "Preferential" orders claims; it never excludes anyone, and "the poor" is not a class.** The picture (3.6): a mother whose child is in hospital gives that child most of her time for a month and disowns no one.

- **The ground is God's preference, not sociology.** Of the widow and the orphan: "If you hurt them, they will cry out to me, and I will hear their cry" (Exodus 22:23, Douay-Rheims); Christ "being rich he became poor for your sakes" (2 Corinthians 8:9, DR) and names himself in the hungry (Matthew 25:40). The instinct is in *Rerum Novarum* 24: "God Himself seems to incline rather to those who suffer misfortune" (Benziger 1903).

| Year | Text | What it said (paraphrased) | Standing as 3.6 marks it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1968 | Medellín (Latin American bishops), XIV.9 | *preferencia efectiva*, effective preference for the poorest sectors in apostolic effort; not yet the phrase | regional; no act of the universal magisterium |
| 1971 | *Octogesima Adveniens* 23 (Paul VI) | the preferential respect owed the poor | papal usage |
| 1979 | Puebla 1134, 1145 (assembly opened by John Paul II, 28 January) | the phrase *opción preferencial*, credited to Medellín; service of the poor the privileged but not exclusive measure of following Christ | regional; John Paul II's letter commending the document (23 March 1979) is reception, not promotion |
| 1986 | *Libertatis Conscientia* 68 (CDF) | a "love of preference" that excludes no one and may not be cast in partisan sociological or ideological categories | rung 3, light grade |
| 1987 | *Sollicitudo Rei Socialis* 42 | a "special form of primacy" in the exercise of charity, witnessed by the whole tradition, bearing on each Christian's life, social responsibilities and decisions about owning and using goods | **rung 3** (encyclical) |
| 1991 | *Centesimus Annus* 11, 57 | never exclusive or discriminatory; not limited to material poverty (CA 57 names the elderly and the sick) | **rung 3** (encyclical) |
| 2007 | Benedict XVI, address at Aparecida (13 May) | implicit in faith in a God who became poor for us | rung 3 at its lightest |
| 2013 | *Evangelii Gaudium* 198–200 | primarily a "theological category", distinct from every ideology, owed above all as privileged religious care | apostolic exhortation, restating |
| 2025 | *Dilexi Te* 87–101, 114 (Leo XIV; begun by Francis, §3) | reads Medellín, Puebla's "social sin" and Aparecida into a papal text; quotes the 1984 instruction approvingly (§98, as EG 194 had); religious care | apostolic exhortation, restating |
| 2026 | *Magnifica Humanitas* 77–78 | places it inside [social justice](#social-justice), which judges an order by whether its weakest can live with dignity (citing SRS 42) | encyclical, restating |

- **Level: rung 3, at a heavy grade** (repetition across four pontificates, LG 25), fixed by SRS 42 and CA 11, 57 with LC 68. Beneath it lies the duty of mercy to every neighbour in need, **at least rung 3** ([`creation-grace-last-things` 7.2](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)). CCC 2448 (quoting LC 68) locates it; it does not fix it.
- **Two architectures, one teaching:** the *Compendium* (182) files the option under the [universal destination of goods](#universal-destination-of-goods), and SRS 42 states them side by side; MH 78 files it under social justice.
- **Elsewhere:** 4.5 weighs it among the principles that pull apart over an automated benefits system; 6.1 reads *Laudato Si'* 158 (in an unjust world the common good becomes a summons to solidarity and a preferential option for the poorest); 6.4 puts it, never exclusive, in the floor every reading must keep, and makes it the centre of the progressive reading.

**Principle or application:** the option is a principle owed religious submission: it ranks claims, excludes no one and belongs to no class. Medellín's redeployment of clergy, SRS 43's international reforms and every parish budget or policy sold as "for the poor" are applications; why the poor are poor and what lifts them are empirical premises.

- **Trap:** "preferential" slides into "exclusive" when need is great: Puebla 1145, LC 68 and CA 57 all shut that door.
- **Trap:** "a Latin American slogan Rome merely tolerated" or "Marxist by definition": demotes a rung-3 principle (SRS 42; CA 57; MH 78); EG 198 calls it a theological category.
- **Trap:** "the option means backing this party, tax or theory of capitalism": inflates; LC 68 forbids making it a partisan choice. It says whose claims weigh first, not which policy works, which group of poor to serve first, or which economics is right.
- **Trap:** "Medellín made the option Church teaching": Medellín never uses the phrase, and a regional assembly is not the universal magisterium. *Dilexi Te* 90's later summary is no evidence that Medellín used it.

*Lessons:* [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Libertatis Nuntius

**The CDF's 1984 instruction on certain aspects of the "theology of liberation": it took the injustice as given and liberation as a Christian theme, and criticized certain forms that borrow Marxist analysis uncritically.** A doctrinal instruction of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith, 6 August 1984, approved by John Paul II in an audience and ordered published.

| What it affirmed | Where |
|---|---|
| the Gospel liberates, first from sin, then from cultural, economic, social and political slavery | Introduction |
| the yearning for liberation is a principal sign of the times | I.1 |
| the gap between rich and poor is a scandal | I.6 |
| effective action presupposes a scientific grasp of poverty's causes; a borrowed method of analysis must first be critically tested | VII.3 (1.3 cites VII.3–5) |
| Scripture has a real political dimension | X.5 |
| structures that are themselves forms of violence need radical reform | XI.8 |

| What it criticized, in "certain forms" | Where |
|---|---|
| Marxist concepts borrowed uncritically, as if the analysis could be detached from the ideology | VI.10, VII.6–9 |
| class struggle as the law of history, and joining it as charity's demand | VIII.5–8, IX.2, IX.7 |
| salvation reduced to earthly liberation | VI.4, IX.4–5 |
| a "Church of the people" set as a class Church against the hierarchy | IX.11–13 (also X.1) |
| a political rereading that makes the Exodus chiefly a symbol of political liberation, and confuses the poor of Scripture with Marx's proletariat | X.5, X.14; IX.10 |
| its Christological charge | X.9–12: [`christology` 6.4](../christology/lessons/06-04-christology-today.md)'s |

- It names no theologian. XI.11 claims the myth of class struggle slows reform and aggravates poverty (an empirical premise). XI.14: the social teaching gives the main lines of ethical orientation and needs people competent in the sciences to guide action ([disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)). XI.15–16: pastors must look after catechesis in base groups ([base communities](#base-communities)).
- **Reception:** Francis quotes it approvingly (EG 194), and so does Leo XIV (*Dilexi Te* 98).
- **Level: rung 3 at a light grade, plus the weight of what it restates.** It is a *doctrinal* instruction, not the canonical instruction of can. 34 that [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)'s genre table describes. Which theologians fall under its criticisms is an **application**.
- **Citation:** sections restart in each chapter (I–XI); the Introduction is unnumbered; the vatican.va English has no VI.6.
- **Boss 3(d)** asks for a reply to the empty-piety-or-Marxism dilemma using LN and LC; the card lists what each says, not that reply.

**Principle or application:** its rejection of class struggle as the law of history and of salvation reduced to politics is a principle owed religious submission; its application to any named theologian is an application; XI.11 is an empirical premise.

- **Trap:** "Rome condemned liberation theology in 1984": LN addressed "certain forms" and named no one.
- **Trap:** "LN is infallible" or "LN is a dead letter": both misread its level; two popes quote it approvingly.
- **Trap:** reading its criticism of political rereadings as a ban on Scripture's political dimension, which X.5 affirms; the error it names is making that dimension the whole.

*Lessons:* [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)

### Liberation theology

**A Latin American theological movement reading the faith from the side of the poor and of Christian practice; the CDF affirmed its cause and criticized certain forms in 1984 and 1986.**

- **Founding text:** Gustavo Gutiérrez, *Teología de la liberación* (Lima, 1971). On his account, theology is critical reflection on Christian practice in the light of the Word, and liberation runs on three levels: political, historical, and from sin. It drew on dependency theory.
- **The critic:** Michael Novak, *Will It Liberate?* (1986): its economics, built on dependency theory, would deepen poverty; property, enterprise and law liberate the poor.
- **Rome's judgment:** [*Libertatis Nuntius*](#libertatis-nuntius) (1984) and [*Libertatis Conscientia*](#libertatis-conscientia) (1986). The Christology of liberation is [`christology` 6.4](../christology/lessons/06-04-christology-today.md)'s.
- **Level.** Gutiérrez and Novak have **no magisterial level**. LN's and LC's criticisms bind at **rung 3** where they apply; which theologians they touch is an **application**. Dependency theory and Novak's case for enterprise are **empirical premises**, standing or falling on evidence.
- **Boss 3(d)** asks you to state each horn of the dilemma from Gutiérrez and from Novak at full strength; the card gives only the lesson's one-line summaries.

**Principle or application:** the movement is theology (no level); its economic claims are empirical premises; the instructions' criticisms are rung-3 principles where they apply.

- **Trap:** "Rome condemned liberation theology": it criticized certain forms and named no one.
- **Trap:** "the option for the poor is liberation theology's, so Marxist": demotes a rung-3 principle the popes teach in their own acts.

*Lessons:* [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)

### Libertatis Conscientia

**The CDF's 1986 instruction on Christian freedom and liberation: LN's promised positive companion, embracing the cause on the Church's terms.** A doctrinal instruction of the CDF, 22 March 1986, approved by John Paul II in an audience and ordered published; LN had announced it (LC 2).

| LC | What it says (paraphrased) |
|---|---|
| 68 | love of preference for the poor, excluding no one, never expressed in partisan sociological or ideological categories (CCC 2448 quotes it) |
| 69 | [base communities](#base-communities) a source of great hope if they live in communion |
| 71 | salvific liberation first, its social dimension as consequence |
| 77 | unions supported without the theory of class struggle |
| 78 | reform rather than the myth of revolution |
| 79 | armed struggle only as last resort against obvious, prolonged tyranny (citing PP 31); 5.1 cites it beside the conditions of [armed resistance](#armed-resistance) |
| 80 | not for pastors to intervene directly in the political building of society: the laity's task ([disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)) |

- **Level: rung 3 at a light grade, plus the weight of what it restates** (the same as LN). LC 68 restates a principle the encyclicals teach (SRS 42; CA 57).
- *1984 refused a method; 1986 embraced the cause on the Church's terms* (3.6).

**Principle or application:** LC 68's love of preference and LC 79's last-resort rule are principles; whether a given regime meets the tyranny condition is an application.

- **Trap:** reading LC as a retraction of LN: the two are companions, read in the light of each other.
- **Trap:** reading LC 79 as a licence for revolution: the exception sits inside the conditions.

*Lessons:* [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)

### Base communities

**Small ecclesial groups, chiefly of the poor, that read Scripture and live the faith together (*comunidades eclesiales de base*).** *Libertatis Conscientia* 69 calls them a source of great hope for the Church if they live in communion with the local and universal Church. *Libertatis Nuntius* XI.15–16 warns that liberation theses spread in base groups lacking catechetical preparation, and charges pastors with their catechesis.

- **3.6 Ex 2, the Saltgate group** (dock cleaners and laid-off fishermen in Dunlin reading Exodus 3:7): LN criticizes the frame of the leader's handout, the Exodus made chiefly a political symbol and not also the figure of baptism (X.5, X.14; 1 Corinthians 10:1–2), the poor of Scripture made a class (IX.10), the pastor discredited in advance as a class enemy (X.1). It would not suppress the text's political dimension (X.5), a sober analysis of how wages are set (VII.3), a union (LC 77), or the group itself (LC 69).
- **Who judges what:** the pastor, with his bishop, may correct the hermeneutic (LN XI.15–16); whether the wages are unjust takes the books and [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)'s measures; whether to strike is the workers' prudential call ([4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md)).
- **Level:** LC 69 and LN XI.15–16 carry the instructions' level, **rung 3 at a light grade**.

**Principle or application:** that such groups are a hope when they live in communion is the instruction's teaching; whether a given group's reading needs correcting, and how, is a pastoral application.

- **Trap:** reading LN's warning about base groups as a ban on them: LC 69 calls them a hope.
- **Trap:** taking a correction of the hermeneutic for a suppression of the text's political dimension.

*Lessons:* [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)

**Module 4: Work, the economy and the two critiques**

### Laborem Exercens

**John Paul II's encyclical on human work (1981), written for *Rerum Novarum*'s ninetieth anniversary: the text that made work the key to the social doctrine.** An encyclical letter to the bishops, the faithful and all people of good will, signed 14 September 1981. What 4.1 takes from it, by section: the two dimensions of work (§§5–6); economism (§§7, 13); the priority of labour over capital (§§12, 15); ownership in the service of labour, and the participation proposals (§14); the direct and indirect employer and the duty of employment (§§16–18); just remuneration, rest and safe conditions (§19); unions and the strike (§20); the disabled worker (§22) and the emigrant (§23); work as sharing in creation and redemption (§§25–27, citing GS 34). LE 3 calls work "probably" the essential key to the social question; *Magnifica Humanitas* 148 drops the hedge. Later lessons use §§14, 17, 19 ([4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)), §14 ([4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)) and §§3, 6, 18 ([4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)).

- **Level.** What it proposes as principle is authentic ordinary magisterium, **rung 3**, owed religious submission of mind and will (LG 25). Much of it restates GS 67–68, also **rung 3**; CCC 2427–2436 locate it and add no weight. Its root, that every person is made in God's image, is held as revealed (**rung 1**), but nothing in LE is defined.
- **Its own disclaimers.** It is not for the Church to analyse scientifically the consequences of technological change (§1); its account of employers is not a treatise on economics or politics but on their moral side (§19). See [the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers).

**Principle or application:** the principles of 4.1's T1–T8 bind (in §14, the rule, not the proposals). LE marks its own applications: the ownership proposals of §14, socialization "in suitable conditions", which services count as essential and what law secures them, and its hopes for the International Labour Organization (§17). Its empirical premises are §1's forecast of job losses from automation and §17's account of how rich countries and transnational firms set prices.

- **Trap:** "an encyclical is the Pope's opinion, written for Poland". It is rung 3 in its principles, addressed to the whole Church and all people of good will; it is not infallible either.
- **Trap:** "LE 1 admits the Church has no competence in economics". It disclaims scientific analysis of technical change and in the same breath claims the defence of workers' dignity and rights: moral, not technical, competence.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md)

### Objective and subjective dimensions of work

**Every job has a *what* (product, technique, tools) and a *who* (the person doing it), and the who governs the what.** LE 5: the objective dimension is man subduing the earth through farming, industry, services and research. LE 6: the subjective dimension is that the worker is a person in God's image, able to plan and to decide about himself. Work may be ranked objectively, and pay may differ, but its dignity comes first from the one doing it; so work is for man, not man for work (LE 6; CCC 2428 locates it). Work must be organized around the person and his home life, with rest (LE 19; GS 67). **Rung 3** (encyclical), restating GS 67; it rests on [human dignity](#human-dignity), whose root, the image of God, is **rung 1**.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. How a given depot or ward is organized, how many shifts, which roles pay what, is prudence.

- **Trap:** "work may be ranked, so low-ranked workers may be treated as variable inputs". That inference is [economism](#economism) (LE 7, 13): 4.1's Marrin role review, and the Kestrow packing line ("the line decides who works here") in 4.3's Flashback, where LE 22 adds that admitting only the fully functional is a serious discrimination (**rung 3**).
- **Ceded:** the philosophy of the acting person that the subjective dimension presupposes is [`phenomenology-and-personalism` 5.4](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/05-04-action-reveals-the-person.md)–[5.5](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/05-05-integration-participation-and-the-bridge.md); the Church's teaching with it, and its level, is here.
- **In use:** [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md) runs the subjective dimension on automation (Tirrenia's claims handlers treated as inputs).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### Economism

**The error of counting the worker as nothing but a cost: labour valued solely by its economic purpose.** LE 13 defines the error: labour considered solely according to its economic purpose, a practical materialism that makes man an instrument of production. LE 7 describes it in action: work treated as "merchandise" or an impersonal "workforce". The condemnation is **rung 3** (encyclical).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Whether a particular rota or pay scheme commits it is judged case by case.

- **Trap:** "LE 7 condemns capitalism as a system". LE uses the word for this reversal of order, persons treated as instruments, which can recur under any programme or name, collectivism included (§§11–15). Judging the systems is [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)'s work.
- **Define it from §13, not §7:** §7 speaks of economistic thought; the definition is §13.
- **Cousins, cited not taught:** Marx's alienated labour is the rival diagnosis ([`history-of-political-thought` 6.5](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/06-05-marx-from-hegel-to-historical-materialism.md)); LE 13 answers that dialectical materialism also treats man as a by-product of production relations. Polanyi's "fictitious commodity" is the economic historian's version of LE 7's merchandise ([`social-theory` 5.3](../social-theory/lessons/05-03-polanyis-great-transformation.md); [`philosophy-of-economics` 5.1](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/05-01-commodification.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)

### Priority of labour over capital

**In production persons are the cause and things the instruments, so capital must serve labour, under any form of ownership.** LE 12: labour is always the primary efficient cause of production; capital, the whole collection of means of production, is an instrument, and is itself the product of past work. LE calls this a principle the Church has always taught, an evident truth drawn from the whole of human historical experience. LE 15: a postulate of social morality, binding under private or collective ownership. LE 14 draws the consequence for property: productive property may not be held against labour or for possession's sake; its only title, private or public, is that it serves labour; "rigid" capitalism's untouchable private ownership and the a priori abolition of private ownership are both rejected. **Rung 3** (encyclical), restating GS 67.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. The forms that give labour its place (co-ownership, profit-sharing, shareholding) are applications: see [worker participation](#worker-participation).

- **Trap (inflation):** "so the Church teaches that workers must own their firms". The priority holds under any ownership (§15); co-ownership and shareholding are §14's proposals, whose feasibility LE leaves open.
- **Trap (demotion):** "a pious sentiment with no implications for how a firm is run". It has teeth: no title to productive property except service to labour, no "rigid" capitalism (§14), economism condemned (§13).
- **Trap:** "LE 12's appeal to experience makes the priority an empirical claim". "Evident truth" names the ground LE offers; the claim concerns the order of causes and values (persons over things), which no economic dataset could revise.

**Reserved for Boss 4(a).** The premise-by-premise reconstruction of LE's argument, with its premises marked, is the Boss's. This entry gives only what LE says, as 4.1 teaches it.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md)

### Worker participation

**Labour's proper place calls for some sharing by workers in ownership, management or profits, in forms prudence must find.** LE 14 commends joint ownership of the means of work, a share in management or profits, and shareholding by labour, and leaves open whether each can be applied concretely. It does not exclude socializing certain means of production "in suitable conditions", and says mere conversion into State property is not yet true socialization. GS 68 asks for active participation in administration and profits, in forms still to be worked out. QA 65 advises tempering the wage contract with partnership where possible ([4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)); CA 43 teaches worker participation in the firm ([4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)).

- **Level.** The principle behind the proposals, that labour's proper place demands some adaptation of ownership and active participation, is **rung 3** (LE 14; GS 68; CA 43). The forms are **applications** by LE's and QA's own qualifiers.

**Principle or application:** principle that participation is owed; every form, threshold and scheme is an application, owed serious, respectful consideration. An invented bishops' conference proposal to seat an elected worker on every board above 50 staff (4.4 P1) is a *particular* application with no universal weight (*Apostolos Suos* 22).

- **Trap (inflation):** making one form (co-ownership, shareholding, a board seat) binding doctrine, as 4.1's union leaflet does.
- **Trap (over-correction):** "LE rejects worker ownership" or "forbids unions to raise it". §14 commends the proposals, and §20 lets unions seek to correct the ownership system for the common good.
- **In use:** 4.3's Maresh case: LE 14 commends worker ownership, but whether *compulsory* conversion of every firm over 50 workers is proportionate is prudential, and whether cooperatives invest and hire as well is empirical.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md)

### Indirect employer

**Whoever shapes a work contract without signing it, the State first, answers for its terms.** LE 16: the direct employer is the person or institution the worker contracts with. LE 17: the indirect employer is every person, institution, collective agreement and rule that substantially shapes the contract: the State in the first place, then the links between states, trade and treaties included. Its responsibility is less direct but real, and it does not excuse the direct employer. LE 18: its first task is work for all, by coordination rather than centralization; unemployment is an evil in every case, and supporting the unemployed is a duty flowing from the common use of goods. In [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md): the State as indirect employer must see that a full-time wage plus social measures lets a worker found and keep a family (LE 17, 19; CA 15; DR 52). **Rung 3** (encyclicals).

**Principle or application:** the responsibility is principle, owed religious submission. LE fixes no figure: any hours cap, benefit design, minimum or treaty term is an application for legislators, firms and unions. LE 17's hopes for the ILO and FAO are applications; its account of price-setting between rich and poor nations is an empirical premise.

- **Trap:** "no contract with the worker, no responsibility". In 4.1 P2, Marrin, whose delivery windows force a subcontractor's drivers into 65-hour weeks, is an indirect employer under §17.
- **Trap:** "the subcontractor's local law makes it just". The indirect employer, the State included, is itself judged.
- **Trap:** "the indirect employer's share excuses the direct one", or the reverse. Neither clears the other (§17).
- **Trap:** treating an employer's question as a buyer's cooperation in sin ([`moral-theology` 3.5](../moral-theology/lessons/03-05-social-sin-and-cooperation-in-the-sins-of-others.md)). LE asks what an employer owes.
- **Averaging:** a town whose *average* wage beats its budget standard has not discharged the duty while its lowest-paid families fall short (4.4's Flashback).
- **Since 2026:** *Magnifica Humanitas* 151 says unemployment at scale calls on the State's responsibility ([4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md) T9). How generous unemployment support should be is modelled in [`public-economics` 7.2](../public-economics/lessons/07-02-optimal-unemployment-insurance-baily-chetty.md).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)

### Three measures of the wage

**Above the floor, Pius XI weighs three things: the worker's family, what the firm can bear, and everyone else's jobs.** *Quadragesimo Anno* (1931, paraphrased) first rejects every one-rule solution, including labour's claim to the whole value of its product (QA 66–68). Then the [just wage](#just-wage) weighs:

- **(a) the worker and his family:** a wage that supports both (QA 71);
- **(b) the firm's condition:** ruinous wages cannot be demanded, but lack of energy or initiative is no reason to cut pay, and whoever crushes a firm with unjust burdens wrongs its workers (QA 72);
- **(c) the common good:** pay too low or too high causes unemployment (QA 74).

GS 67 adds each person's role and productivity; MM 71 leaves pay neither to the market nor to the stronger. Beneath all three lies RN 45's floor, a frugal and well-behaved wage-earner's living. **Rung 3**, at a heavy grade by LG 25's sign of frequency (encyclicals from 1891 to 2026, and GS 67); none defines (can. 749 §3).

**Principle or application:** the measures are principles, owed religious submission. How they net out for a given firm, the figure, and the method of measuring need are applications. Whether a given rise prices workers out of jobs, the question measure (c) makes relevant, is an empirical premise ([`econometrics` 4.3](../econometrics/lessons/04-03-difference-in-differences.md) estimates such effects).

- **Trap:** dropping QA 72's inertia clause turns "the firm's condition" into a licence to underpay. A plant whose sales fell because its owners neglected to modernize may not cut pay below the floor (4.2 P2).
- **Trap:** "RN 45 is the whole teaching", a floor set by need independent of contribution and agreement, as [`philosophy-of-economics` 5.4](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/05-04-markets-and-desert.md)'s card puts it. True of the floor; above it QA 72, QA 74 and GS 67 weigh the firm, the common good and productivity. The courses agree.
- **Bridge:** QA 66–68's refusal of any single rule is the Catholic reply to taking either the marginal product or the whole product as the wage ([`philosophy-of-economics` 5.4](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/05-04-markets-and-desert.md), not argued here).

**Reserved for Boss 4(b).** Running the three measures on a firm's payroll, with the family-support arithmetic, is the Boss's. 4.2 works only households.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)

### Family wage

**Pay for an adult responsible for a family must suffice to found and keep one, by a single wage or by wage plus social measures.** LE 19 (paraphrased): just remuneration for an adult responsible for a family suffices to establish and maintain it and provide for its future, given either as a single family wage or through social measures such as [family allowances](#family-allowance) matched to dependants. QA 71 asks a wage that supports the worker and his family. CA 15: society and the State must ensure such wage levels, and unions are decisive in negotiating minimums. DR 51–52: social justice is unsatisfied while workers cannot support families. *Rerum Novarum* 46 already names the family as the measure, in the Benziger 1903 English: wages enough "to maintain himself, his wife, and his children in reasonable comfort", so that he can save and become an owner.

- **Level.** The family measure is a principle, **rung 3** (QA 71; LE 19; CA 15; DR 52). The route that closes a household's gap and every figure are **applications**; every figure has **no rung**.
- **Two logics in QA 71** (4.2's description, not a document's term): a family-sufficient wage for every adult worker, and pay that rises with family burdens. QA 71 supports both.
- **Who owes it.** The employer owes the floor in commutative justice; a family shortfall above it falls on the system, social justice and the [indirect employer](#indirect-employer), since QA 71 assigns a shortfall to social justice and LE 19 lets wage and social measures meet the measure together (4.2 P2).
- **The hard edge.** QA 71 and LE 19 picture a father's wage and a mother at home. Yet LE 19 makes allowances an equal route, and GS 67 states the measure with no household model. The wage teaching binds the floor and the family measure; the mother's role belongs to the teaching on the family. LE 19's adult responsible for a family includes a lone parent (4.2 Example 1).

**Principle or application:** principle that work must be able to keep a family; application which route (wage, allowance, both) and which figure.

- **Trap (inflation):** "the Church requires a family wage of every employer", so any remaining gap is the employer's breach. Overstated: the principle is right, the assignment of the duty wrong.
- **Trap (demotion):** calling the family measure an ideal, or "one opinion about labour markets".
- **Trap:** averaging. The measure is owed to each worker and family; an average wage above the standard hides the gaps of the lowest-paid (4.4's Flashback).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)

### Family allowance

**A social payment matched to the number of dependants: LE 19's second route to a family's just remuneration.** LE 19 names family allowances beside the single family wage; QA 71 praises adjusting pay to family burdens. The instrument is an **application** and its size a figure with **no rung**; what it serves, the family measure, is **rung 3**. In 4.2's invented town of Orlanda a child allowance closes a lone parent's gap (Example 1), and fails as prices rise if it is not indexed (P1).

**Principle or application:** application, owed serious, respectful consideration. A Catholic may prefer a higher minimum to a larger allowance, or the reverse, without dissent (GS 43; 4.2 Example 2's two bills).

- **Trap:** an allowance used to justify pay below the floor. The employer's floor binds regardless (RN 45; QA 72); neither bill in 4.2 may cover pay below it.
- **Trap:** "the allowance can go because the average wage beats the standard" (4.4's Flashback): the averaging error. Abolishing an allowance is not itself forbidden; leaving the gap open is.
- **Empirical premise:** whether allowances let employers trim wages. Polanyi laid that charge against Speenhamland, and later research doubts his account ([`social-theory` 5.3](../social-theory/lessons/05-03-polanyis-great-transformation.md)). Wage supplements and tagged benefits are modelled in [`public-economics` 5.4](../public-economics/lessons/05-04-participation-and-the-eitc.md) and [7.3](../public-economics/lessons/07-03-tagging-ordeals-and-in-kind-benefits.md).
- [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) uses the same split: a wage figure and the choice between a higher floor and an allowance are applications.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)

### Divini Redemptoris

**Pius XI's encyclical on atheistic communism (19 March 1937).** DR 10: communism makes the person a "cog-wheel" in the system. DR 58: communism is "intrinsically wrong", and no one who would save Christian civilization may collaborate with it in any undertaking whatsoever. The course also uses DR 51–53 ([4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md): besides commutative justice there is social justice, unsatisfied while workers cannot support their families; an employer helpless under competition must support the institutions that make fair pay possible) and DR 51 and 54 ([2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md): social justice asks of each what the common good requires; the vocational design to be adapted to place and circumstance).

- **Level.** "Intrinsically wrong" is a principle, **rung 3** (encyclical), owed religious submission; **not a definition**. DR 51–53's duties of social justice are **rung 3**.
- **The collaboration ban** is a 1937 directive: 4.3 files it under applications; [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) calls it a [practical directive](#practical-directive), binding in conduct within its competence while in force. PT 160 handed the question back to prudence (whether, when and how far to cooperate). The Holy Office decree of 1 July 1949 is a penalty: law, **no rung** ([`church-history` 9.1](../church-history/lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)).

**Principle or application:** the judgment on communism as a doctrine is principle; the ban was an application, and it lapsed while the principle stands.

- **Trap:** treating the ban as a permanent principle because it sits beside "intrinsically wrong" (4.3 P1).
- **Trap:** stating the ban without its subject. DR 58 addresses it to whoever would save Christian civilization; keep that subject in any paraphrase.
- **Trap:** "PT 159–160 softened the judgment on communism". The philosophy, PT says, does not change; DR 58's judgment stands, and only the directive lapsed ([error and the person who errs](#error-and-the-person-who-errs)). The wider line on socialism is [critique of socialism](#critique-of-socialism).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)

### Subjectivity of society

**Families and intermediate groups are real agents with their own autonomy, not instruments of the State.** CA 13 (1991): the social nature of man is realized in intermediate groups, beginning with the family, each with its own autonomy; John Paul II calls this the "subjectivity" of society, and "Real Socialism" cancelled it together with the individual's. SRS 15 (1987) speaks in the same register of the citizen's "creative subjectivity", which suppressing economic initiative destroys ([right of economic initiative](#right-of-economic-initiative)). **Rung 3** (encyclicals).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission; it stands beside [subsidiarity](#subsidiarity) and [intermediate bodies](#intermediate-bodies). Whether a given plan absorbs a group's own work is judged case by case.

- **In 4.3:** part of socialism's anthropological error (CA 13): with the person, "Real Socialism" cancels the family and the intermediate groups as agents.
- **In 4.4:** beside the right of initiative, as the social side of enterprise.
- **Cite it right:** CA 13 (and CA 46). Not CA 49, which is on charity, volunteering and family policy.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Critique of liberal capitalism

**The market serves; it cannot rule. An economy left to free competition, with labour a commodity and the market made absolute, wrongs the person and the common good.** The line, as 4.3 gives it:

- ***Rerum Novarum* 3** (1891, Benziger 1903): the workers "surrendered, all isolated and helpless, to the hard-heartedness of employers and the greed of unchecked competition".
- ***Quadragesimo Anno* 88** (1931): the error is the belief that free competition directs economic life by itself; competition is useful within limits, but [social justice](#social-justice) and [social charity](#social-charity) must direct it. QA 105–109: [economic dictatorship](#economic-dictatorship).
- ***Populorum Progressio* 26** (1967): an "unbridled liberalism" of profit as chief spur, competition as supreme rule, ownership as absolute; the evils come from these ideas, not from industrialization itself (4.5's Flashback).
- ***Octogesima Adveniens* 26** (1971): no adherence to liberal ideology, any more than to Marxist.
- ***Centesimus Annus* 34, 40** (1991): the free market appears the most efficient instrument for needs backed by purchasing power, but some needs find no place on it and some goods must never be sold; it risks an [idolatry](#idolatry-of-the-market).
- ***Evangelii Gaudium* 53–55** (2013, apostolic exhortation): an economy of exclusion; an "idolatry of money"; [trickle-down theories](#trickle-down-theories) unconfirmed. *Fratelli Tutti* 168 (2020): the market by itself cannot resolve every problem. *Magnifica Humanitas* 158 (2026): promises of automatic prosperity often prove illusory.

- **Level.** The principles are **rung 3**: QA 88, PP 26, OA 26 (an apostolic letter), CA 34's limit on the market and CA 40, EG's principles (an apostolic exhortation). The efficiency claim of CA 34 and the verdicts of EG 54 and MH 158 are empirical premises with **no rung**.

**Principle or application:** principles: the person is never a mere part of the economic mechanism; competition cannot rule economic life alone; some goods are not commodities; economic freedom must be framed by law. Applications: any verdict that a named economy fails [CA 42](#centesimus-annus-42); a tax rate or welfare design. What the critique leaves standing: markets, property, enterprise, profit (4.3's map).

- **Trap:** "the Pope condemned capitalism". It condemns the market *made absolute*; CA 42 answers yes and no.
- **Trap:** "PP 26 condemns profit, the market or capitalism", or "blames industrialization". It condemns each one made absolute (the *main* engine, the *supreme* rule, the *absolute* right) and expressly credits industrialization.
- **Trap (demotion):** "the critique of the market is just the Pope's economics".
- **Cited, not taught:** QA 88's self-directing market is Polanyi's self-regulating market ([`social-theory` 5.3](../social-theory/lessons/05-03-polanyis-great-transformation.md)); Hayek's knowledge argument is its strongest secular defence ([`philosophy-of-economics` 5.5](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/05-05-hayek-knowledge-order-and-the-mirage-of-social-justice.md)); CA 40's unsellable goods meet commodification ([`philosophy-of-economics` 5.1](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/05-01-commodification.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### Economic dictatorship

**Pius XI's name for what unlimited competition produced: power over economic life concentrated in the hands of those who control money and credit.** QA 105–109 (1931, paraphrased): by 1931 an economic dictatorship of trustees controlling credit had replaced free competition, and abroad ran an "internationalism of finance", an "international imperialism" (QA 109). PP 59 (1967), as [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md) reads it: between very unequal nations, unchecked competition can create an economic dictatorship, and free trade is just only when it meets the demands of social justice.

- **Level.** The principle the term serves, that competition cannot direct economic life and social justice and social charity must (QA 88), is **rung 3**. 6.2 marks PP 59's principle, that consent between very unequal parties does not by itself make trade fair, **rung 3**.

**Principle or application:** the subjection of economic power to justice and the common good is principle; calling a given market today an economic dictatorship is a judgment about conditions, an application.

- **Quote it right:** QA 109's English reads "internationalism of finance" or "international imperialism". "International imperialism of money" is PP 26's English, quoting Pius XI.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Idolatry of the market

**The market is a useful instrument that becomes an idol when it is trusted with goods that cannot and must not be bought or sold.** CA 40 (1991): the State must defend the collective goods, natural and human environments, that market forces cannot safeguard; some needs find no place on the market; some goods by their nature cannot and must not be bought or sold. CA 40 also lists the market's real advantages, and warns of the risk of an "idolatry" of the market. EG 55 (2013) extends it to an "idolatry of money". **Rung 3** (encyclical).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Which goods, in a given society, must be kept off the market, and by what law, is prudence.

- **Trap:** "the 'idolatry of the market' is Francis's phrase". It is CA 40 (1991); EG 55 extends it to money.
- **Trap:** quoting the idol without the instrument. CA 40 grants the market's advantages in the same section (it *also* lists them; it does not open with them).
- **Later use:** CA 40's collective goods are cited by MH 108 for data ([data as a shared good](#data-as-a-shared-good)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)

### Trickle-down theories

**The claim that market-driven growth will by itself bring justice and inclusion to the poor. Recent teaching judges it unconfirmed by the facts, and that verdict is empirical, with no rung.** EG 54 (2013, apostolic exhortation, paraphrased): trickle-down theories, which assume that free-market growth will inevitably bring justice and inclusion, have never been confirmed by the facts. FT 168 (2020): such theories offered as the only solution; the market by itself cannot resolve every problem. MH 158 (2026): promises of automatic general prosperity often prove illusory.

- **Level.** The empirical verdict has **no rung**; a Catholic economist may test it without dissent. The moral claim beside it, that the excluded may not be written off, is a principle (**rung 3**, with EG's principles).

**Principle or application:** empirical premise, beside a principle. A principle applied to a false premise yields a mistaken application, not a false principle.

- **Trap (inflation):** "EG 54's verdict binds as doctrine", or giving it a rung because an exhortation states it.
- **Trap (demotion):** "since it binds no one, the paragraph says nothing". EG 53–55's principles stand.
- **Trap:** attaching the word "trickle-down" to MH 158. MH 158 does not use it, and it says "often".

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)

### Centesimus Annus 42

**John Paul II's two-part answer, after communism's fall, to "is capitalism the model?": yes to a market economy inside a strong legal and moral frame; no to economic freedom as an absolute.** As [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) teaches it (CA 42, encyclical, 1 May 1991, paraphrased):

- **The question.** After communism's failure, is capitalism the victorious social system, the goal for the countries rebuilding their economy and society, and the model to propose to the Third World?
- **Yes**, if "capitalism" means an economy that recognizes the fundamental and positive role of business, the market, private property and the resulting responsibility for the means of production, and of free human creativity in the economy; though "business economy", "market economy" or "free economy" are better names.
- **No**, if it means a system in which economic freedom is not circumscribed within a strong juridical framework that places it at the service of human freedom in its totality, a freedom whose core is ethical and religious.
- **The warning.** The section ends against a "radical capitalistic ideology" that leaves the poor to market forces.
- **The later reading.** Leo XIV, citing CA 42, recognizes the market's potential only if it stays subordinate to the moral law and guided by solidarity (MH 39, 2026); [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) adds that MH 39 restates CA's conditional acceptance.

- **Level.** **Rung 3**, read whole with both conditions, as MH 39 reads it. CA 42–43 belong to the floor every reading of the corpus must keep (6.4 T1: economic freedom outside a strong juridical frame is condemned).

**Principle or application:** principle, conditional. Whether a named economy meets the conditions is an application. Whether CA *endorsed* or only *conditionally accepted* the market economy is **rung 5**: see [reception of *Centesimus Annus*](#reception-of-centesimus-annus).

- **Rule:** never quote or paraphrase the yes without both conditions.
- **Trap:** "the Pope condemned capitalism" and "the Church blessed the free market in 1991" each cut a real paragraph loose from its conditions (4.3).
- **Trap:** "Chesterton's 'Capitalism' is what CA 42 accepts" ([6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md)). He means wage-dependence on a small owning class; CA 42 accepts a market economy only within the frame its conditions set.

**Reserved for Boss 4(c).** Diagnosing an editorial that says the encyclical "blessed the free market", step by step, is the Boss's. This entry only states what CA 42 says.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Reception of Centesimus Annus

**Catholics dispute whether *Centesimus Annus* endorsed democratic capitalism or only conditionally accepted the market economy; the question is open (rung 5).**

- **The endorsement reading.** Michael Novak (*The Catholic Ethic and the Spirit of Capitalism*, 1993), Richard John Neuhaus (*Doing Well and Doing Good*, 1992) and George Weigel read CA 42 as accepting Novak's democratic capitalism: a market economy, a democratic polity and a moral-cultural order.
- **The critical reading.** David L. Schindler and the *Communio* school (*Heart of the World, Center of the Church*, 1996) replied that the liberal idea of freedom beneath that system is what CA's last condition, a freedom whose core is ethical and religious, judges.
- **Level.** The readings have **no magisterial level**; whether CA endorsed or only conditionally accepted markets is **rung 5**. MH 39 (2026) reads CA 42 with its conditions, and *Magnifica Humanitas* takes no reading's side (6.4).

**Principle or application:** neither: a dispute among theologians about how to read a rung-3 text. A Catholic may hold either reading, provided he keeps CA 42 whole.

- **Trap:** "the Church endorsed democratic capitalism" (inflation of a reading into teaching); "CA 42 was a pope's opinion" (demotion of the text the readings dispute).
- **Trap:** treating "democratic capitalism" as a magisterial term. It is Novak's. Novak also answered liberation theology's economics (*Will It Liberate?*, 1986; [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)).
- Set up in 4.3; staged as the [Catholic liberal reading](#catholic-liberal-reading) against its critics in [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)

### Catholic liberal reading

**The reading that takes the corpus through *Dignitatis Humanae* and *Centesimus Annus*: the free society, rightly grounded, is where the tradition has been heading.** As [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) T2 gives it:

- [Jacques Maritain](#maritain): *Integral Humanism* (1936) proposed a "new Christendom", pluralist and secular rather than sacral; *Man and the State* (1951) made the state an instrument of the body politic, with citizens of different creeds agreeing in practice on a charter of rights. Postwar Christian democratic movements drew on him.
- [John Courtney Murray](#john-courtney-murray) (*We Hold These Truths*, 1960): the American religion clauses as "articles of peace", not articles of faith; Leo XIII's confessional teaching shaped by the paternal states and anticlerical liberalism of his century; limited constitutional government without competence to judge religious truth.
- Michael Novak, George Weigel and Richard John Neuhaus: CA 42 as accepting democratic capitalism ([reception of *Centesimus Annus*](#reception-of-centesimus-annus), from 4.3). Maritain was himself a sharp critic of bourgeois capitalism, so the reading's economic half is Novak's, not his.
- **Its neighbours** in 6.4: [political integralism](#integralism), the [progressive reading](#progressive-reading), and [distributism](#distributism) as a fourth position.

- **Level.** **No magisterial level**. Its distinctive premise is **rung 5**; its programme (democratic capitalism) is an **application**, which a Catholic may weigh and reject without dissent (GS 43). All three readings stand on one **rung-3** floor (6.4 T1). *Magnifica Humanitas* takes no reading's side, and Leo XIV's addresses that the readings quote (for example his Élysée address of 25 September 2026, cited by liberal and integralist alike) are **rung 3 lightest** at most, never the Pope taking sides.

**Principle or application:** neither a principle nor an application of the Magisterium: a theologians' reading, whose key premise is rung 5 and whose programme is an application.

- **Trap (inflation):** "the Church endorsed democratic capitalism", the inflation 6.4 names for this reading.
- **Trap:** sorting an encyclical by who drafted it. Weigel's proposal to mark *Caritas in Veritate*'s passages gold (Benedict's) and red (the Pontifical Council's) misplaces the weight, which comes from what a passage proposes and how; the act is the Pope's (6.4). The mirror error is "Francis replaced John Paul II": MH 39 restates CA's conditional acceptance.
- **Secular frame:** political liberalism, argued in [`political-philosophy` 4.1](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-01-political-liberalism.md).

**Reserved for Boss 6(c).** Stating the three readings as their best defenders would, and naming the single premise on which each pair disagrees, is the Boss's.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### The firm as a community of persons

**A business is people working together and serving society, not merely a stock of capital earning a return.** CA 35 (1991, paraphrased): a firm's purpose is not simply profit but its existence as a community of persons who meet their own needs while serving the whole of society. CA 43: a business is a "society of persons" as well as a society of capital goods, whether people bring capital or labour; ownership of the means of production is legitimate when it serves useful work. **Rung 3** (encyclical), repeated in CA 35 and 43.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. How a firm is organized, where it builds, whether it closes a plant are the owners' and the board's prudence.

- **Ground:** [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md)'s subjective dimension of work is why a firm is persons before it is capital.
- **Trap (demotion):** "the community-of-persons line is management sentiment" (4.4).
- **Cite it right:** "community of persons" is CA 35; "society of persons" is CA 43.
- **In use:** 4.4 P2 (an invented foundry closure pushed by a majority fund): the teaching permits closing a plant that cannot be saved and forbids closing *merely* to lift returns, without regard to the firm's long-term health or the harm where its capital was earned (CA 35; CiV 40). The trial period and the terms are the board's prudence.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Profit as an indicator

**Profit is a legitimate sign that a firm uses its resources well and meets real needs, but not the only sign of its health.** CA 35: the Church acknowledges profit's legitimate role, since it shows that productive factors were well used and real needs met; but the accounts can be in order while the people, the firm's greatest asset, are humiliated, which is morally inadmissible and in the end also hurts efficiency; other human and moral factors matter at least as much over the long term. 4.4's image: profit is a thermometer, which a doctor must read and must not mistake for the patient.

- **Level.** Profit's legitimacy, its insufficiency as sole indicator, and the inadmissibility of humiliating staff are **rung 3** (encyclical). Beneath them, the dignity humiliation offends is **at least rung 3**, its root, the image of God, **rung 1**. That humiliation eventually costs efficiency is an **empirical premise**, **no rung**, though an encyclical states it.
- **Locators** (no weight of their own): CCC 2424 condemns profit as the exclusive norm and final end of economic activity; CCC 2432 says profits fund investment and secure jobs (its note points to CA 37, not CA 35).

**Principle or application:** principle (legitimate, not sole); what to charge and where to invest are prudence.

- **Trap:** "not the only indicator" read as "profit is suspect". CA 35 begins by acknowledging profit's role.
- **Trap (document misread):** "CA makes profit the only measure of a firm" (4.4 P3). CA 35 says the opposite.
- **Trap (document misread):** "CiV says profit is immoral". CiV 38 commends enterprise that aims higher without rejecting profit; CiV 46 treats profit as a means.
- **Trap:** giving CA 35's efficiency claim rung 3 because an encyclical says it; and, the other way, calling profit-only management licit, which contradicts CA 35.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Right of economic initiative

**Everyone has a right to use their talents in enterprise; it serves both the person and the common good, and economic freedom is not absolute.** SRS 15 (1987): the right of economic initiative serves both the individual and the common good; as "experience shows", suppressing it kills the citizen's "creative subjectivity" and breeds dependence on bureaucracy. CA 32 praises entrepreneurial ability and its virtues. *Magnifica Humanitas* 157 (2026) sets the limit: economic freedom is not absolute but answers to the common good and every person's dignity, and enterprise can be a genuine vocation when it treats creating decent jobs as part of its service to society. CCC 2429 locates it.

- **Level.** The right and its limit are **rung 3** (encyclicals). SRS 15's "experience shows" claim is an **empirical premise**, **no rung**.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Which business to start, and how a law balances initiative against the common good, are prudence.

- **Trap:** reading the right as unlimited (MH 157), or the limit as hostility to enterprise (SRS 15; CA 32).
- **Related:** the [subjectivity of society](#subjectivity-of-society) (CA 13) sits beside it in 4.4. In 4.3, CA 24 traces collectivism's inefficiency to violated rights of initiative and ownership, a historical judgment that CA 3 says is not definitive.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Consumerism

**A culture of consumption that appeals to instinct and aims life at "having" rather than "being".** CA 36 (paraphrased): how needs are defined reflects a picture of the person; consumerism appeals to instinct and creates life-styles directed toward having rather than being; so consumers need educating in the responsible use of their power of choice; and choosing where to invest is a moral and cultural decision, never a neutral one. **Rung 3** (encyclical).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Which campaigns, curricula or rules educate consumers is an application.

- **Cite it right:** the sentence that every purchase is a moral act is CiV 66, not CA 36; CA 36's moral-choice sentence is about *investment* ([purchasing as a moral act](#purchasing-as-a-moral-act)).
- **In use:** 4.4 Example 1 (Harrowgate Packaging): CA 36 and CiV 40 deny that a choice of where to grow is merely technical.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Caritas in Veritate

**Benedict XVI's social encyclical (29 June 2009) on charity in truth and integral development, which puts the logic of gift inside economic life.** 4.4 uses CiV 9 (no technical solutions), 34–39 (the [economy of gift](#economy-of-gift)), 38 and 46 (hybrid forms; the [civil economy](#civil-economy)), 40 ([stakeholders](#stakeholders)) and 66 ([purchasing as a moral act](#purchasing-as-a-moral-act)). Elsewhere the course cites CiV 12 (not two social doctrines, one before the Council and one after; [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md)), 15 (the social doctrine an essential element of evangelization; [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md)), 18 (integral development; [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)), 57 ([subsidiarity](#subsidiarity); [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md)), 58 (subsidiarity and solidarity held together; [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md)), 59 (the natural law as common ground; 1.3) and 67 (a world authority; [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md), 6.2). Finance and credit (CiV 45, 65) belong to [`theology-of-debt` 6.1](../theology-of-debt/lessons/06-01-usury-and-finance-in-modern-teaching.md). *Magnifica Humanitas* 162 (2026) repeats its claim that justice concerns every phase of economic activity.

- **Level.** An encyclical: its principles are **rung 3**; none is framed as a definition, so default down (can. 749 §3). Its institutional proposals (new legal and tax forms, consumer cooperatives, the economy of communion as a model) are **applications**.

**Principle or application:** principles in CiV 34–40, 66; applications in CiV 46's hopes and 66's cooperatives; one empirical premise flagged in 4.4 (CiV 46: plural forms make markets more competitive).

- **Trap:** "CiV defined that Catholic-owned firms must become non-profits or join the economy of communion" (4.4 P3): a level misstated and an application inflated; CiV 46 even calls dividends secondary.
- **Trap (demotion):** dismissing the gift chapter as pious decoration, or "an encyclical about economics binds nobody". The Church disclaims technical solutions (CiV 9), not moral competence.
- **Trap:** sorting its passages by drafter (Weigel's gold and red); the act is the Pope's ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md).

### Purchasing as a moral act

**Every purchase is a moral choice, never merely an economic one, so consumers share social responsibility with firms.** CiV 66 (paraphrased): purchasing is always a moral and not simply an economic act; the consumer has a social responsibility alongside the enterprise's, and consumers need educating in it. The same section commends consumer cooperatives and new ways of marketing goods from deprived areas. The principle is **rung 3** (encyclical); the cooperatives and marketing schemes are **applications**.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission; which goods to buy and which schemes to join are the buyer's prudence.

- **Trap:** reading it as making every purchase from a tainted supply chain a sin. CiV 66 states a responsibility; how far a buyer shares another's wrong is the cooperation apparatus of [`moral-theology` 3.5](../moral-theology/lessons/03-05-social-sin-and-cooperation-in-the-sins-of-others.md) and [4.2](../moral-theology/lessons/04-02-double-effect-and-cooperation-as-catholic-method.md).
- **Secular rival:** Young's social connection model of responsibility for structural injustice ([`political-philosophy` 4.4](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-04-feminist-political-philosophy.md)).
- **Cite it right:** CiV 66, not CA 36 (which is consumerism and investment).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Economy of gift

**Gratuitousness, trust and reciprocity belong inside economic activity itself, not only beside it or once the money is made.** *Caritas in Veritate* (paraphrased):

- **CiV 34:** gift neither replaces justice nor stands beside it as an add-on.
- **CiV 36:** friendship, solidarity and reciprocity belong within economic activity itself, and gratuitousness must find a place in normal business.
- **CiV 37:** economic life needs three logics: contract, just laws with redistribution, and works of gift; justice concerns every phase of economic activity (repeated by MH 162).
- **CiV 38:** three subjects, the market, the State and civil society.
- **CiV 39:** if market and State each keep a monopoly over their own area, society is corroded; yet no law can create gratuitousness.

4.4's picture: the three logics are layers of one economy, not three sectors, and gift runs through all of them. **Rung 3** (encyclical), restated by MH 162.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. The institutions that embody it (the economy of communion, hybrid firms) are applications; and because gratuitousness cannot be legislated, nothing fixes how much a firm should give.

- **Trap (demotion):** "pious decoration".
- **Trap (inflation):** "CiV binds Catholic owners to the economy of communion".
- **Where it stops:** 4.4 Example 2 (Thorncastle Cycle Works, a loss-making social enterprise): gift needs a viable vehicle, and the State may help without absorbing; the mix of prices, hiring and contract is the board's judgment.
