- **Secular test:** Titmuss's gift relationship and the crowding-out cases ([`philosophy-of-economics` 5.1](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/05-01-commodification.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Civil economy

**The broad middle ground between profit and non-profit: firms that use profit as a means to human and social purposes.** CiV 38: the market should let firms with different institutional ends compete on equal terms, so that hybrid forms emerge. CiV 46: between profit and non-profit lies a broad middle area, the "civil economy" and the "economy of communion", firms that treat profit as a means; Benedict hopes such firms find suitable legal and tax forms in every country.

- **Level.** 4.4 sorts this material three ways. The hope for legal and tax forms and the economy of communion as a model are **applications**, owed serious, respectful consideration ("it is hoped" marks them). That more forms of enterprise make markets more competitive (CiV 46) is an **empirical premise**, **no rung**; doubting it is not dissent. What they serve, gift inside economic life, is the rung-3 [economy of gift](#economy-of-gift).

**Principle or application:** application, beside an empirical premise. 4.4's in-words: "for-profit versus non-profit" is too thin a map.

- **Trap:** "CiV requires Catholic owners to become non-profits or join the economy of communion" (inflation, often with a level misstated: "defined").
- **Cite it right:** "hybrid forms" is CiV 38; the civil economy and economy of communion are CiV 46.
- **Forward:** [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md) asks whether cooperatives and the civil economy are the teaching's natural implementation or one proposal among many.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Stakeholders

**Those besides owners who contribute to and depend on a firm, workers, clients, suppliers and the community, to all of whom management must answer.** CiV 40 (paraphrased): management must answer not only to owners but to every stakeholder; capital can do good abroad, but justice asks how the capital was generated and what harm follows if it leaves; relocating just to chase cheaper conditions is wrong, and relocating to exploit is worse. The duty to stakeholders is **rung 3** (encyclical).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission; judging a particular relocation is the owners' prudence.

- **In use:** 4.4 Example 1 (Harrowgate Packaging choosing where to grow): the teaching forbids the option whose savings come from unenforced safety rules and a ban on unions, permits a plant abroad run to home standards or expansion at home, requires the home workforce to count as stakeholders either way, and gives no verdict between the two permitted options.
- **Trap:** reading the duty as forbidding any relocation or closure; CA 35 lets real losses count (see [the firm as a community of persons](#the-firm-as-a-community-of-persons)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)

### Technocratic paradigm

**The habit of letting efficiency, control and profit alone decide, so that technology becomes the measure of everything; 6.1 owns it, 4.5 follows it into AI.** *Laudato Si'* 101, 106–109 (2015), as [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md) T3 gives it: a technocratic paradigm treats reality as formless matter to be mastered, welcomes unlimited growth, and comes to rule economics and politics; technology is never neutral (LS 107); its companion is a [throwaway culture](#throwaway-culture) that discards things and people alike (LS 22). *Laudate Deum* 20–33 (2023, an apostolic exhortation) extends it. *Magnifica Humanitas* 92–93 (2026): AI spreads the paradigm, in which efficiency, control and profit alone decide ([4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md) T1).

- **Level.** The critique of the paradigm is a principle, **rung 3** (encyclical; 6.1). Who holds the power it serves, private and often transnational actors controlling platforms, data and computing (MH 5, 95), is an **empirical premise** (4.5).

**Principle or application:** principle (the critique); its diagnoses of present conditions are empirical; remedies are applications.

- **Trap:** "not neutral" read as "evil". MH calls technology neither hostile to humanity nor evil in itself (§§4, 9). See [AI is not morally neutral](#ai-is-not-morally-neutral).
- **Secular cousin:** Weber's rationalization ([`social-theory` 4.4](../social-theory/lessons/04-04-bureaucracy-rationalization-and-disenchantment.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### AI is not morally neutral

**Every AI system builds choices into what it counts, leaves out and optimizes for, so its design, not only its use, must be judged.** MH 104 (paraphrased): a system builds choices into what it counts, leaves out and optimizes for; if it treats some lives as less worthy, it has built in a criterion contrary to dignity; so discernment must judge design, not only use. MH 4 and 9 prepare it: technology is neither hostile to humanity nor evil in itself, but in practice never neutral, taking on the aims of those who design, fund and use it (compare LS 107). **Rung 3** (encyclical).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Which audits, tests and laws give it effect (MH 106's legal frameworks and oversight) are applications.

- **Trap:** "not morally neutral" means "AI is bad", or "MH banned AI". MH calls technology neither hostile nor evil in itself (§§4, 9); to "disarm" AI is not to reject it (§110); developers' work can share in creation (§111).
- **Trap:** "§106 orders a halt". It asks for prudence, rigorous evaluation and at times a slower pace, under law and independent oversight (4.5 P3).
- **In use:** 4.5 Example 2 (Vasskar's benefit algorithm): a postcode proxy may count some lives for less (§104).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### Responsibility at every stage

**A machine can sort people; only a person can answer for the sorting, so responsibility must be assigned at every stage from design to use.** MH 103: when no one bears an algorithm's judgment, exclusion hides behind a look of objectivity. MH 105: responsibility must be assigned at every stage, from those who design a system to those who use it. MH 107: "alignment" does not suffice when a few choose the values aligned to. **Rung 3** (encyclical): it applies the teaching that only persons are subjects of moral acts ([`moral-theology` 3.5](../moral-theology/lessons/03-05-social-sin-and-cooperation-in-the-sins-of-others.md); *Reconciliatio et Paenitentia* 16).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. The means, legal frameworks and oversight (MH 106) and contestable, inspectable decisions (MH 164), are applications in 4.5's map.

- **Trap (demotion):** "'someone must answer' is a pious hope for engineers".
- **In use:** 4.5 Example 2: MH does not tell Vasskar to bring back its caseworkers; it asks that an official answer for each refusal, that applicants can appeal to a person, that the city can inspect and correct the model, and that discriminatory proxies go (§164). 4.5 P2: where doctors keep the final say, §105 is met.
- **Forward:** no lethal or irreversible decision may be entrusted to a machine (MH 198; [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### Data as a shared good

**Data is made by many, so it falls under the universal destination of goods and should be managed as a regulated shared good, not sold off or handed to a few.** MH 67: patents, algorithms, platforms and data fall under the [universal destination of goods](#universal-destination-of-goods) ([3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)). MH 108: data, made by many people, should not be sold off or left to a few but managed as a regulated shared good (citing CA 40 on collective goods). MH 66: property's social function is Church doctrine, not a mere theological opinion.

- **Level.** That data falls under the universal destination is a principle, **rung 3**. The data regime (MH 108) is an **application**: a Catholic may weigh it and prefer another regime that still honours the principle. That AI tends to strengthen whoever already has money, know-how and data (MH 108) is an **empirical premise**, **no rung**.

**Principle or application:** principle (the scope of the destination); application (the regime).

- **Trap:** "MH requires public ownership of data". It asks regulation and shared management; the form is prudential (4.5 P2).
- **Trap:** "the destination's reach to data is a theologian's curiosity" (4.5 P3). §67 applies a rung-3 principle, and §66 refuses to call property's social function an opinion.
- **In use:** 4.5 P2 (an invented health board granting a firm exclusive, perpetual rights to patient records): the exclusive, perpetual transfer contradicts §§67 and 108; patients and their council must have a voice (§§71–72, 108, 178); AI triage and a private partner are not forbidden.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### Truth as a common good

**Truth in public life belongs to everyone, not to whoever has the power to amplify, so who curates what we see is a common-good question.** MH 132: AI amplifies disinformation, and truthful information does not come from centralized or automated control. MH 134: democracy rests on a shared commitment to the facts. MH 137: truth is a [common good](#common-good), not the property of the powerful; hence an "ecology of communication": transparent rules for selecting content, stronger intermediary bodies and journalism, critical education. **Rung 3** (encyclical), owed religious submission.

**Principle or application:** principle; the communication norms of MH 137, and any law on platforms, are applications; that AI amplifies disinformation (MH 132) is an empirical premise with no rung.

- **In use:** 5.2's Flashback (an invented election and two platform bills): the principle does not carry "Catholics must vote for" either bill, since which law serves it is an application and no one may claim the Church's authority for his own (GS 43). MH's text weighs against centralized automated deletion (§132), and its own means are of the disclosure-and-journalism kind (§137).
- **Trap:** "MH forbids any law on platforms" (§137 asks for norms); "MH requires this bill" (inflation); "truth as a common good is Leo's opinion" (demotion).
- **Related:** democracy and its relation to truth is [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)'s ([democracy and truth](#democracy-and-truth)).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Artificial intelligence and work

**Work stays the key to the social question: the machine may take the drudgery, but jobs may not become the cost to cut, and the transition is steered by social criteria with no single model.**

- **From 4.1 (MH 148–152).** Work is "the essential key" to the social question (MH 148, quoting LE 3, which said "probably"). Reading LE 18, MH 151 calls unemployment a "grave evil" that, at scale, calls on the State. MH 152: relieving arduous, repetitive or dangerous tasks is welcome, but protecting employment must remain the "general rule": profit cannot justify systematically sacrificing jobs, because the person is an end, not a means.
- **The AI side (4.5).** MH 150, quoting *Antiqua et Nova* 67: current approaches can deskill workers and subject them to automated surveillance, and frequently tie them to the machine's pace, so systems must be designed around the person, not performance alone. MH 153: no single model of change or universal remedy. MH 155: unions must reach new kinds of work. MH 156: "one viable path" is social criteria for innovation, with verifiable measures for jobs, retraining and participation alongside each automation, its costs not left on individuals alone.
- **Level.** MH 152's rule is **rung 3**, as a *general rule*, not an exceptionless norm; the principles of T6 restate LE 6 and 18 (**rung 3**). MH 156's social criteria are an **application**; MH 150's deskilling and MH 151's fear of a rapid fall in jobs are **empirical premises**, **no rung**; MH 153 is a disclaimer.

**Principle or application:** principle (the person, not profit, is the end of work; employment protected as the rule); applications (MH 156's path; redistribution and retraining schemes); empirical premises (what current AI does to work).

- **Trap:** "MH 152 bans automation". It welcomes relieving people of arduous or dangerous tasks; "general rule" and "systematically" make it a norm about patterns of decision.
- **Trap (inflation):** "MH obliges every firm to match each automation with verifiable job guarantees". §156 is one viable path beside "no single model" (§153).
- **Trap:** "unemployment is a grave evil" quoted as LE's words. LE 18 says an evil in every case; "grave" is MH 151's reading. And "LE 3 says work is the essential key": LE says "probably".
- **Trap:** treating MH 150's deskilling claim as doctrine. It reports what current approaches can do, in words MH quotes from a dicastery note.
- **In use:** 4.5 Example 1 (Tirrenia, an invented insurer cutting 300 of 420 claims handlers to pay the savings as dividends): the plan's logic fails; one built on redeployment, retraining and human answerability passes; how many and how fast is the firm's prudence, worked out with the union. How generous the State's response to job loss should be is modelled in [`public-economics` 7.2](../public-economics/lessons/07-02-optimal-unemployment-insurance-baily-chetty.md).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### The MH 98 caveat

**The encyclical marks its own empirical layer: anything said about AI may soon be outdated, and everyone, designers included, understands these systems only in part.** MH 98 prefaces the AI chapter with both warnings; present systems are more "cultivated" than built, and no one yet knows how they work inside. MH 97: the encyclical points to existing work on AI, chiefly [*Antiqua et Nova*](#antiqua-et-nova). 4.5's reading: the caveat qualifies **statements about AI**, not statements about persons, and reaches an application only as far as the application rests on such a statement.

- **Level.** The statements it qualifies are **empirical premises**, **no rung**. It leaves MH's principles (responsibility, the universal destination, grace) at **rung 3**.

**Principle or application:** neither: the document's own marker of which register a sentence is in, a document marking its own empirical layer for [the three assents](#the-three-assents).

- **Trap (demotion):** "MH admits none of it binds". That drops the caveat's object.
- **Trap (inflation):** treating MH's descriptions of AI (MH 98's "cultivated", MH 101's energy and water use, MH 150's deskilling) as doctrine.
- **How far it reaches (4.5 P1):** directly to statements about AI's nature and effects; to applications such as §156 and §108's data regime only so far as they rest on those statements; not at all to statements about persons, goods and grace.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

### Antiqua et Nova

**A 2025 Note of two Vatican dicasteries on artificial and human intelligence: MH's main background text on AI, and not papal teaching.** A Note of the Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith and the Dicastery for Culture and Education on the relationship between artificial intelligence and human intelligence, approved by Francis on 14 January 2025 and dated 28 January 2025; MH cites it by the January 14 date. MH 150 quotes AeN 67 on what current approaches can do to workers; [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md) takes AeN 99 on autonomous weapons, which MH 197 takes up.

- **Level.** A dicastery note approved in ordinary form is the dicasteries' act: **rung 3 at a light grade**, plus the weight of what it restates (genres in [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)).

**Principle or application:** whatever it restates keeps its own level; its descriptions of AI are empirical premises; its proposals are applications.

- **Trap:** citing it as "the Pope teaches", or as part of the encyclical. It is a dicastery note; quoting it in MH 150 does not turn its description of AI into doctrine.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)

**Module 5: The political community, peace and the stranger**

### Forms of government

**No form of government is prescribed: the right to rule may take any form that serves the general welfare, and the citizens choose the regime and its holders.** Leo XIII, *Immortale Dei* 4 (1885, Benziger 1903): the right to rule is "not necessarily ... bound up with any special mode of government"; it may take "this or that form, provided only that it be of a nature to insure the general welfare". The next sentence turns from the form to its holders: "whatever be the nature of the government", rulers take God as their "exemplar and law". *Diuturnum* 7 (1881): rule by one or by many may be approved if it is just and for the common advantage. GS 74 (1965) leaves the choice of regime and the appointment of rulers to the citizens' free will. Pius XII's Christmas radio message of 1944 held that democracy can exist in monarchies and in republics alike. That no form is prescribed is **rung 3** (encyclicals; a pastoral constitution).

**Principle or application:** the principle (no form prescribed, every form tested by the general welfare, every holder under God's law) asks religious submission. Which constitution, voting system or electoral calendar a people adopts is application, and the choice is the citizens'. Because that no form is prescribed is itself **rung 3**, "forms are applications" is the corpus's own answer, not one reading's ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) uses it: the three readings divide elsewhere).

- **Trap (two directions):** "*Immortale Dei* shows Leo XIII rejected democracy" and "it shows he held democracy best". The passage neither condemns nor ranks: "this or that form", "provided only".
- **Trap (inflation):** "the Church requires democracy". That inflates CA 46's conditional "inasmuch as" (see [democracy and truth](#democracy-and-truth)) into a mandate the corpus denies (GS 74; ID 4).
- **Trap (text):** the vatican.va English of ID 4 drops the proviso ("to insure the general welfare. But whatever be the nature"). Quote the 1903 print.
- **Designation, not conferral:** rulers may in some cases be chosen by the people, and the choice designates the ruler without conferring the right to rule (*Diuturnum* 6); Leo rejects the view that all power is delegated by the people and revocable at their will (*Diuturnum* 5; cf. PT 78). See [political authority](#political-authority).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)

### Limits of obedience

**A law or command against the moral order does not bind in conscience, and the citizen is obliged, not merely permitted, to refuse it.** PT 51 (1963, citing Acts 5:29): laws against the moral order have no binding force in conscience. Leo XIII, *Sapientiae Christianae* 10 (1890, Benziger 1903): when laws are manifestly at variance with the divine law, "to resist becomes a positive duty, to obey, a crime". CCC 2242 (a locator: Mt 22:21, Acts 5:29, GS 74) states that the citizen is obliged in conscience not to follow directives contrary to the moral order, the fundamental rights of persons or the Gospel. **Rung 3.**

- **The two brakes.** Under an authority that oversteps, citizens must still give what the common good objectively requires, and may defend their rights only within the natural law and the Gospel (GS 74; CCC 2242). And force has its own, much higher, bar: [armed resistance](#armed-resistance).
- **Unwise is not unjust.** A measure judged merely unwise, but within the moral order, still binds (GS 74); the remedy is [participation](#participation): argue, petition, vote (GS 75), and others may disagree "with equal sincerity" (GS 43).

**Principle or application:** the principle (an unjust command does not bind; refusal is a duty) asks religious submission. That a given law is unjust is application. Where a harsh general rule turns unjust in one case the doctrine does not say: that is first the agent's formed conscience about his own act (helped by the cooperation distinctions of [`moral-theology` 4.2](../moral-theology/lessons/04-02-double-effect-and-cooperation-as-catholic-method.md)); how to refuse (openly, accepting the penalty; through the courts; with others; by resignation) is [prudential judgment](#prudential-judgment); changing the law is for legislators and courts.

- **Trap (demotion):** "Romans 13 makes every command binding; obey and protest afterwards". The Leo XIII who called revolt treason (*Immortale Dei* 5) called obedience to a law against God's a crime (SC 10).
- **Trap (mirror):** "any law I dislike is unjust". GS 74: a measure within the moral order binds.
- **Trap:** treating refusal as merely permitted (CCC 2242 obliges it), or dropping SC 10's "manifestly".
- **Ceded:** conscience and its errors are [`moral-theology` 2.4](../moral-theology/lessons/02-04-conscience-its-nature-and-its-errors.md)–[2.5](../moral-theology/lessons/02-05-the-primacy-of-conscience-rightly-understood.md)'s; tyranny and the remedies against it as political thought are [`history-of-political-thought` 2.4](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-04-aquinas-on-kingship-tyranny-and-property.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)

### Armed resistance

**Armed resistance to oppression is legitimate only if all five of the Catechism's conditions hold at once.** CCC 2243: (1) certain, grave and prolonged violation of fundamental rights; (2) all other means of redress exhausted; (3) no worse disorder provoked; (4) well-founded hope of success; (5) no better solution reasonably foreseeable. The paragraph carries no footnote; the list is the Catechism's own formulation. Its nearest magisterial source is *Libertatis Conscientia* 79 (CDF instruction, 1986, approved in ordinary form): armed struggle as a last resort to end an obvious and prolonged tyranny gravely damaging fundamental rights and the common good. Behind that stands PP 31 (1967), whose exception covers only manifest, long-standing tyranny gravely damaging fundamental rights and the common good; [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) marks the exception **rung 3** and PP 31's remark that uprisings breed new injustice an empirical premise. The conditions are **rung 3**; LC 79 is **rung 3, light grade**. Nothing here is defined.

**Principle or application:** the conditions are principle, owed religious submission. Whether a given rising meets them is application, and two of the five (no worse disorder; hope of success) are forecasts, that is, [empirical premises](#empirical-premise). So the principle can rule a rising out now without deciding for later.

- **Trap:** "any unjust law licenses force". A single unjust order, with courts and other remedies still open, fails at least "prolonged" and "other means exhausted". Refusing the order ([limits of obedience](#limits-of-obedience)) is one thing; taking up arms is another.
- **Trap (level):** "CCC 2243 defines when revolution is just". The Catechism locates; the conditions are rung 3.
- **Ceded:** Aquinas on tyranny is [`history-of-political-thought` 2.4](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-04-aquinas-on-kingship-tyranny-and-property.md)'s, not re-run here; force between states is [just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria) (5.3).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)

### Participation

**Taking part in promoting the common good is a duty rooted in dignity: first in one's own responsibilities, and as far as possible in public life, the vote included.** GS 75 (1965): citizens have the right and the duty to use their free vote for the common good. CCC 1913–1915 locate participation as an obligation inherent in the dignity of the person. *Christifideles Laici* 42 (post-synodal apostolic exhortation, 1988): every person has the right and the duty to take part in public life. PT 73 (1963): the right to take part in public affairs follows from the person's dignity. **Rung 3.**

**Principle or application:** the duty to take part asks religious submission. Which party or candidate, and which voting rules a country should have, are applications. A bishops' conference's call for a voting rule is particular teaching, authentic in its territory and never universal ([bishops' conference teaching](#bishops-conference-teaching)), owed serious consideration; a claim about the rule's effect on turnout is an [empirical premise](#empirical-premise).

- **No Catholic party is owed.** A plurality of parties in which Catholics may serve is legitimate because many political choices are contingent (CDF Doctrinal Note, 2002, §3; GS 43), but no appeal to pluralism covers a policy that undermines fundamental ethical requirements (Note §5). The Note is a CDF act approved in ordinary form: **rung 3, light grade**.
- **Trap (inflation):** "Catholics must vote for a Catholic party wherever one stands". Plurality is legitimate; no party carries the Church's authority (GS 43).
- **Trap (demotion):** "the Church is no party, so Catholics should stay out of politics". GS 75 makes the vote a duty; ordering society justly is directly the laity's task ([the lay mission in temporal affairs](#the-lay-mission-in-temporal-affairs)).
- **Ceded:** the laity's secular character is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-03-the-laity-charisms-and-the-sensus-fidei.md)'s; religious parties as political actors are [`comparative-politics`](../comparative-politics/syllabus.md) 6.4's.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)

### Democracy and truth

**The Church values democracy for what it secures (participation, accountability, peaceful replacement of rulers), on condition of the rule of law and a correct conception of the person; a majority does not fix the truth.** *Centesimus Annus* 46 (1991, paraphrased): the Church values democracy "inasmuch as" it ensures citizens' participation and lets the governed elect their rulers, hold them to account and replace them peacefully; she cannot encourage narrow ruling groups that usurp the State's power. Authentic democracy is possible only in a State ruled by law and on a correct conception of the person. CA 46 rejects agnosticism and sceptical relativism as democracy's philosophy, and the claim that truth is fixed by majorities: a democracy without values easily turns into "open or thinly disguised totalitarianism". The same section also rejects a fanaticism that would impose its own idea of truth. The CDF Doctrinal Note (2002, §3) and *Magnifica Humanitas* 39 (2026) restate it. Pius XII's 1944 Christmas radio message had already called democracy possible in monarchies and republics alike. **Rung 3**, a conditional principle; Pius XII's radio message is **rung 3 at its lightest**, at most.

**Principle or application:** the conditional valuation is principle, owed religious submission. That a given regime meets CA 46's conditions, or whether an emergency justifies a short postponement of an election, is application and fact.

- **Trap (inflation):** "the Church teaches that democracy is the only legitimate regime". "Inasmuch as" is a condition, not a mandate; no form is prescribed ([forms of government](#forms-of-government)).
- **Trap (dropping the conditions):** "whatever a democratic majority enacts binds, because the people are the source of authority". Authority binds only within the moral order (GS 74); no will of individuals or groups is the sole source of its binding force (PT 78; *Diuturnum* 5).
- **Trap (demotion):** "CA 46 is one pope's view of democracy". It is rung 3, restated by the 2002 Note and MH 39.
- **Trap (level):** "Pius XII's 1944 message made democracy the form the Church requires". A radio message is rung 3 at its lightest, at most, and defines nothing.
- **Bridge:** [`political-philosophy` 5.1](../political-philosophy/lessons/05-01-why-democracy.md) sorts justifications of democracy; CA 46 values it both for what it secures (accountability) and for what dignity requires (participation, PT 73), and refuses to let a vote fix truth. CA 13's anthropological error ([4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)) is what CA 44–46 turn into the critique of totalitarianism and of a democracy without values.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)

### Human ecology

**The natural and moral structure of the human person that social decisions must respect; its first and fundamental structure is the family founded on marriage.** CA 38 (1991) names the moral conditions for an authentic "human ecology": man must respect the natural and moral structure with which he has been endowed. CA 39 calls the family founded on marriage the first and fundamental structure for human ecology. The "first and vital cell" of society is *Apostolicam Actuositatem* 11 (conciliar decree, 1965), quoted by the *Compendium* 211 and *Caritas in Veritate* 44; *Magnifica Humanitas* 165 (2026) calls the family the fundamental and irreplaceable cell of every community organization. **Rung 3.**

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Which family policies serve it (allowances, housing, working hours) are applications; see the [family wage](#family-wage) and [family allowance](#family-allowance).

- **Trap (attribution):** "*Centesimus Annus* calls the family the first cell of society". CA's words are "human ecology" and the family as its first and fundamental structure; the "first and vital cell" is AA 11.
- **Trap:** "CA's human ecology is Robert George's moral ecology". George's term ([`political-philosophy` 4.3](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-03-perfectionism-and-the-common-good.md)) serves a perfectionist case for morals legislation; CA's names the structure of the person, family first, that policy must respect. Neighbours, not synonyms.
- **Later use:** *Laudato Si'* folds a human ecology that respects the moral law in our nature and bodies into [integral ecology](#integral-ecology) (LS 147–155; [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md)

### Dignitatis Humanae

**Vatican II's Declaration on Religious Freedom, promulgated by Paul VI on 7 December 1965 (the same day as *Gaudium et Spes*): every person's right to immunity from coercion in religious matters, within just public order.** A conciliar declaration whose act formula is "This Vatican Council declares" (DH 2); the genre did not cap its weight ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)). John Courtney Murray shaped the text, which passed 2,308 to 70 ([`church-history` 9.2](../church-history/lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)). What 5.2 takes from it, by section:

| Section | What it says (paraphrased) | Level as 5.2 marks it |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The one true religion subsists in the Catholic Church; persons are bound to seek the truth; religious freedom concerns immunity in civil society, so it leaves untouched the traditional doctrine on the moral duty of persons and societies toward the true religion | the duty: **at least rung 3** ([duty of societies](#duty-of-societies-toward-the-true-religion)) |
| 2 | The right: immunity from coercion by any human power, privately or publicly, alone or with others, within due limits; grounded in the dignity of the person, known by revelation and reason; to become a civil right | **rung 3** ([religious liberty](#religious-liberty)) |
| 3 | The duty and right to seek truth, met as persons only free of coercion; government exceeds its competence if it commands or forbids religious acts; it should favour religious life | **rung 3** |
| 4–5 | Communities (worship, self-government, their own ministers, buildings, public teaching; dishonourable persuasion an abuse) and parents (religious education, choice of schools) share the right | **rung 3** |
| 6 | Government safeguards the right; special civil recognition of one community is permitted if everyone's religious freedom is effective, with equality before the law | conditional permission: **application** |
| 7 | The limit: [just public order](#just-public-order) | that a moral limit exists: **rung 3**; where it falls: application |
| 9 | Revelation does not affirm the right in so many words, but discloses the dignity of the person in full | cited for the ground (with DH 2–3, 10) |
| 10 | The act of faith is free by nature, so no one may be forced to embrace the faith | **at least rung 3**; its defined neighbour, *Dei Filius* (1870) on faith's assent not being necessitated by rational argument, is narrower: **rung 1** |
| 12 | Admits ways of acting in the Church's history hardly in accord with the Gospel, while the doctrine that no one is to be coerced into faith has always stood firm | cited, not levelled |
| 15 | Most constitutions already declare the right; present conditions make a constitutional guarantee especially urgent | the report: **empirical premise**, no rung; the urgency: **application** |

- **Level of the core.** **Rung 3.** Some theologians read the core as definitive; that is not manifest, so default down (can. 749 §3). Development or reversal of Leo XIII: **rung 5**, freely disputed.
- **Restated.** *[Magnifica Humanitas](#magnifica-humanitas)* 34 (2026) restates the right, exercised "privately and publicly": **rung 3**.
- **The readings.** Murray: a juridical principle, government's incompetence in religion, with Leo XIII answering conditions now gone. Benedict XVI's [hermeneutic of reform](#hermeneutic-of-reform), developed by Rhonheimer. [Thomas Pink](#thomas-pink): DH addresses the state's coercive authority as a civil authority, not the Church's over the baptized. All three: **no magisterial level**.

**Principle or application:** DH 1–3 and 10, and that a moral limit exists (DH 7), are principles, owed religious submission. DH 6's conditional permission and DH 15's urgency judgment are the Council's applications, owed serious consideration; where public order falls in a case is left to political prudence (CCC 2109). DH 15's count of constitutions is an empirical premise. The document gives a right, a ground, a limit and a criterion, not case-by-case lines or a constitutional model ([the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)): an established church with freedom for all and strict separation both fit.

- **Trap (both bloggers):** "Vatican II made the Church liberal" and "it changed nothing". DH teaches a civil immunity and leaves the duty toward the true religion untouched; on any reading the nineteenth-century texts are hard to square with DH 2.
- **Trap (level):** "DH defined religious liberty". Rung 3; a definition is never presumed.
- **Trap (inflation):** "DH requires a secular state". DH 6 permits recognition on conditions.
- **Trap (demotion):** "DH binds no one's mind" ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)). Its core is a rung-3 act; a reading that refuses DH 2 any assent demotes it.
- **Compared, never merged:** the US First Amendment limits government and forbids an establishment, which DH 6 permits ([`constitutional-law`](../constitutional-law/syllabus.md) Module 7).

**Reserved for Boss 5.** The close reading of *Immortale Dei* 36 against DH is Boss 5(a)'s; stating Pink's and Murray's readings at full strength, naming the crux between them and arguing which fits DH's text is Boss 5(c)'s. Whether 1888 to 1965 is development or reversal is [`fundamental-theology`](../fundamental-theology/syllabus.md) Boss 5's development case, run with Newman's notes ([`fundamental-theology` 5.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-02-newmans-theory-of-development.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Religious liberty

**Every person's right not to be forced to act against his religious convictions, or stopped from acting on them, within just public order: an immunity owed to persons for what they are, held even by those who use it badly.** DH 2: immunity from coercion by individuals, groups or any human power, privately or publicly, alone or with others, within due limits; it is to become a civil right in constitutional law. Communities share it (DH 4: public worship, self-government, choosing and appointing their own ministers, buildings, funds, public teaching; dishonourable persuasion, especially of the poor or uneducated, is an abuse of the right), and so do parents (DH 5: religious education, free choice of schools).

- **The ground (DH 2–3, 10).** The person's dignity, known by revelation and by reason. Persons are bound to seek the truth, above all about God, and can do so as persons only when free from coercion; so the right lies in their nature, not in their dispositions, and persists in those who do not seek. Government exceeds its competence if it commands or forbids religious acts (DH 3). The act of faith is free by nature (DH 10). The duty to seek truth is the reason for the freedom, not its rival. Faith as a free assent is [`fundamental-theology` 2.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/02-01-the-act-of-faith.md)'s; the dignity beneath the right is [human dignity](#human-dignity) ([3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md)).
- **The picture.** A patient's right to refuse surgery is not a judgment that refusing is wise: his doctor may call it foolish, with all the force of the truth; no one may strap him to the table.
- **Level.** **Rung 3** (a conciliar declaration), restated by *Magnifica Humanitas* 34 (2026). Not defined.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Where the line of public order falls in a given case (a permit, a prosecution, a registration) is application, for legislators and courts ([just public order](#just-public-order)).

- **Trap (misread):** "DH gives all religions equal rights to the truth". The right is an immunity of persons in civil society; DH 1 professes the one true religion, and CCC 2108 says it is neither a moral licence to adhere to error nor a right to error.
- **Trap (demotion):** "religious liberty is a truce, the hypothesis to drop when Catholics are a majority". DH 2 grounds it in nature and has it persist in those who do not seek the truth ([thesis and hypothesis](#thesis-and-hypothesis)).
- **Trap (level):** "DH defined religious liberty". Rung 3.
- **Not toleration.** Leo XIII's [toleration](#toleration-for-a-greater-good) is a concession for a greater good; DH's right is owed to the person by nature.
- **Compared, never merged:** Rawls grounds religious liberty freestanding, in public reason and what free and equal citizens can reasonably accept ([`political-philosophy` 4.1](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-01-political-liberalism.md)–[4.2](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-02-public-reason-and-religious-argument.md)); [`political-philosophy` 6.3](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-03-conscience-and-exemptions.md) treats DH as one position on exemptions; Locke's toleration is [`history-of-political-thought` 4.2](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/04-02-locke-limited-government-and-toleration.md)'s. Augustine's case for coercing the Donatists ([`patristics` 4.4](../patristics/lessons/04-04-augustine-against-the-donatists.md)) is not DH's position; DH 10's note cites his earlier writings.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Toleration for a greater good

**Leo XIII's teaching that public authority may tolerate what is at variance with truth, to avoid a greater evil or obtain a greater good, and only as far as that cause requires.** *Libertas* 33–34 (1888). *Libertas* 42 (5.2's Source, Benziger 1903, p. 161) denies unconditional freedom of thought, speech, writing or worship held "as if these were so many rights given by nature to man", since such a right would license refusing obedience to God; but such freedom "may be tolerated wherever there is just cause", with moderation. *Immortale Dei* 36 (1885) says rulers may tolerate other forms of worship for a great good. **Rung 3 at its date, at a heavy grade**, as taught in 1888; not a definition.

**Principle or application:** taught in 1888 as principle. Whether there is just cause to tolerate in a given state, and how far, was the ruler's prudence.

- **How the readings use the Source:** continuity readings lean on "unconditional" and "as if" (Leo targets liberty claimed as a moral licence to do wrong); reversal readings lean on "tolerated" (a concession, not a right).
- **Trap:** reading DH's right as a wider toleration. Toleration is permitted for a cause and may be withdrawn when the cause lapses; DH 2's right lies in the person's nature ([religious liberty](#religious-liberty)).
- **Reserved for Boss 5(a).** The close reading of *Immortale Dei* 36 (what Leo says of the state's duty toward religion and of liberty of worship, and which words carry each claim) is the Boss's.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Duty of societies toward the true religion

**The traditional doctrine, which DH 1 says it leaves untouched, that persons and societies have a moral duty toward the true religion and the one Church of Christ.** DH 1: religious freedom concerns immunity from coercion in civil society, so it leaves untouched the traditional Catholic doctrine on the moral duty of persons and societies toward the true religion. CCC 2105 restates it (a locator). **At least rung 3**: the duty is affirmed, not merely spared.

**Principle or application:** the duty is principle, owed religious submission, and every reading must keep it. What it requires of a state now, and whether the [confessional state](#confessional-state) is the ideal, is **rung 5**: [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)'s question. DH itself lets government favour religious life (DH 3, 6) and permits special recognition on conditions (DH 6).

- **Trap (demotion):** "the old talk of a duty toward the true religion was quietly dropped". DH 1 leaves it untouched and CCC 2105 restates it.
- **Trap (inflation):** "DH 1 proves the confessional state is still the ideal every Catholic must work toward". That presents a rung-5 reading as settled.
- **Two clauses, two sides:** DH 1 gives each side a clause, the duty left untouched and the doctrine of recent popes developed; no act settles which governs ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Just public order

**The one limit on religious freedom: the state may stop harm, not error.** DH 7: the right is exercised within juridical norms conforming to the objective moral order, needed for (1) the effective safeguard of the rights of all citizens and the peaceful settlement of conflicts of rights, (2) adequate care of genuine public peace, and (3) proper guardianship of public morality. Government may not act arbitrarily or in a partisan spirit; freedom is respected as far as possible and curtailed only when and insofar as necessary. CCC 2109: the limits are fixed for each social situation by political prudence, and public order is not conceived in a positivist or naturalist manner. That a moral limit exists, and its criterion, are **rung 3** (DH 7).

**Principle or application:** the limit and its criterion are principle, owed religious submission. Where the line falls in a given case is application, left to political prudence and the civil authority (CCC 2109): legislators and courts judge it, it has **no magisterial level**, and it often rests on empirical premises (does the gathering block the gate, endanger the site?).

- **In 5.2's Example 1** (the invented Fellowship of the Still Pool): a court order to give a diabetic child insulin is within the limit, since the child's life is another's right; closing the sect's hall is outside it, since the hall harms no one and DH 4 protects worship and buildings.
- **Equality matters.** A rule that burdens religious gatherings alone, where others need only the ordinary permit, also meets DH 6's equality before the law; the ordinary criminal law applied impartially is not coercion in religion. [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)'s Flashback runs the limit at a military base.
- **Trap (inflation):** "the Council has settled that our gathering may never be moved". The line at a given place is application.
- **Trap (demotion):** "where the line falls is our call and no teaching binds us". DH 7's limit and criterion bind every government.
- **Trap:** "any permit rule is a violation". DH 7 itself expects regulatory norms, applied equally.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Quanta Cura and the Syllabus

**Pius IX's encyclical of 8 December 1864 and the list of eighty condemned propositions sent with it: the sharpest nineteenth-century statements against liberty of conscience and worship as a right.** *Quanta Cura* 3 (paraphrased) condemned two clauses: the opinion that liberty of conscience and worship is each person's own right, to be proclaimed in law in every rightly constituted society; and a right to a liberty restrained by no authority. It repeats Gregory XVI, *Mirari Vos* (15 August 1832), who condemned as madness the claim that liberty of conscience must be secured for everyone. The Syllabus condemned, among others, propositions 15 and 77–79: 77 is time-indexed ("in the present day"); 78 condemned praise of laws granting immigrants public worship in Catholic countries; 79's condemnation assumes that civil liberty of worship corrupts morals and spreads indifferentism.

- ***Quanta Cura*'s level.** A disputed candidate *ex cathedra* act, in which Newman heard "that infallible teaching voice" (*Letter to the Duke of Norfolk*, 1875, §8): **at least rung 3, heavy grade, not manifestly defined**. What QC 3 condemns exactly is **rung 5**.
- **The Syllabus's level.** Whether it is infallible as such was disputed; Newman: "the Syllabus then has no dogmatic force" (§7). Never present it as defined; each proposition keeps at least the weight of its source act.
- ***Mirari Vos*:** an encyclical, **rung 3 at its date**.

**Principle or application:** as taught in 1864, principle, at the weights above; Syllabus 77's "in the present day" marks a time-indexed judgment. Syllabus 79's assumption about what liberty of worship does to morals is an empirical premise, from 1864.

- **Trap (understated):** "*Quanta Cura* was plainly not *ex cathedra*". Can. 749 §3 forbids presuming a definition, but "not manifest" is not "plainly not".
- **Trap (overstated):** "*Quanta Cura* infallibly defined ...", an integralist overstatement ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)). Not manifestly defined.
- **Ceded:** the close reading of QC 3 and Syllabus 77–79 is [`church-history` 9.2](../church-history/lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)'s; this course states the texts and their levels only.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Thesis and hypothesis

**The nineteenth-century distinction between the Catholic ideal (thesis: the confessional state) and what may be accepted in actual conditions (hypothesis: toleration, civil liberties).** It entered theological usage in an unsigned article in *La Civiltà Cattolica* (1863, traceable to C. M. Curci) answering Montalembert's Malines speeches; Bishop Félix Dupanloup of Orléans applied it to the Syllabus in his 1865 pamphlet. **No magisterial level**: an interpretive distinction of theologians and bishops, not an act of the magisterium.

**Principle or application:** neither; a reading tool. Whether the confessional state is still the thesis after DH is **rung 5** ([confessional state](#confessional-state)).

- **Trap (demotion):** "religious liberty is the hypothesis, to drop when Catholics are a majority". DH 2 grounds the right in nature, not in the balance of numbers ([religious liberty](#religious-liberty)).
- **Trap (origin):** "Dupanloup's distinction". He made it famous in 1865; it was aired in *La Civiltà Cattolica* in 1863.
- **Secular analogue:** modus vivendi against overlapping consensus ([`political-philosophy` 4.1](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-01-political-liberalism.md)): is a church's acceptance of liberty principled or a truce while outnumbered? DH answers for the Catholic Church.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md)

### Confessional state

**A state whose constitution professes or establishes one religion; in the nineteenth-century teaching, the Catholic ideal.** Leo XIII taught that the state is bound to God "by the public profession of religion" (*Immortale Dei* 6, Benziger 1903) and may not treat all religions alike (*Libertas* 21): **rung 3 at its date, at a heavy grade**, never defined. DH 6 permits special civil recognition of one religious community where circumstances warrant, provided everyone's religious freedom is recognized and effective in practice, with equality before the law: a conditional permission (**application**), neither a mandate nor a prohibition. The confessional state as the ideal toward which a Catholic people should tend today is **rung 5**.

**Principle or application:** the floor, binding every reading, is principle: DH 6's conditions and DH 2's right, so a statute penalizing another faith's worship beyond [just public order](#just-public-order) breaches DH 2. Whether a given country adopts or keeps an establishment is its citizens' prudence; a Catholic may vote either way without dissent, if he keeps the floor and claims no Church authority for his vote (GS 43).

- **5.2's Monteral** (invented): the establishment may stay, and Monteral may favour religious life (DH 3, 6); a ban on other religions' public worship falls outside DH (DH 2, 4, 6, 7). Why it is hard: Syllabus 78 condemned praise of laws granting immigrants public worship in Catholic countries.
- **6.4's Castrel** (invented): an amendment recognizing one church that forbids no one's worship sits on the floor, and the readings part over it. The integralist sees the duty of societies honoured; the liberal a circumstantial permission, doubting with Murray a government's competence in religious truth; the progressive cites GS 76: the Church does not rest her hope on privileges from civil authority.
- **Trap (misread):** "DH never applied to confessional states". DH 6 addresses exactly them, and DH 2 binds any human power.
- **Trap (inflation):** "DH requires a secular state"; and its mirror, "DH 6 proves the confessional state is the thesis". DH 6 is a conditional permission either way.
- **Compared, never merged:** the US Establishment Clause forbids what DH 6 permits ([`constitutional-law`](../constitutional-law/syllabus.md) Module 7).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Hermeneutic of reform

**Benedict XVI's proposal for reading Vatican II: continuity of principle, with discontinuity in contingent applications; religious freedom was his example.** Address to the Roman Curia, 22 December 2005 (paraphrased): he set a "hermeneutic of discontinuity and rupture" against a hermeneutic of reform: renewal within the continuity of the one Church, which remains a single subject; the Church's decisions on contingent matters are themselves contingent while principles remain; the Council reviewed or even corrected certain historical decisions while preserving her nature. Martin Rhonheimer developed it (*Nova et Vetera*, 2011), reading DH as a real reform of earlier teaching on the state; Thomas Pink replied (*Nova et Vetera*, 2013). An address: **rung 3 at its lightest**, at most, for any principle it teaches; its reading of DH is one side of a **rung-5** dispute, not a settlement.

**Principle or application:** a proposed way of reading, not a ruling. Whether DH develops or reverses Leo XIII stays at rung 5.

- **Trap (misquotation):** "Benedict's hermeneutic of continuity". That tag is not the address's phrase; it set rupture against reform ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)).
- **Trap (overstated):** "Benedict settled the DH question". An address, rung 3 at its lightest, and every reading quotes half of it.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Integralism

**Political integralism (not the anti-modernist "integralism" of [`church-history` 8.3](../church-history/lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)): the reading on which the state's duty toward the true religion is a permanent principle and the temporal power is subordinate to the spiritual.** *The Josias*, an integralist journal once edited by the Cistercian Edmund Waldstein, put it in three sentences (2016): political rule must order man to his final end; man has a temporal and an eternal end, so two powers rule him; the temporal end is subordinate to the eternal, so the temporal power is subordinate to the spiritual. Leo XIII is read as still governing (*Immortale Dei* 6; *Libertas* 21); DH is read narrowly, as [Thomas Pink](#thomas-pink) reads it, or as rupture. Thomas Crean and Alan Fimister's *Integralism: A Manual of Political Philosophy* (2020) systematizes it. Adrian Vermeule's [common-good constitutionalism](#common-good-constitutionalism) is a neighbour, not the same thing: it does not rest on the two powers.

- **Levels.** The reading has **no magisterial level**. Its key premise splits in two: the duty of societies (DH 1, **at least rung 3**, shared by every reading; see [duty of societies](#duty-of-societies-toward-the-true-religion)) and the [confessional state](#confessional-state) as the ideal today (**rung 5**). The temporal subjection of rulers to the pope was never defined ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md)). Leo XIII's teaching on the state: **rung 3 at its date, at a heavy grade**, never defined; *Quanta Cura*: a disputed candidate *ex cathedra* act, **not manifestly defined**.

**Principle or application:** a reading, not a principle; its programme (a confessional polity) is an application, owed serious consideration, which a Catholic may weigh and reject without dissenting (GS 43).

- **Trap (inflation):** "integralism is the real teaching". No reading has a magisterial level.
- **Trap (misread):** "DH condemned integralism". DH 1 affirms the duty of societies and DH 6 permits recognition; what DH excludes is coercion beyond public order (rung 3).
- **Trap (demotion):** a rupture reading that refuses DH 2 any assent demotes a rung-3 act. Pink's reading claims to keep DH's teaching.
- **Trap (level):** "*Quanta Cura* infallibly defined that ...". Not manifestly defined; what its §3 condemns exactly is rung 5.
- **Pink's texts:** 5.2 cites his reply to Rhonheimer (*Nova et Vetera*, 2013); 6.4 cites "Conscience and Coercion" (*First Things*, 2012). Both are real.
- **Reserved for Boss 6(c).** Stating the three readings in 100 words each and naming the cruxes among them is the Boss's; see [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Right intention

**Aquinas's third condition for a just war: those who wage it must intend the advancement of good or the avoidance of evil, not the disordered desires that are war's real evils.** Its source is Augustine's reply to Faustus (*Contra Faustum* XXII.74, c. 400, tr. Stothert, NPNF first series vol. 4, 1887): the evil in war is not the death of some who will soon die in any case; "The real evils in war are love of violence, revengeful cruelty, fierce and implacable enmity, wild resistance, and the lust of power". Aquinas quotes the list in ST II-II q.40 a.1 (English Dominican Province) to show that a war with lawful authority and a just cause can still be "rendered unlawful through a wicked intention". **No magisterial level** (a Father; Aquinas's article is theology, rung 4 common teaching).

**Principle or application:** a condition of the tradition the Catechism restates ([just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria)), not itself an act of the magisterium. Whether a given government's aim is right is a judgment about its will, not something the texts settle.

- **Two things the modern teaching does not repeat** (5.3's Source note, which [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md) needs): death in war set aside as "cowardly dislike", and war framed as punishment. The Catechism frames armed force as legitimate defence (CCC 2308–2309).
- **Trap:** reading Augustine as saying killing in war is morally indifferent. He says the deaths are not *the* evil, and he still requires lawful authority and a right will.
- **Trap:** making intention the only condition. A war can have authority and cause and still fail on intention; intention alone does not make it just.
- **Attribution:** the "True religion looks upon as peaceful those wars ..." text that q.40 a.1 credits to Augustine is not his; the English Dominican translators' note finds it in Gratian. Do not cite it as Augustine.
- **Ceded:** reconstructing q.40 a.1 as a disputed question is [`philosophical-method`](../philosophical-method/syllabus.md) Boss 4's ([4.4](../philosophical-method/lessons/04-04-arguing-within-a-tradition-the-disputed-question.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md)

### Legitimate defence

**A government may, and for those responsible for others must, repel an unjust aggressor by armed force once peaceful means are exhausted; defending a people is never licence to conquer others.** GS 79 §4 (1965): while the danger of war lasts and there is no competent and sufficiently powerful international authority, governments cannot be denied the right of legitimate defence once every means of peaceful settlement has been exhausted; defending a people differs from seeking to subjugate other nations. CCC 2308 quotes it, with the duty of all citizens and governments to work to avoid war; CCC 2265 makes defence a grave duty for those responsible for others' lives (its note cites ST II-II q.64 a.7). *Dignitas Infinita* 38 (DDF declaration, ordinary approval, 2 April 2024) restates the inalienable right to self-defence, quoting Francis (2023). The soldier acts on public authority ([`moral-theology` 5.1](../moral-theology/lessons/05-01-the-inviolability-of-innocent-life.md), which fixes the level and the public/private asymmetry). **At least rung 3.**

- **The floor that never moved** ([5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)): CCC 2308–2309 are unrevised; *Magnifica Humanitas* 192 (2026) writes without prejudice to the right to self-defence "in the strictest sense"; Leo XIV's World Day of Peace message for 2026 still names legitimate defence as a principle.

**Principle or application:** the right and the duty are principle, owed religious submission. Whether this use of force is defence, and how it is fought within the limits, is the judgment of those responsible for the common good (CCC 2309; see [just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria)).

- **Trap (demotion):** "the Church no longer accepts any war". MH 192 keeps self-defence in the strictest sense; CCC 2308–2309 stand; DI 38 reaffirmed the right.
- **Trap (inflation):** "the Church obliges the nation to fight this war". The duty is the government's, and the verdict on a particular war is its judgment, not the Church's.
- **Trap:** "a just defence makes every act in it just". The limits in war bind absolutely ([non-combatant immunity](#non-combatant-immunity); [the condemnation of total war](#the-condemnation-of-total-war)).
- **Neighbours:** armed resistance inside a state has its own five conditions ([armed resistance](#armed-resistance)); defence of others beyond one's borders is the [responsibility to protect](#responsibility-to-protect); the state's power to punish, the death penalty included, is [`moral-theology` 5.4](../moral-theology/lessons/05-04-the-death-penalty-and-ccc-2267.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Just-war criteria

**The conditions under which armed defence may be morally legitimate, as the Catechism states them, with the verdict on any particular war assigned to those responsible for the common good.**

- **The source** (ST II-II q.40 a.1, English Dominican Province). A just war needs "three things": the sovereign's authority; a just cause, some fault in those attacked; and a [right intention](#right-intention). No magisterial level; common teaching (rung 4).
- **The conditions** (CCC 2309, paraphrased). Defence by military force must meet, at one and the same time, strict conditions: (1) the aggressor's damage to the nation or community of nations is lasting, grave and certain; (2) every other means of ending it has been shown impractical or ineffective; (3) there are serious prospects of success; (4) arms will not produce evils and disorders graver than the evil to be eliminated, where the power of modern weapons weighs very heavily. The Catechism calls these the traditional elements of what is called "just war" doctrine. **Rung 3** (the Catechism locates GS 79 and the tradition); never defined. Unrevised.
- **The assignment** (CCC 2309, last sentence). Evaluating the conditions belongs to the [prudential judgment](#prudential-judgment) of those responsible for the common good. That is competence, not immunity: rulers stay open to moral criticism, and no critic may claim the Church's authority for his verdict (GS 43). **Rung 3.**
- **Ad bellum and in bello.** CCC 2309 governs resort to force; CCC 2312–2314 and GS 79–80 govern conduct in war. The conditions feed one judgment, assigned to rulers; the absolute limits feed none.
- **The atomic age** (PT 127, 1963). John XXIII judged it contrary to reason (*alienum est a ratione*) in the atomic age to think war fit *ad violata iura sarcienda*, for repairing violated rights. The *Compendium* 497 widens the object to war as an instrument of justice; the Latin targets redress, and GS 79 §4 kept defence two years later. **Rung 3**, its scope argued.

**Principle or application:** the conditions and the assignment are principles, owed religious submission. Whether this war or this intervention meets them is an application with **no rung**; what a weapon destroys and whether an army can win are [empirical premises](#empirical-premise). The Church drafts no war plan ([the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)).

**The challenge** ([5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)). *Fratelli Tutti* 258 (2020) finds it very difficult nowadays to invoke the criteria of earlier centuries, while invoking CCC 2309's conditions. *Magnifica Humanitas* 192 (2026, paraphrased, with the qualifier always kept): without prejudice to the right to self-defence "in the strictest sense", just-war "theory", all too often used to justify any kind of war, is "now outdated"; its note cites FT 258, which itself invokes CCC 2309. Theologians read it two ways:

1. It narrows licit war to strict self-defence and retires the criteria as a framework for justifying anything more (a development some will call a revision of rung-3 teaching).
2. It judges the theory's *use* and present warfare, leaving CCC 2309's conditions as the content of self-defence in the strictest sense. The just-war theorists' reply takes this line: the criteria are what condemn the wars that misuse them, since war on cities fails [non-combatant immunity](#non-combatant-immunity) and nuclear exchange fails proportion.

Not disputed on either reading: legitimate defence under strict conditions (**at least rung 3**) and GS 80's condemnation. MH 192 is an encyclical's sentence, so it is never "just an opinion"; what level its claim carries is left to [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md) and Boss 5(d).

- **Trap (demotion):** "the just-war conditions are Aquinas's opinion". The article has no magisterial level, but CCC 2309 restates its tradition at rung 3.
- **Trap (overstatement):** "the criteria are defined". No act defines them.
- **Trap:** "CCC 2309 puts rulers beyond criticism", or "a bishop's verdict on this war binds". It assigns competence only (GS 43).
- **Trap:** "PT 127 abolished defence". That drops its object, *ad violata iura sarcienda*.
- **Never write:** "MH abolished just war", "MH defined pacifism", "MH revised the Catechism", "MH 192 is just an opinion". The 2016 Appeal ([just peace](#just-peace)) has no magisterial level either.
- **Ceded:** double effect is [`moral-theology` 4.2](../moral-theology/lessons/04-02-double-effect-and-cooperation-as-catholic-method.md)'s and [`ethics` 3.2](../ethics/lessons/03-02-the-doctrine-of-double-effect.md)'s (strategic and terror bomber included); the law on force and humanitarian intervention are [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) 4.2 and 4.4; Valladolid argued this teaching for the Indies ([`church-history` 7.1](../church-history/lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)).

**Reserved for Boss 5(d).** Applying 5.3's teaching to a strike on a military facility sited inside a city (what the doctrine decides, what GS 80 forbids absolutely, which judgments CCC 2309 assigns to rulers), and saying whether FT 258 and MH 192 change that answer and at what level each is taught, is the Boss's.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Conscientious objection

**Refusing to bear arms for reasons of conscience: the state should provide fairly for objectors, who stay bound to serve the community another way.** GS 79 §3 (1965); CCC 2311: public authorities should make equitable provision for those who for reasons of conscience refuse to bear arms, while they remain obliged to serve the human community in some other way. **Rung 3.**

**Principle or application:** that objectors are to be provided for is principle, owed religious submission; how alternative service is organized is application. *The Harvest of Justice Is Sown in Peace* (US bishops, 1993) backed legal protection for conscientious and selective objectors, including those who cannot serve in strategic nuclear forces because of convictions about indiscriminate killing: particular teaching of one conference, an application.

- **Not a rule for states:** renouncing violence, using the means of defence available to the weakest, is a witness of evangelical charity (CCC 2306; GS 78), a vocation, not a duty laid on governments.
- **Distinct from refusing criminal orders:** that is a duty for every soldier, objector or not (GS 79 §2; CCC 2313; [non-combatant immunity](#non-combatant-immunity)); the domestic parallel is the [limits of obedience](#limits-of-obedience).
- **Trap:** "objectors owe the community nothing". They serve another way (CCC 2311).
- **Compared:** exemptions for pacifists are argued secularly in [`political-philosophy` 6.3](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-03-conscience-and-exemptions.md).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Non-combatant immunity

**Non-combatants, the wounded and prisoners may never be made the object of attack and must be treated humanely; a criminal order binds no one.** GS 79 §2 (1965): the universal natural law keeps its permanent binding force in armed conflict; actions deliberately against it, and orders commanding them, are crimes; blind obedience excuses no one; the extermination of a people is condemned. CCC 2312: the moral law stays valid during armed conflict, and the outbreak of war does not make everything licit. CCC 2313: non-combatants, wounded soldiers and prisoners must be respected and treated humanely; genocide must be resisted. **Rung 3**; the norm beneath (never directly kill the innocent) is **rung 1** (*Evangelium Vitae* 57; [`moral-theology` 5.1](../moral-theology/lessons/05-01-the-inviolability-of-innocent-life.md)).

- **Foreseen harm** to civilians is weighed by double effect, a **rung-4** framework ([`moral-theology` 4.2](../moral-theology/lessons/04-02-double-effect-and-cooperation-as-catholic-method.md); [`ethics` 3.2](../ethics/lessons/03-02-the-doctrine-of-double-effect.md)); the US bishops judged foreseen civilian deaths by proportion (*The Challenge of Peace* §§180–182).
- **Universally binding, by a conference's own sorting:** *The Challenge of Peace* §§9–10 lists non-combatant immunity and proportionality among the universally binding principles it restates ([1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md)).
- **Extended to intention:** *The Challenge of Peace* §147 (never use nuclear weapons to destroy population centres, quoting GS 80) and §178 (no one may intend to kill the innocent as part of a deterrence strategy) restate the norm and keep its weight ([nuclear deterrence](#nuclear-deterrence)).

**Principle or application:** principle, absolute: no ruler's judgment can make a direct attack on the innocent lawful. What harm is foreseeable and whether it is proportionate are judgments of fact and proportion.

- **Trap (demotion):** "non-combatant immunity is a papal preference". Its core is rung 1.
- **Trap:** "a just war makes every act in it just" (CCC 2312).
- **Trap:** "I was ordered to" (GS 79 §2; CCC 2313: blind obedience excuses no one).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Terrorism

**Violence that threatens, wounds and kills indiscriminately: gravely against justice and charity.** Named in 5.3, not developed. CCC 2297 (no footnote): terrorism threatens, wounds and kills indiscriminately and is gravely against justice and charity. GS 79 §1 notes terrorism regarded as a new way of waging war. The *Compendium* 513–515 (a locator) condemns it in the most absolute terms, holds that there is a right of defence against it exercised within moral and legal limits (514, quoting John Paul II, World Day of Peace 2002), insists that responsibility is always personal and never extends to religions, nations or ethnic groups, and calls terrorism in God's name a profanation (515). **Rung 3.**

**Principle or application:** the condemnation is principle. How a state defends itself against terrorism, within the moral law, is application; *Fratelli Tutti* 262's doubt that nuclear deterrence answers terrorism is an empirical premise ([nuclear deterrence](#nuclear-deterrence)).

- **Trap:** blaming a religion or people for terrorists' acts. Responsibility is personal (*Compendium* 514).
- **Trap:** "a just cause licenses terror". Terrorism kills indiscriminately, against [non-combatant immunity](#non-combatant-immunity); armed resistance has its own conditions and never licenses terrorism ([armed resistance](#armed-resistance)).
- **Modelled, not judged:** [`conflict-and-bargaining`](../conflict-and-bargaining/syllabus.md) 4.3 models terrorism and leaves its morality here.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md)

### The condemnation of total war

**Any act of war aimed indiscriminately at destroying whole cities or wide areas with their population is a crime against God and man: no cause licenses it.** GS 80 (1965, paraphrased): scientific weapons compel an evaluation of war "with an entirely new attitude" (its note cites PT 127); making its own the condemnations of total war by recent popes (its note: Pius XII, 1954; John XXIII, *Pacem in Terris*; Paul VI at the UN, 1965), the Council issues a declaration that such acts are a crime against God and man, meriting firm and unhesitating condemnation. CCC 2314 quotes it (a locator).

- **Level.** **At least rung 3, at the heaviest conciliar grade**; commonly called the Council's only formal condemnation. Some theologians hold it infallibly taught; that is not manifest, so default down (can. 749 §3). The norm it applies, never directly kill the innocent, is **rung 1** (EV 57). A Catholic may not hold the destruction of a city licit on any reading.
- **Carried into deterrence:** *The Challenge of Peace* §147 quotes it; §178 forbids even intending it as part of a deterrence strategy ([nuclear deterrence](#nuclear-deterrence)).

**Principle or application:** principle, with no application layer: there is no ruler's prudential judgment that can make it lawful. Whether a particular strike is indiscriminate in this sense is a question of fact about its aim and means.

- **Trap (demotion):** "GS is a pastoral constitution, so not binding" ([2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md)). The constitution rests on doctrine, and GS 80 is its heaviest grade.
- **Trap (overstatement):** "GS 80 is an infallible definition". Not manifest; default down.
- **Not GS 81.** GS 80 condemns war on cities; GS 81 judges the arms race and leaves deterrence itself unjudged. GS 81's level is weighed in Boss 1(a)(viii), not here.
- **Reserved for Boss 5(d)** (what GS 80 forbids absolutely when a military facility sits inside a city): see [just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md)

### Autonomous weapons

**Weapon systems that can select and strike targets without direct human intervention: the teaching applies last resort and discrimination to them, and forbids leaving a lethal decision to a machine.** *Antiqua et Nova* 99 (DDF and Dicastery for Culture and Education, note, 28 January 2025, approved in ordinary form): the ease with which autonomous weapons make war more viable undermines the principle of war as a last resort in legitimate defence. *Magnifica Humanitas* 197 (2026) takes this up; MH 198: no lethal or otherwise irreversible decision may be entrusted to artificial systems; MH 199–200: identifiable personal responsibility, time for moral judgment, the identification and protection of civilians, effective human control and traceability, within a shared international framework. **Rung 3** (an encyclical); [*Antiqua et Nova*](#antiqua-et-nova) **rung 3, light grade**.

**Principle or application:** principles: last resort, discrimination, and no delegation of lethal decisions to machines (MH 198), owed religious submission. Application: MH 200's international framework and its design. Empirical premise: that autonomy lowers the threshold of war (MH 197; AeN 99).

- **Trap:** "a new technology needs a new morality". It is last resort and [non-combatant immunity](#non-combatant-immunity) applied to a new machine.
- **Trap (inflation):** "MH requires this treaty text". The framework is an application.
- **Context:** MH's frame for AI as such is [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md)'s ([Magnifica Humanitas](#magnifica-humanitas); [AI is not morally neutral](#ai-is-not-morally-neutral)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md).

### Responsibility to protect

**Every state's primary duty to protect its own population from grave violations, and the international community's duty to act when a state cannot or will not.** The *Compendium* 504 joins the right of defence to the duty to protect innocent victims; 506 (paraphrased) holds that the international community has a moral obligation to intervene for groups whose survival is threatened; that when other means prove ineffective it is legitimate and even obligatory to take concrete measures to disarm the aggressor (quoting John Paul II, World Day of Peace 2000, §11); that national sovereignty is no bar; and that measures must fully respect international law. The *Compendium* does not use the phrase itself: it is Benedict XVI's, to the UN General Assembly (18 April 2008): every state has the primary duty to protect its population, and if it cannot, the international community must intervene with the juridical means of the UN Charter. *Dignitas Infinita* 38 (2024) quotes Francis on the responsibility to protect.

- **Levels.** The duty to protect the innocent is principle. The acts that state the responsibility are papal messages and an address (**rung 3 at their lightest**) and DI 38 (a DDF declaration, ordinary approval); the *Compendium* is a locator with **no magisterial level**. Responsibility to protect as institutional doctrine, and every intervention decision, are **applications**.

**Principle or application:** the duty is principle; whether this coalition acts, and how, is its governments' judgment. 5.3's Example 2 (invented: three neighbours weighing intervention against Tasca, the regional security council deadlocked) shows the conditions straining: authority without a mandate, prospects of success when a cornered regime may kill faster, proportion when the war may spread.

- **Trap:** "the *Compendium* 506 coined the responsibility to protect". The phrase is Benedict XVI's (2008) and Francis's (in DI 38).
- **Trap:** "sovereignty forbids intervention" and its mirror "any atrocity licenses any intervention". Sovereignty is no bar (*Compendium* 506), but the just-war conditions and international law still govern ([just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria)).
- **Ceded:** the law and history of humanitarian intervention are [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) 4.4's.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md)

### Political realism

**The view that international politics is a struggle for power among states, so the statesman must be guided by the national interest defined as power and judged by prudence: the secular rival to judging wars by moral criteria.** Hans J. Morgenthau (1904–1980), *Politics Among Nations* (Knopf, 1948): international politics, like all politics, is "a struggle for power".
