The 1948 edition records that some moral rules bar policies absolutely (the duty not to kill non-combatants on purpose among them) and that total war erodes them; that the Western powers' wish to avoid war at any price before 1939 worked to their disadvantage; and that the first rule of diplomacy is to divest it of the crusading spirit. The "six principles of political realism" first appear in the second edition (1954): interest defined as power; universal moral principles filtered through time and place, with prudence the supreme political virtue; the state may not say *fiat justitia, pereat mundus* for those in its care; no nation's moral aspirations identified with the moral law. **No Catholic level** (a secular rival).

- **Catholic texts that meet it.** *Magnifica Humanitas* 205 (2026) calls "false realism" the belief that war is an inevitable part of human nature, and calls a *Realpolitik* that resigns consciences to war truly irresponsible; MH 218 asks for a "healthy realism" that identifies interests, fears and power without reducing politics to morality or surrendering to violence. Neither names Morgenthau. **Rung 3** (an encyclical).

**Principle or application:** not a Catholic position. Where it meets the teaching: CCC 2309 also assigns the war judgment to those responsible, and its conditions of success and proportion are consequence-weighing.

- **Trap (caricature):** "realism means might makes right". That is Thucydides' Athenians or Thrasymachus, not Morgenthau, who kept some moral barriers and wrote to preserve peace by diplomacy.
- **Trap (dating):** citing the six principles to 1948. They are the 1954 edition's.
- **Trap:** making Morgenthau a structural realist (Waltz), or saying he held war inevitable and good.
- **Ceded:** realism, the Melian Dialogue and Morgenthau as theory are [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) 2.1's; Machiavelli's counsel that rulers learn not to be good is [`history-of-political-thought` 3.1](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/03-01-machiavelli-the-prince.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md)

### Nuclear deterrence

**Holding nuclear weapons so that the threat of retaliation prevents attack: never blessed, at most tolerated as a step toward disarmament, and now judged ever more severely.** The line of statements, as 5.4 reads it:

| Year | Act | What it says (paraphrased) | Level as 5.4 marks it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1963 | *Pacem in Terris* 112–113 | nuclear weapons should be banned; peace rests on mutual trust, not an equal supply of arms | **rung 3** (encyclical) |
| 1965 | *Gaudium et Spes* 80–81 | GS 80 condemns war on whole cities; GS 81, "whatever be the facts" about deterrence, judges the arms race no safe road to peace and leaves deterrence itself unjudged | GS 80 **at least rung 3**, heaviest conciliar grade; GS 81 not levelled here (Boss 1(a)(viii)) |
| 1982 | John Paul II to the UN, §8 | in current conditions, as a step toward progressive disarmament, deterrence may still be judged acceptable ([the 1982 condition](#the-1982-condition)) | papal message, **rung 3 at its lightest** |
| 1983 | *The Challenge of Peace* | a [strictly conditioned acceptance](#strictly-conditioned-acceptance) | particular teaching of one conference |
| 1992 | CCC 2315 | deterrence by accumulating arms raises "strong moral reservations" (unrevised) | a locator; adds no level |
| 1993 | *The Harvest of Justice Is Sown in Peace* | deterrence justified only temporarily, with active commitment to disarmament its only moral basis | particular teaching of one conference |
| 2017 | Holy See signs and ratifies the TPNW (20 Sept), the day it opens | a treaty banning nuclear weapons; in force 2021; no nuclear-armed state a party | juridical act in international law, **no doctrinal rung** |
| 2017, 2019 | Francis's addresses | the threat of use and the very possession are to be condemned ([possession](#possession-of-nuclear-weapons)) | **rung 3 at its lightest**, at most |
| 2020 | *Fratelli Tutti* 262 | deterrence through fear is no solution; doubts that nuclear deterrence answers today's threats; total elimination a moral and humanitarian imperative; does **not** call possession immoral | **rung 3** (encyclical) |
| 2026 | Leo XIV, World Day of Peace message | deterrence rests on fear and force, not law and trust; legitimate defence still a principle | **rung 3 at its lightest** |
| 2026 | *Magnifica Humanitas* 194 | the treaty an important step that risks staying symbolic without the major powers; the belief that deterrence is indispensable to security "widespread yet erroneous"; no possession language | **rung 3** (encyclical) |

**Principle or application:** *principles*, owed religious submission at their rung: never intend or do the destruction of cities; deterrence is never an end and is ordered to disarmament; elimination is a moral goal; legitimate defence stands. *Applications*, owed serious, respectful consideration: 1982's toleration; the bishops' conditions and no first use; the ratification; on one reading, the possession judgment and MH 194. *Empirical premises*: that deterrence has prevented major war (John Paul II at UNESCO in 1980 called it "probably true", quoted in *The Challenge of Peace* §176); the risk of accident; that deterrence fails against terrorism (FT 262); that the powers are not disarming (MH 194).

**Where it stops.** The doctrine fixes boundaries: no plan or intention to strike population centres; no deterrence held as permanent or as an end; real work toward verified reduction. Which weapons to cut first, how fast, by treaty or alone, inside which alliance, whether to sign the treaty: prudential judgments for those responsible for the common good, as CCC 2309 assigns them for war. The documents say so: John Paul II would not enter the technical questions (1982 §1); the US bishops called the decisive judgments highly technical (§192); Leo XIV said the Church brings no technical answers ([the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)).

- **Trap:** "the Church reversed itself" and "that's just this pope". Both skip the condition the 1982 judgment hung on.
- **Not in any act:** "the arms race is in itself a grave injustice". The acts that judge the arms race are GS 81 and PT 112.
- **Ceded:** deterrence as strategy (second strike, mutual assured destruction, counterforce against countervalue) is [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) 2.4's, formalized in [`conflict-and-bargaining`](../conflict-and-bargaining/syllabus.md) 2.3–2.4; the credible threat is [`grad-game-theory` 3.2](../grad-game-theory/lessons/03-02-backward-induction-subgame-perfection.md)'s. The parallel development case is the death penalty ([`moral-theology` 5.4](../moral-theology/lessons/05-04-the-death-penalty-and-ccc-2267.md)): a rung-3 text, a disputed reading.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### The 1982 condition

**John Paul II judged deterrence acceptable only "in current conditions" and only as a step toward progressive disarmament: quote both qualifiers, always.** Message to the UN General Assembly's Second Special Session on Disarmament, dated 7 June 1982 (AAS 74 (1982) 872–883), read to the Assembly on 11 June 1982 by Cardinal Agostino Casaroli, Secretary of State. §8 (paraphrased): in current conditions, deterrence based on balance, not as an end in itself but as a step on the way toward progressive disarmament, may still be judged morally acceptable; but no one may rest content with this minimum, always open to the real danger of explosion. §1: he will not enter the technical and political aspects. §8 also names the missing supranational authority and calls negotiation the only realistic response ([6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md) takes it up). A papal message: **rung 3 at its lightest**.

**Principle or application:** the sentence holds both. *Principle:* deterrence is never an end; it is defensible at most as ordered to progressive disarmament; no one may rest content with it (restating PT 113). *Application:* that in current conditions it may *still* be judged acceptable; "current" and "still" carry a time-index. *Not said:* that deterrence is good, that a threat against cities is licit (GS 80; *The Challenge of Peace* §178), or that the toleration is permanent.

- **Trap (inflation):** "John Paul II approved deterrence". That drops both qualifiers.
- **Trap:** "1982 was an error". It wrote its own expiry clause.
- **Trap (demotion):** "1982 still stands, so Francis only gave his opinion". That drops the condition and demotes a papal act.
- **The dispute it opens:** whether later judgments mean the condition failed or the principle grew ([possession of nuclear weapons](#possession-of-nuclear-weapons)): **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md).

### Strictly conditioned acceptance

**The US bishops' 1983 verdict, by their own prudential judgment, that deterrence may be tolerated only on strict conditions, never as a long-term basis for peace.** *The Challenge of Peace: God's Promise and Our Response* (NCCB, pastoral letter, 3 May 1983). §186: applying John Paul II's 1982 evaluation "through our own prudential judgments", a "strictly conditioned moral acceptance" of deterrence. §188's three criteria: deterrence only to prevent others' use, never plans for prolonged nuclear war or to prevail; sufficiency, not superiority; each new weapon or doctrine judged by whether it brings progressive disarmament nearer. Around it: §147 restates GS 80 (never use nuclear weapons to destroy population centres); §178: no one may intend to kill the innocent as part of a deterrence strategy; §150: no first use, which §10 gives as its own example of a prudential judgment; §192 declined to condemn all deterrence, since that would require highly technical judgments about hypothetical events; §§180–182 judged foreseen civilian deaths by proportion. [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) read the letter's self-marking (§§9–10); 5.4 applies it.

- ***The Harvest of Justice Is Sown in Peace*** (approved by the full body of US bishops on 17 November 1993; a reflection on the tenth anniversary, not a pastoral letter) kept the 1983 conditions as a guide, reported Catholics divided over abandoning them after the Cold War, allowed deterrence only temporarily with an active commitment to disarmament as its only moral basis, and marked its own levels: universal moral principles bind, while no first use, not targeting non-nuclear states and the call for nuclear disarmament are judgments about which Catholics may disagree.
- **Level.** [Particular teaching of one conference](#bishops-conference-teaching), before *Apostolos Suos* (1998). Restated principles keep their own weight (§§147 and 178 carry the rung-1 norm); the conference's applications bind no one's assent, by their own words: not binding in conscience, but owed serious attention and consideration (§10).

**Principle or application:** §§147 and 178 restate principles; the acceptance, its three criteria and no first use are applications, and so are *Harvest*'s.

- **Trap:** "*The Challenge of Peace* approved deterrence". Its acceptance is strictly conditioned, its own prudential judgment (§186), not binding in conscience (§10), and one conference's.
- **Trap (inflation):** "no first use binds Catholics everywhere with religious submission". It is the letter's own example of an application, and particular teaching.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md).

### Possession of nuclear weapons

**Francis's judgment that not only the use and the threat of use, but the very possession of nuclear weapons, is to be condemned; and the dispute over whether it develops the principle or applies 1982's condition to new facts.** The acts are two addresses: to the Vatican symposium "Prospects for a World Free of Nuclear Weapons and for Integral Disarmament" (10 November 2017), on two grounds (the catastrophic effects of any use; the risk of accidental detonation), concluding that the threat of their use and their "very possession" are to be firmly condemned; and at the Hiroshima Peace Memorial (24 November 2019), calling possession immoral, recalling 2017. Seven weeks before the 2017 address, on 20 September 2017, the Holy See signed and ratified the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons the day it opened (in force 22 January 2021; no nuclear-armed state is a party): a juridical act, **no doctrinal rung**.

- **Not the encyclicals.** *Fratelli Tutti* 262 rejects deterrence through fear and makes elimination the goal; it does not call possession immoral. *Magnifica Humanitas* never uses possession language: MH 194 calls the belief in deterrence's necessity erroneous, a judgment that fits either reading below.
- **Level.** Addresses: **rung 3 at its lightest**, at most. Defined: nothing.
- **The two readings** (**rung 5**, freely disputed among theologians): (a) *development of principle*: a deterrent works only if its threat is credible; credibility seems to need a conditional intention to do what GS 80 forbids; intending the forbidden is already wrong (*The Challenge of Peace* §178); so possession for deterrence is wrong whatever the conditions. (b) *The 1982 principle on new facts*: the toleration depended on progress toward disarmament that never came, so the condition failed and the toleration lapsed. Game theory bears on one premise of (a): if a threat that leaves something to chance can deter without a settled intention to strike, that premise weakens ([`grad-game-theory` 3.2](../grad-game-theory/lessons/03-02-backward-induction-subgame-perfection.md)).

**Principle or application:** principle on reading (a), application on reading (b). Either way it is owed at least serious, respectful weighing. 5.4's Example 2 (invented: Edda Rennick, Altmark's defence minister) shows what binds on either reading: no plan, directive or intention to strike population centres, even as a threat; the arsenal treated as a stage, with verified, balanced reductions pursued and superiority refused. How fast, alone or in step, inside which alliance, is her judgment; if she could serve only by intending what GS 80 forbids, she must resign.

- **Trap (misattribution):** citing *Fratelli Tutti* 262 for "possession is immoral", or attributing the possession judgment to Leo XIV. It is the 2017 and 2019 addresses.
- **Trap (inflation):** "since 2017 possession is defined as intrinsically evil, and every Catholic in a nuclear force is in mortal sin". Two addresses define nothing, the addresses do not use that phrase, the reading is disputed, and mortal sin has its own conditions.
- **Trap (demotion):** "it is only an address, so it may be set aside unweighed". A papal address is papal teaching, never a private opinion.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Just peace

**A proposed framework centred on active nonviolence, which a 2016 conference asked the Church to put in place of just-war reasoning; papal teaching since has added nonviolence as a complement instead.** The Rome conference "Nonviolence and Just Peace" (11–13 April 2016), sponsored by the Pontifical Council for Justice and Peace and Pax Christi International, issued "An Appeal to the Catholic Church to Re-Commit to the Centrality of Gospel Nonviolence": there is no "just war"; the theory has too often endorsed rather than limited war; the Church should stop using or teaching it and shift to a "Just Peace" approach; it asked for an encyclical on nonviolence, and cited research claiming nonviolent resistance is twice as effective. **No magisterial level** (a conference statement). Francis's message for the World Day of Peace 2017, "Nonviolence: a Style of Politics for Peace" (signed 8 December 2016), §6: active nonviolence is the "natural and necessary complement" to the Church's continuing efforts to limit the use of force by moral norms: **rung 3 at its lightest**.

**Principle or application:** the Appeal's proposal to drop the criteria is an argument, not teaching. The 2017 message's principle (nonviolence as complement) is owed religious submission at its rung; the Appeal's claim about nonviolent resistance's effectiveness is an empirical premise. Renouncing violence as a personal witness is CCC 2306's ([conscientious objection](#conscientious-objection)).

- **Trap:** "the Appeal is Church teaching, since a pontifical council co-sponsored the conference". It has no magisterial level.
- **Trap:** "the Appeal (or MH 192) abolished just-war teaching". CCC 2308–2309 are unrevised, DI 38 (2024) reaffirmed self-defence, and the 2017 message made nonviolence a complement, not a replacement ([just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria)).
- **Trap (demotion):** "nonviolence is a private devotion with no public force". The 2017 message proposes it as a style of politics.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md)

### Right to migrate

**The person's right, for just reasons, to emigrate and to settle where he hopes to provide better for his family, held as one half of a pair with the receiving community's right and duty to regulate for the common good.** *Pacem in Terris* 25 (1963): when there are just reasons, a person must be permitted to emigrate and take up residence elsewhere, for citizenship of one state does not take him out of the human family (its note cites Pius XII's Christmas radio message of 1952). PT 106: he has a right to enter a country where he hopes to provide better for himself and his dependants; so rulers must receive newcomers and, so far as their community's genuine good allows, further the aims of those who want to join it. In the Latin the qualifier (*non fucatum bonum*, the community's genuine, literally "unpainted", good; vatican.va: "rightly understood") governs that second clause, the help to those who would join. GS 65 saves the personal right of migration; GS 66 asks that migrant workers be treated as persons, not tools of production, joined by their families and incorporated into social life (LE 23 is [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md)'s).

**The paired paragraph** (CCC 2241, paraphrased; it footnotes nothing, so find the acts behind each clause):

| Clause | Whose duty or right | The acts behind it | Level |
|---|---|---|---|
| Better-off nations must, as far as they are able, take in the foreigner seeking the security and livelihood he lacks at home | the receiving nation's duty | PT 106; MH 81 | **rung 3**, qualifier included |
| Public authorities must uphold the natural right that places a guest under the protection of those who receive him | the authorities' duty | no source found in the corpus; carried by the Catechism's own promulgation (*Fidei Depositum*, 1992) | **rung 3**, nothing higher manifest |
| Political authorities, for the common good in their charge, may set juridical conditions on the right to immigrate, chiefly the immigrant's duties | the authorities' right and duty | PT 106's limit; restated in EMCC 29, Francis's letter of 2025 (§§4–5), Leo XIV (23 October 2025) | **rung 3** |
| The immigrant must respect with gratitude his new country's material and spiritual heritage, keep its laws and carry his share of civic burdens | the migrant's duties | the citizen's duties of CCC 2238–2240 (Rom 13:7) applied to the newcomer | **rung 3** |

*Erga Migrantes Caritas Christi* 29 (instruction of the Pontifical Council for the Pastoral Care of Migrants and Itinerant People, approved by John Paul II on 1 May 2004, dated 3 May 2004) sets the same pair side by side: the right to emigrate, and every country's right to an immigration policy that promotes the common good. **Rung 3** (PT 25, 106, restating Pius XII; repeated in GS 66, EMCC 29, *Magnifica Humanitas* 81); EMCC is **rung 3, light grade** for the doctrine it restates.

- **The newest acts.** Francis's letter to the bishops of the United States (10 February 2025): dignity is the measure of laws, not the reverse (§3); a rightly formed conscience must reject any measure that equates lacking legal status with criminality, yet a nation may defend itself against those who have committed violent or serious crimes (§4); an orderly, legal migration policy is not impeded (§5); the true *ordo amoris* is the Samaritan's, not love spreading outward in rings of interest (§6). **Rung 3 at its lightest** for what it restates; its judgment on one country's deportation programme is an application. On its plain words §6 rejects rings of interest, not ordered duties (the lesson's reading); Aquinas's order of charity is [`moral-theology` 3.2](../moral-theology/lessons/03-02-faith-hope-and-charity.md)'s. MH 81 (2026) makes the treatment of migrants a "litmus test" of social justice, with two complementary commitments: safe and legal routes, dignified reception and integration; and the [right to remain](#right-to-remain).

**Principle or application:** both rights, the duties on each side and the qualifier are principles, owed religious submission; the qualifier, as far as they are able, belongs to the principle. *Applications*, owed serious, respectful consideration (GS 43): every number, quota, cap, criterion, procedure, deadline and visa type. A Catholic who weighs a papal judgment on a programme and reads the facts otherwise dissents from nothing; one who waves it away unweighed has not done what the documents ask. *Empirical premises:* what migration does to wages, budgets, crime and cohesion; how fast newcomers integrate; whether aid reduces emigration.

**Where it stops.** The principles do not say how many, on what criteria or how fast. CCC 2241 places that [prudential judgment](#prudential-judgment) with political authorities, for the common good in their charge, much as CCC 2309 places the judgment of a war ([just-war criteria](#just-war-criteria)). It is bounded: "able" is not "willing"; the good in question is the community's genuine good, which includes its share of the [universal common good](#universal-common-good); and no capacity licenses treating a person as less than a person (FT 39). FT 132 asks for shared planning and global governance of migration, designing none. A bishop may teach, judge and plead, but his figure is an application ([1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md)); the doctrine offers no immigration model ([the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)).

- **Trap (inflation):** "the Church teaches open borders; every visa rule is a sin". That drops the qualifier and the second half of the pair.
- **Trap (demotion):** "migration is pure policy, so the Church has no teaching", or "the state may set whatever conditions it likes". Two rung-3 rights are demoted, and "for the common good" is dropped.
- **Trap (the qualifier):** "as far as they are able makes the duty a matter of taste". Calling every limit a sin inflates the welcome; treating any limit at all as enough demotes it.
- **Trap (level):** "CCC 2241 is rung 3 because the Catechism says so". It footnotes nothing; its weight comes from the acts that teach the same pair: rung 3, neither more nor less.
- **Trap (the 2025 letter):** "it made opposing one country's programme a duty of faith" and "it is only a letter". It restates principles at the lightest rung-3 grade, judges a programme as an application, and affirms a nation's right to act against serious criminals (§4) and to regulate legal migration (§5).
- **Trap (citation):** EMCC 21 cites "cf. GS 87" for the right to regulate migration; GS 87 concerns population policy and movement from country to city. Do not cite GS 87 for the regulation of immigration.
- **Bridge:** [`political-philosophy` 6.2](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-02-immigration-may-a-state-exclude.md)'s exit/entry asymmetry (Carens, Wellman, Miller) is the pressure point PT 25 and 106 answer, pairing a right to leave with a right to enter under the receiving community's genuine good: the right to migrate, the exit/entry asymmetry and the [universal destination of goods](#universal-destination-of-goods) are one question asked three ways.

**Reserved for Boss 5(b).** Marking the clauses of a visa-and-asylum bill as principle applied or prudential judgment, and diagnosing a diocesan statement that calls a cap figure "contrary to Catholic teaching", is the Boss's.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md)

### Exsul Familia

**Pius XII's apostolic constitution of 1 August 1952 on the spiritual care of migrants, called the Church's magna charta on migration: it grounds migration in the earth made for the use of all.** *Exsul Familia* ("de spirituali emigrantium cura"; on vatican.va in Latin only) opens with the exiled Holy Family, emigrants into Egypt and refugees there from a wicked king, as the type, model and protector (*typus, exemplar et praesidium*) of every migrant and refugee driven out by fear of persecution or by want (*metu persecutionum vel egestate compulsi*). Quoting Pius XII's Pentecost radio message of 1 June 1941 (for *Rerum Novarum*'s fiftieth anniversary), it holds that the earth, created for the use of all, offers room to live, that families have a right to living space, and that migration tends to a more equitable distribution of people over the earth; it calls these general principles of natural law. Its second title laid down norms, canon law for the pastoral care of migrants. EMCC 20 (2004) and Francis (2025, §2) call it the magna charta.

- **Level.** Its doctrinal opening: **rung 3, light grade**. Its norms were **law**, since replaced (Paul VI's norms of 1969, then EMCC's regulations of 2004).

**Principle or application:** the doctrine is principle: migration is first a question of the [universal destination of goods](#universal-destination-of-goods) ([3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)). The norms were law for their time; their pastoral arrangements were applications.

- **Trap (rendering):** the Holy Family as the migrants' "patron". The Latin says type, model and protector.
- **Trap (dating):** PT 25's note cites Pius XII's Christmas message of 1952; *Exsul Familia* quotes the Pentecost message of 1941.
- **Trap:** "its norms still govern the pastoral care of migrants". They were revised in 1969 and replaced by EMCC (2004).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md)

### Right to remain

**The right not to emigrate: to be able to live with dignity in one's own homeland, which lays on everyone a duty to tackle the causes that force people out. It complements the duty of welcome; it never replaces it.** *Fratelli Tutti* 38 (2020), quoting Benedict XVI's message for the 2013 World Day of Migrants and Refugees: a right not to emigrate, to remain in one's homeland. EMCC 29 (2004) affirms the same right. FT 129: ideally unnecessary migration would be avoided by creating conditions for a dignified life at home; but until substantial progress is made, everyone's right to find a place that meets his and his family's basic needs must be respected. *Magnifica Humanitas* 81 (2026): the second of at least two complementary commitments, promoting the right to remain in one's homeland in peace and security by addressing root causes, economic injustice and the climate crisis among them. Precedents: PT 101–102 preferred bringing work to the workers, where possible, over uprooting them (an application); GS 66 asks that jobs be created in migrants' own regions as far as possible. **Rung 3** (FT 38, an encyclical).

**Principle or application:** the right and the duty to address causes are principle. How much aid, where, and by what programme are applications; whether aid in fact reduces emigration is an empirical premise.

- **5.5's Example 2** (invented: wealthy Halvenor triples aid and funds camps near origin in place of admitting anyone for ten years). *Decided:* tackling causes is a real duty, not a dodge (FT 129; MH 81). *Forbidden:* using the second commitment to cancel the first; MH 81 calls them complementary, and FT 129 binds now, before conditions improve. *Permitted:* a mix weighted toward aid, with protection near home where it is genuinely safe; Wellman's view that a state may sometimes help refugees where they are ([`political-philosophy` 6.2](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-02-immigration-may-a-state-exclude.md)) is the secular cousin, part of the answer, never all of it. *Where it stops:* the proportions, and whether aid works.
- **Trap:** using the right to remain to cancel the duty of welcome now (FT 129's "until"; MH 81's "complementary").
- **Trap (source):** *Rerum Novarum* 47's remark that no one would leave a homeland affording a decent living is not a root of this right: [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) grades it an 1891 empirical prediction, not a teaching about migration.
- **Forward:** root causes, development and the international order are [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md)

### Refugees

**Those forced to flee because they cannot find security at home: the case where the duty of welcome presses hardest.** *Pacem in Terris* 103–105 (1963): political exiles (103); rulers who restrict just freedom too far (104); refugees are persons, and they do not lose their rights as persons by losing their citizenship (105). PT 106 is the general right to enter; 107–108 praise relief undertakings and international agencies. CCC 2241's clause on the security a person cannot find at home names the need at its sharpest. FT 130 asks for indispensable steps, especially for those fleeing grave humanitarian crises (among them visas, sponsorship and humanitarian corridors for the most vulnerable, housing, documents, access to justice, work, education for minors, family reunion). Leo XIV told the World Meeting of Popular Movements (23 October 2025) that states have the right and the duty to protect their borders, balanced by the moral obligation to give refuge. The Church's category is wider than the 1951 Convention's: driven out by fear of persecution *or* by want (*Exsul Familia*); MH 81 names those forced to move by poverty, violence, climate change and environmental disasters.

- **Levels.** PT 103–105: **rung 3** (an encyclical); FT 130's steps are applications in a rung-3 encyclical; Leo XIV's address: **rung 3 at its lightest**. That refugees are the strongest case is said by **no act** in so many words, so default down: it is what CCC 2241, PT 105 and FT 130 plainly imply; hold it as their content, not as a separate teaching.
- **Not sending a refugee back to persecution.** In 5.5's Example 1 (Ostrevia's asylum rule, invented) the lesson infers it from PT 105, CCC 2241's guest protection and the cooperation distinctions of [`moral-theology` 3.5](../moral-theology/lessons/03-05-social-sin-and-cooperation-in-the-sins-of-others.md); international law's non-refoulement rule is **law, no rung**.

**Principle or application:** the refugee's surviving rights and the duty of welcome are principle. Corridors, camps, ceilings, relocation schemes, the design of temporary protection and FT 130's list of steps are applications.

- **Trap (level):** quoting "the Church teaches refugees are the strongest case" as a sentence of any act. No act says it; the texts imply it.
- **Trap (category):** equating the Church's refugee with the Convention's. The Church's includes those driven by want; the legal definition is [`political-philosophy` 6.2](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-02-immigration-may-a-state-exclude.md)'s.
- **Trap:** reading Leo XIV's address as either half alone: the right and duty to protect borders, and the moral obligation to give refuge, come together.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md).

### The four verbs

**Francis's summary of the response owed to migrants and refugees (welcome, protect, promote, integrate): a pastoral programme, not doctrine, resting on principles that are.** Message for the 104th World Day of Migrants and Refugees (for 14 January 2018; signed 15 August 2017); the formula was first used in his address to the International Forum on "Migration and Peace" (21 February 2017). FT 129 restates it; FT 130 lists steps; the 2025 letter (§5) recalls it. Its contents, paraphrased:

- **Welcome:** wider safe and legal entry (humanitarian visas, family reunification, private and community sponsorship, humanitarian corridors for the most vulnerable, temporary visas for those fleeing neighbouring conflicts); no collective or arbitrary expulsions; personal safety put before national security; alternatives to detention.
- **Protect:** rights and dignity whatever one's legal status; consular help, documents, access to justice and bank accounts; minors spared detention for their migratory status; a universal right to a nationality.
- **Promote:** work, language, religious freedom; family reunion independent of financial requirements; aid to developing countries receiving large inflows, and to vulnerable local communities.
- **Integrate:** integration is not assimilation (quoting John Paul II); citizenship free of financial or linguistic requirements; special legalization after long residence.

FT's second chapter (56–86) reads the Good Samaritan ([`new-testament` 2.3](../new-testament/lessons/02-03-luke.md)): we do not choose who is near enough to be our neighbour; we become one (FT 80).

- **Level.** The message: **rung 3 at its lightest** as a document. The programme: **no rung** beyond the principles beneath it (the right to migrate, the duty of welcome, dignity).

**Principle or application:** the principles beneath are owed religious submission at their rung; the programme's concrete proposals are applications, owed serious, respectful consideration, and a Catholic who weighs them and judges otherwise does not dissent.

- **Trap (inflation):** "the four verbs are binding doctrine".
- **Trap (demotion):** "only a slogan". It rests on rung-3 principles and asks to be weighed.
- **Trap:** "integrate means assimilate". The message says the opposite.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md)


**Module 6: Creation, development, and the arguments among Catholics**

### Laudato Si

**Francis's encyclical on care for our common home (24 May 2015): the corpus's first systematic social encyclical on the environment.** An encyclical letter, signed at St Peter's on Pentecost, 24 May 2015, addressed to every person living on the planet (LS 3); it places itself within the body of the Church's social teaching (LS 15). Six chapters, 246 sections: what is happening to our common home (17–61; water 27–31); the Gospel of creation (62–100); the human roots of the crisis (101–136; the technocratic paradigm 106–114); integral ecology (137–162); lines of approach and action (163–201; international 164–175, national and local 176–181); ecological education and spirituality (202–246). What [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md) takes from it: creation and [stewardship](#stewardship) (66–68, 76, 90, 116, 118); [integral ecology](#integral-ecology) and the two cries (49, 137–162); the [technocratic paradigm](#technocratic-paradigm) and [throwaway culture](#throwaway-culture) (22, 101, 106–109); the [common good](#common-good) across generations (23, 156–159); the [universal destination of goods](#universal-destination-of-goods) applied to water and land (30, 93–95); its disclaimers (60, 61, 188); its policies (165, 170–173). *Magnifica Humanitas* 43 (2026) calls it the first significant systematic treatment of the environmental crisis in a social encyclical (paraphrased).

- **Level.** The act is **rung 3**. Its principles are owed religious submission of mind and will. Its teaching on creation restates CCC 373 (**rung 3**, per `creation-grace-last-things` 2.1); its premise, that God created all things, is defined (Lateran IV; Vatican I). Its teaching on property restates LE 19 and GS 69 (**rung 3**, [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)). MH 43 and 84 repeat the teaching: **rung 3**, heavier by LG 25's sign of frequency, no definition.
- **It marks its own kinds of claim.** Its principles bind. Its science is labelled borrowed: the best scientific research available (LS 15), LS 23, LS 28–31 on water: **no doctrinal rung**, weighed on the evidence ([empirical premise](#empirical-premise)). Its policies (LS 164–181) are applications, owed serious and respectful consideration (GS 43; *Donum Veritatis* 24). Its disclaimers, that there is no one path to a solution (LS 60), that on many concrete questions the Church has no reason to offer a definitive opinion (LS 61), and that she does not presume to settle scientific questions or to replace politics (LS 188), are rung-3 principles about its own applications: see [the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers).
- **LS 23 has two sentences, not one** (paraphrased): the scientific consensus that the climate system is warming is called very solid; *separately*, a number of studies are said to trace most recent warming mainly to greenhouse gases from human activity. Extreme events are linked to it only tentatively. The same section teaches a principle: the climate is a common good, of all and for all (**rung 3**).
- **Reception.** *Laudate Deum* (2023) follows it up ([Laudate Deum](#laudate-deum)); MH 84 makes [integral human development](#integral-human-development) the benchmark of integral ecology ([6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)); a [Mass for the care of creation](#mass-for-the-care-of-creation) entered the Missal in 2025. Leo XIV's message to COP30 (Belém, November 2025, read by Cardinal Parolin) tied peace to the care of creation: **rung 3 lightest** at most. His message for the World Day of Prayer for the Care of Creation (1 September 2026) calls war and ecological ruin a vicious cycle.

**Principle or application:** the three assents run on one encyclical ([the three assents](#the-three-assents)): religious submission to its principles, serious consideration of its policies, and to its science what the evidence weighs. Disputing the science is not dissent, though LS 188 asks that interests and ideologies not bend the debate. The principles hang on no temperature: water would still be a right, and future people would still count, if warming were slower. In [see-judge-act](#see-judge-act) terms, climate science and hydrology see, the principles judge, prudence acts ([1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md)'s promise, kept in 6.1).

- **Trap (inflation):** "LS defined the climate science, so doubting a projection is dissent", or "LS teaches a carbon tax". LS 188 disclaims the first; LS endorses no instrument ([carbon credits](#carbon-credits)).
- **Trap (demotion):** "integral ecology is the Pope's opinion on science". Creation, stewardship, the poor's water and future generations are rung-3 principles.
- **Trap:** "LS 23's consensus sentence asserts the human cause". The consensus sentence is about warming; the human-cause sentence cites studies. *Laudate Deum* 11 is where the human origin is held beyond doubt.
- **Reserved for Boss 6(a).** Sorting the Boss's eight paraphrased claims and diagnosing its two op-eds is the Boss's work; this entry gives only the kinds and disclaimers as 6.1 teaches them. LS 51 (ecological debt) is already sorted in [`theology-of-debt` 6.2](../theology-of-debt/lessons/06-02-international-debt-and-the-jubilee-call.md).
- **Ceded:** the physical claims are [`climate-science` 3.5](../climate-science/lessons/03-05-detection-attribution.md) and [5.2](../climate-science/lessons/05-02-extremes-event-attribution.md) (where LS 23's caution about single extremes returns as event attribution); ecological debt (LS 51–52) is `theology-of-debt` 6.2's; the new creation of LS 243–244 is [`creation-grace-last-things` 7.2](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Integral ecology

**One complex crisis, social and environmental, seen whole: the polluted river, the poor who drink from it and the firm paid to pollute it are one case.** *Laudato Si'* Chapter 4 (LS 137–162), by its own headings: environmental, economic and social ecology (138–142); cultural ecology (143–146); the ecology of daily life (147–155), which includes a "human ecology" that respects the moral law inscribed in our nature and the acceptance of our bodies (155, drawing on Benedict XVI's "ecology of man"); the principle of the common good (156–158); justice between the generations (159–162). LS 139 (paraphrased): there are not two crises, environmental and social, but one complex crisis, so remedies must fight poverty, restore dignity to the excluded and protect nature together. LS 49: [the cry of the earth and the cry of the poor](#the-cry-of-the-earth-and-the-cry-of-the-poor). Its roots are the [technocratic paradigm](#technocratic-paradigm) and the [throwaway culture](#throwaway-culture) (LS 101, 106–109, 22). *Magnifica Humanitas* 84 (2026, paraphrased): integral human development is the benchmark for evaluating integral ecology, which has become an "indispensable dimension" of the social doctrine; MH 43: LS joined care for the common home to the [preferential option for the poor](#preferential-option-for-the-poor).

- **Level: rung 3** (LS, 2015), repeated by a second pope's encyclical (MH 84), heavier by LG 25's sign of frequency; no definition.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. Which remedy serves both the poor and nature in a given valley or country is prudence resting on empirical premises.

- **Trap:** "human ecology is one of Chapter 4's ecologies". It is not a chapter heading: it sits inside the ecology of daily life (LS 155). (The syllabus's list of five ecologies is wrong on this.)
- **Trap:** "integral ecology is a biocentrism", or divinizes the earth. LS 90 and 118 reject both: not all living beings are on the same level, and the human being keeps a unique worth ([stewardship](#stewardship)).
- **Trap (demotion):** "the Pope's opinion on science". The principle hangs on no temperature.
- **In use:** [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md) takes integral human development, which MH 84 makes integral ecology's benchmark.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md).

### Stewardship

**Human beings are stewards of a gift: neither owners free to use it up nor one more species in it.** The doctrine is owned by `creation-grace-last-things`: CCC 373, man and woman as stewards of God, not by arbitrary and destructive domination (**rung 3**, [`creation-grace-last-things` 2.1](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)). What 6.1 adds from *Laudato Si'* (paraphrased): "nature" is usually seen as a system to study and control, "creation" as a gift within God's loving plan in which every creature has its own value (LS 76). Human life rests on three relationships, with God, neighbour and the earth, whose rupture is sin (LS 66). The earth was here before us and was given to us; reading Genesis as licence for unbridled exploitation is not how the Church understands the Bible; Gen 1:28 is read through 2:15, where tilling means cultivating, ploughing or working and keeping means caring for, protecting, overseeing and preserving; each community may take what it needs but must keep the earth fruitful for coming generations (LS 67). Excluded: a "tyrannical anthropocentrism" careless of other creatures (LS 68), and a "biocentrism" or divinized earth that denies the human being's unique worth (LS 90, 118). Dominion properly understood is "responsible stewardship" (LS 116).

- **Level: rung 3** (LS 67, 116, restating CCC 373). Its premise, that God created all things, is defined (Lateran IV; Vatican I). The ITC's *Communion and Stewardship* (2004): **no magisterial level**.
- **The Douay text** (Challoner, public domain), as 6.1 P1 prints it: "dominion" stands at Gen 1:26 ("let him have dominion over the fishes of the sea"); 1:28 reads "subdue it, and rule over"; 2:15 reads "to dress it, and to keep it". LS's English cites "cf. Gen 1:28" for dominion because it follows a modern translation. Never quote "dominion" from the Douay's 1:28.
- **What LS 67 fixes:** that dominion licenses no absolute domination. It is not a definition, and it does not fix the literal sense of "subdue" and "rule", which is the exegetes' work ([`old-testament` 2.1](../old-testament/lessons/02-01-two-accounts-of-creation.md)).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. What a farm or a valley may take, and how fast, is prudence on empirical premises.

- **Trap:** "LS denies human dominion". LS 116 keeps it, as responsible stewardship.
- **Trap:** "LS 67 is Francis's green reading, optional" (demotion), or "LS corrected the Hebrew" (it rules on the Church's reading, not the philology).
- **Ceded:** the image of God and the doctrine of stewardship are [`creation-grace-last-things` 2.1](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md) (its card's *Stewardship* entry); the two Genesis accounts are [`old-testament` 2.1](../old-testament/lessons/02-01-two-accounts-of-creation.md).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### The cry of the earth and the cry of the poor

**Every true ecological approach turns into a social one: it hears the earth and the poor together.** LS 49 (paraphrased): the excluded, who are most of the planet's people, are often treated as an afterthought or collateral damage by professionals and centres of power far from them; an ecology worth the name must build justice into environmental debate, and so hear the cry of the earth and the cry of the poor at once. LS 48: the human and the natural environment deteriorate together, and the poorest suffer the gravest effects. *Magnifica Humanitas* 43 (2026): the two cries cannot be separated.

- **Level: rung 3** (LS), repeated in MH 43.
- **When the cries pull apart** (6.1 Example 2, the invented Sanduva, choosing between a coal plant in three years and financed solar in eight): the texts give priorities but not a verdict. For poor countries the priority must be ending extreme poverty, while they are bound to develop cleaner energy with help from the countries that grew rich by polluting (LS 172). Coal above all is to be replaced progressively and without delay, though until renewables are widely accessible the less harmful alternative is legitimate (LS 165). The poor may not be made to pay for others' emissions (LS 170). Which plan serves Sanduva's poor and the earth best turns on costs, timelines, health effects and finance: empirical premises, and the choice of its government and citizens (GS 43).

**Principle or application:** principle that both cries are heard; how they are weighed in a case is prudence. Catholics may back different plans without dissenting; none may say one cry does not count, or claim *Laudato Si'* for his plan. The rich countries' duty to help (LS 172) binds another party ([the duty of rich nations](#the-duty-of-rich-nations)).

- **Trap:** "the jobs settle it" (or "the emissions settle it"): hearing one cry only.
- **Trap:** dropping LS 165's qualifiers, "progressively" and the legitimacy of the less harmful alternative meanwhile, to make it a ban.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Throwaway culture

**A mentality that discards things and people alike, matched by an industrial system that has not learned to reuse its waste.** LS 22 (paraphrased): these problems are closely linked to a throwaway culture that affects the excluded as quickly as it turns things into rubbish; no circular model of production has yet been adopted. Also LS 16, 43 and 123, on the logic of "use and throw away". It is the companion of the [technocratic paradigm](#technocratic-paradigm) (LS 101, 106–109): a way of seeing comes before a way of producing. *Magnifica Humanitas* 43 (2026) names the mindset of waste among the threats to labour.

- **Level.** The moral critique is **rung 3** (LS), with the critique of the paradigm. LS's readings of particular trends are applications; claims about how much is wasted, and how fast, are empirical premises.

**Principle or application:** principle that persons are never disposable and goods are not made to be thrown away; every recycling target, product rule or waste charge is an application.

- **Trap:** "it is about rubbish". LS 22 joins discarded things to discarded people, the excluded first.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Intergenerational justice

**Future generations are inside the common good: what we owe them is justice, not generosity.** LS 159 (paraphrased): the common good reaches forward to future generations; there is no sustainable development without solidarity between the generations; that solidarity is no optional extra but a matter of basic justice, because the world we received belongs also to those who follow. Chapter 4's last section, "Justice between the generations", runs LS 159–162. LS 67 already asks each community to keep the earth fruitful for those who come after. 6.1 keeps [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md)'s promise of solidarity between generations here.

- **Level: rung 3** (LS 159), principle.
- **In use:** 6.1 Example 1, the invented Linnea valley aquifer falling a metre a year: no mining out the aquifer, because the common good includes those who will drink and farm there later (LS 159; LS 67). The safe yield is hydrology.

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. How future people are weighed in a cost-benefit appraisal, a safe yield, a phase-out date: empirical premises and prudence.

- **Trap:** "LS 159 fixes a discount rate". It says future people count in justice; how to discount them (Stern's 1.4 percent against Nordhaus's 5.5) is [`philosophy-of-economics` 4.2](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/04-02-the-ramsey-equation-and-the-stern-nordhaus-debate.md).
- **Trap (demotion):** treating care for the unborn generations as an optional extra. LS 159 says the opposite in so many words.
- **Sibling:** [`philosophy-of-debt` 6.2](../philosophy-of-debt/lessons/06-02-public-debt-and-the-unborn.md) uses LS 159 on public debt and points here.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Right to water

**Access to safe drinking water is a basic and universal human right: water's first destination is everyone's drinking, before any market in it.** LS 30 (paraphrased): safe drinking water is a basic and universal human right, because survival depends on it and other rights presuppose it; LS 30 notes a growing tendency in some places to privatize water and make it a market commodity, and says the world owes a serious social debt to the poor without drinking water, to be paid in part by funding clean water and sanitation. The context is LS 27–31: demand outstripping supply (28), unsafe water and the poor (29), and studies warning of acute shortage within decades (31). LS 164 counts universal access to drinking water among the aims of a global consensus. With LS 93–95, it is 3.5's [universal destination of goods](#universal-destination-of-goods) where it binds hardest: the environment is a collective good, and what we make our own we hold to administer for all (LS 95).

- **Level.** The right is **rung 3**, principle: the universal destination applied, in an encyclical, to a good essential to life. The privatization tendency and the scarcity forecasts are **empirical premises**. Who operates a supply (public body, cooperative, private firm) is an application, for [subsidiarity](#subsidiarity) and prudence ([3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md)).
- **In use:** 6.1 Example 1, the Linnea valley's pumping rule. Required: drinking water for every household first, the poorer east side's included (LS 30; LS 158). Permitted: caps, meters and charges beyond basic need; the farmers' well permits are real titles that carry the [social mortgage](#social-mortgage) and yield to regulation (LS 93). Forbidden: letting the deepest pumps win, or cutting the farms off with no regard for their work and food (LS 141).

**Principle or application:** principle that access comes first and the poor may not be priced out of drinking water; the cap, tariff, licence term and operator are prudence.

- **Trap:** "LS 30 bans private water firms or bottled water", or forbids selling water. It affirms a right of access and warns against making water a commodity ruled by the market at the cost of that access.
- **Two questions, not one:** what water is *for* (6.1) is a different question from who should *run* the supply ([3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md)'s question).
- **Bridge:** an aquifer is a common-pool resource in [`political-economy` 3.3](../political-economy/lessons/03-03-the-commons-and-common-pool-resources.md).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Carbon credits

**LS 171's hedged prudential judgment that trading "carbon credits" can become speculation and a ploy for the rich to keep consuming.** LS 171 (paraphrased, keeping its hedges): trading "carbon credits" *can lead* to a new kind of speculation that does nothing to lower global emissions; it *seems* to offer a quick, easy fix, yet permits none of the deep change the moment calls for; and it *may* end up as a device by which some countries and sectors go on over-consuming. It names no cap-and-trade scheme or carbon tax and endorses no instrument (LS 60, 188). Its neighbours are applications too: fossil fuels, coal above all, to be replaced progressively and without delay (LS 165); common but differentiated responsibilities, so that poorer countries are not penalized (LS 170); poor countries' priorities and the duty of those who grew rich by polluting (LS 172); enforceable international agreements (LS 173); stronger international institutions (LS 175, quoting CiV 67's world political authority: [international authority](#international-authority), 6.2's). *Laudate Deum* 59 hoped for a binding, efficient, readily monitored energy transition at COP28.

- **Level.** An **application** in a **rung-3** document: owed **serious and respectful consideration** (GS 43; *Donum Veritatis* 24), not religious submission. It is a judgment resting on an empirical premise, whether trading actually cuts emissions.

**Principle or application:** application. After weighing it, a Catholic may judge an instrument otherwise without dissenting; he may not call LS 171 weightless or claim the Church's authority for his own instrument. The principles of 6.1 bind whatever instrument is chosen.

- **Trap (inflation):** "LS 171 forbids Catholics to back permit trading", or "LS teaches a carbon tax".
- **Trap (demotion):** "LS 171 is only the Pope's opinion, so ignore it". It asks more than that.
- **Trap:** calling LS 171 a bare empirical claim. It is a judgment resting on one.
- **Trap:** dropping the hedges ("can lead", "seems", "may") to make it a verdict.
- **Bridge:** the instruments are [`public-economics` 3.1](../public-economics/lessons/03-01-taxes-standards-and-tradable-permits.md) (taxes, standards, tradable permits; grandfathering against auction) and [3.2](../public-economics/lessons/03-02-prices-vs-quantities.md) (prices against quantities).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Laudate Deum

**Francis's apostolic exhortation to all people of good will on the climate crisis (4 October 2023), a follow-up to *Laudato Si'*.** Given at the Basilica of St John Lateran on the feast of St Francis of Assisi, 4 October 2023. 73 sections in six chapters: the global climate crisis (5–19); a growing technocratic paradigm (20–33); the weakness of international politics (34–43); climate conferences, progress and failures (44–52); what to expect from COP28 in Dubai (53–60); spiritual motivations (61–73). What 6.1 takes (paraphrased): it answers those who deride the evidence, dismissive Catholics included (LD 5–14); it holds that the human origin of climate change can no longer be doubted (LD 11); it extends the [technocratic paradigm](#technocratic-paradigm) (LD 20–33); it hoped COP28 would adopt binding forms of energy transition, efficient and readily monitored (LD 59).

- **Level.** An **apostolic exhortation**, not an encyclical (*Magnifica Humanitas* note 220 so calls it): **rung 3 range**, mostly empirical (5–19) and applied (44–60). Its science has **no doctrinal rung**; LD 59 is an application.

**Principle or application:** it restates LS's principles; its new content is mostly empirical premise and application. Disputing LD 11's science is not dissent, though LD 14 criticizes dismissive and scarcely reasonable opinions and LS 188 asks for honest debate.

- **Trap:** calling it "the second ecological encyclical", or an encyclical at all.
- **Trap:** "LD 11 made the human cause of warming a doctrine". It is an empirical premise stated with confidence; it has no doctrinal rung.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Mass for the care of creation

**A Mass formulary for the care of creation (*pro custodia creationis*), added to the Roman Missal's Masses for various needs in 2025.** Decree of the Dicastery for Divine Worship and the Discipline of the Sacraments, dated 8 June 2025 (Pentecost) and published 3 July 2025; Leo XIV approved the formulary and its biblical readings, drawn up in Latin, and the dicastery declares it the typical text. The decree cites LS 2 and LS 236 (the Eucharist as a source of motivation for care of the environment). Source note: the Holy See Press Office bulletin that published it heads it, wrongly, as from the Dicastery for Legislative Texts.

- **Level: liturgical law, no doctrinal rung.** It is reception of the teaching in the Church's prayer, not new teaching.

**Principle or application:** neither: an act of worship and law. It adds no principle and settles no policy.

- **Trap:** "the Missal now binds Catholics to LS's policies or science". The formulary prays for creation's care; the levels of LS's claims are unchanged.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md)

### Superdevelopment

**A rich country can be badly developed too: an excess of goods for some that enslaves people to possession is as inadmissible as underdevelopment.** *Sollicitudo Rei Socialis* 28 (paraphrased): the merely economic idea of development has entered a crisis; beside the miseries of underdevelopment stands a superdevelopment, equally inadmissible: a glut of material goods for some groups, which easily enslaves people to possessing and to instant gratification. Drawing on the distinction between *having* and *being* (PP; GS), it holds that having perfects no one unless it serves his being; the evil is not having as such but possessing with no regard to which goods matter more. Development has a necessary economic dimension, but is not limited to it. *Caritas in Veritate* 22 finds a wasteful superdevelopment among some groups even in poor regions.

- **Level: rung 3** (encyclical, 1987), principle: the ordering of having to being, within [integral human development](#integral-human-development).

**Principle or application:** principle, owed religious submission. What counts as excess for a household or a nation, and what policy answers it, is application. The consumer's side, the choice of what to buy and how to live, is 4.4's [consumerism](#consumerism) (CA 36).

- **Trap (demotion):** "development is growth in output", or "integral development is 1960s economics". PP 14 and SRS 28 are rung 3.
- **Trap (over-correction):** "integral development is anti-growth", or condemns having as such. SRS 28 grants development a necessary economic dimension and blames disordered possession, not possession; CiV 21 credits growth with lifting billions out of misery.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### The duty of rich nations

**The rich owe help, fair terms and a share in the work: aid, just trade and a world community where no one's progress is bought at another's expense.** *Populorum Progressio* 44 (paraphrased): the duty falls first on the wealthier nations and is threefold: *mutual solidarity*, the aid the richer nations must give; *social justice*, the rectifying of trade relations between strong and weak nations; *universal charity*, a world community where all can give and receive and no one's progress is bought at another's expense. PP 49: what wealthier nations have beyond their needs should be put at poorer nations' disposal. SRS 32: the duty to commit oneself to the development of peoples binds nations, not only individuals. CA 52, which calls development another name for peace, adds a collective responsibility for development, like the one for avoiding war, which may cost the developed economies positions of income and power. CiV 60: developed nations should give a larger share of national product to aid, keeping the commitments the international community has undertaken; it names no figure.

- **Level: rung 3**, principle (PP 44, 49; SRS 32; CA 52). The share, the figure, the channel and the timing are **applications**.
- **Fixed elsewhere, not re-levelled:** the justice-duty where prosperity rests on resources not fairly paid for (CCC 2439) is **rung 3** in [`theology-of-debt` 6.2](../theology-of-debt/lessons/06-02-international-debt-and-the-jubilee-call.md). Source note: CCC 2439 carries no footnote; the notes to SRS 16 and CA 26 belong to CCC 2440.
- **The rich duty's partner** is the poor nations' own: [agents of their own development](#agents-of-their-own-development). The ground of solidarity between nations is the universal destination of goods (SRS 39; [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md)).

**Principle or application:** principle that the duty binds nations; every aid share, cut, pledge or channel is prudence, owed serious consideration where a document proposes it.

- **Trap (demotion):** "aid is charity, which the Church recommends and no nation owes". PP 44 and 49 make it a duty; SRS 32 makes it bind nations.
- **Trap (inflation):** "CiV 60 makes our aid pledge Church teaching". It names no figure; the duty is rung 3, the pledge an application.
- **Trap (both ways):** "any cut breaks Church teaching", or "no figure, so no duty". The duty may not be treated as optional charity that lapses to nothing; its size in a recession is prudence (6.2 P3).
- **Trap:** "development is the new name for peace" is PP's heading over §§76–87, not a numbered sentence; CA 52 has the sentence form ([2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md)).
- **Not taught here:** the debts of nations (CA 35, SRS 19, LS 51–52, CCC 2439, the Jubilee appeals, MH 172 on structural indebtedness) are `theology-of-debt` 6.2's.
- **Bridge:** [`political-philosophy` 6.1](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-01-global-justice-cosmopolitans-and-statists.md) states this teaching as one position between the cosmopolitans (Beitz, Pogge) and the statists, and argues the secular case.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Agents of their own development

**The poor are the subjects of development, never its objects: they owe initiative, reform and solidarity with one another, and aid must enlist them.** PP 77 (paraphrased): nations are the architects of their own development and must bear its burden, though they cannot achieve it in isolation. SRS 44: development demands above all a spirit of initiative from the countries that need it, not expecting everything from the more favoured; some must reform unjust structures, replacing corrupt and dictatorial government with participatory government; literacy and basic education are a direct contribution to development. SRS 45: developing nations owe solidarity to one another and to the neediest. CiV 47: a programme's beneficiaries should be directly involved in planning and running it.

- **Level: rung 3**, principle (PP 77; SRS 44–45; CiV 47). Which reforms come first is **application**: each local situation will show which are most urgent (SRS 43).

**Principle or application:** principle that poor nations are agents and owe initiative and self-reform; the order and design of reforms is theirs to judge.

- **Trap:** leaving the poor nation's own duties out, as though development were only something the rich do to the poor (6.2 P3).
- **In use:** 6.2 Example 1, *Programme B* in the invented Sarrin province: Pradovans are the agents (PP 77; SRS 44), and its accounts are open (CiV 47). See [aid and subsidiarity](#aid-and-subsidiarity).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Aid and subsidiarity

**Aid that does not build up the recipient's own capacity fails its purpose: subsidiarity and solidarity, joined, govern aid between nations.** *Caritas in Veritate* 58 (paraphrased) keeps [subsidiarity](#subsidiarity) and [solidarity](#solidarity) together (3.4's [pairing](#subsidiarity-and-solidarity)) and applies "this general rule" to international development aid: aid, whatever the donors' intentions, can lock people into dependence and even feed local oppression and exploitation; it must not pursue secondary objectives; it should be distributed with the involvement not only of the receiving government but of local economic agents and the cultural bodies of civil society, local Churches included; programmes should increasingly be shaped and finished by people at the grass roots; the most valuable resources in receiving countries are human. CiV 22: aid is diverted through irresponsibility in the chain of donors and the chain of beneficiaries alike. CiV 47: projects built on subsidiarity, with beneficiaries involved and results monitored; no solution is universally valid; agencies should publish what share of their funds reaches the programmes. CiV 60: a larger share of national product for aid, and letting citizens decide how part of their taxes is spent ("fiscal subsidiarity") as one possible approach, provided it does not degenerate into promoting special interests.

- **Level.** That aid must serve and build up the recipients' own agents rather than bypass them is **rung 3**, principle: subsidiarity applied, the general rule CiV 58 names. Which agents, in what mix, with what safeguards, the aid share and fiscal subsidiarity are **applications**. That aid can breed dependence (CiV 58) and that it is diverted (CiV 22) are **empirical premises** (3.4 marked the first so).

**Principle or application:** split, as CiV 58 itself is: the involvement rule binds; the channel list and the design are prudence.

- **In use:** 6.2 Example 1. *Programme A* (an Ostmoor contractor, foreign doctors, drugs bought from Ostmoor firms, reporting only to Ostmoor, clinics closing when the grant ends) fails CiV 58 on every count: a secondary aim, no receiving government, no local agents, no human capital left behind. *Programme B* (Sarrin's district boards and a nursing college with the diocese and a farmers' cooperative, rising local co-funding, published accounts) meets it. Its design and budget are still application; whether it saves more lives is empirical (CiV 47 wants results monitored).
- **Trap:** "CiV 58 forbids ever bypassing a corrupt government". It states a rule for involvement, not a ban on safeguards; CiV 22 admits diversion on both sides.
- **Trap:** "corruption cancels the subsidiarity rule". It is a reason for caution and safeguards, not a licence for aid that leaves nothing behind; the recipient owes reform and transparency ([agents of their own development](#agents-of-their-own-development)).
- **Trap:** levelling CiV 58 whole, either as binding policy or as mere counsel. The involvement rule is principle; the channel list application.
- **Evidence:** whether aid works is [`institutions-and-development`](../institutions-and-development/syllabus.md) 5.4's empirical question.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Trade between unequal partners

**Trade is judged by whether the weaker partner can really share in it: between very unequal economies, consent alone does not make the terms just.** *Populorum Progressio* (paraphrased), section by section. PP 57: the raw materials and crops poor countries sell swing sharply in price and do not share in the rising value of manufactures. PP 58: free trade alone can no longer govern international agreements; it works when the parties are roughly equal. PP 59: *Rerum Novarum*'s rule that the consent of very unequal parties does not by itself guarantee a fair contract (RN 45, on the wage: [just wage](#just-wage), 4.2) holds between nations too; unchecked competition can create an [economic dictatorship](#economic-dictatorship); free trade is just only when it meets the demands of social justice. PP 60: rich nations protect their own agriculture. PP 61: one standard should hold for all, so that what applies within rich economies applies to trade between rich and poor; competition is to be kept within limits, not abolished. Later: CA 33 judges that the chief problem *seems* to be fair access to the international market, since countries that cut themselves off stagnated while those that joined developed; CiV 58 calls market access the principal help poor countries need, and CiV 33 notes rich countries' high tariffs still block their products. Technology too: SRS 43 notes forms of technology denied or useless; MH 161 makes access to the benefits of innovation a matter of justice and notes wealth concentrated within and between countries.

- **Level.** PP 59's principle on unequal consent and PP 61's one standard: **rung 3**, principle. PP 57's terms of trade, CA 33's record of closed economies and MH 161's concentration of wealth: **empirical premises**, no rung. CA 33's chief-problem judgment, CiV 58's market-access counsel, and every tariff, phase-in or treaty: **applications**. The "just only when" sentence is PP 59, not PP 61.

**Principle or application:** principle that unequal consent does not make terms just, by one standard for all; whether a given agreement meets social justice is an application for the governments and negotiators concerned, the most exposed heard.

- **In use:** 6.2 Example 2, the invented Pradova–Ostmoor agreement (tariff-free coffee and cloth for zero tariffs on machinery and grain in five years, while Ostmoor keeps subsidizing its grain). Decided: consent alone does not make the terms fair (PP 59); demanding open grain markets while protecting one's own breaks PP 61's one standard; using a partner's need to extract terms an equal would refuse is forbidden; yet market access is real help, so refusing all trade is not required. Required: the stronger answers for the weaker (SRS 39, applied to nations). Where it stops: whether Pradova's poor gain on net is an empirical premise; whether to sign, renegotiate or phase in is for the two governments, Pradova's farmers heard.
- **Trap (inflation):** "PP condemns free trade", or "PP 59 forbids this treaty", or "voting for the bill is dissent".
- **Trap (demotion):** "PP 56–61 is refuted dependency economics, so set it aside". Half right: PP 57's prices are revisable, but PP 59's principle survives any revision of them.
- **Trap:** "CA 33 made open markets doctrine", or "PP 59 made them suspect". CA 33 is a hedged application on an empirical record; PP 59 a principle that settles no tariff. Catholic liberal readers lean on the one, progressive readers on the other ([6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)).
- **Evidence:** who gains from trade, and the governance of the WTO, IMF and World Bank, are [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) 5.4–5.5's.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Sollicitudo Rei Socialis

**John Paul II's encyclical for the twentieth anniversary of *Populorum Progressio* (dated 30 December 1987): authentic development after twenty years, and the source of the corpus's language of solidarity and structures of sin.** An encyclical letter, given at St Peter's on 30 December 1987. What the lessons take from it, by section:

- **§3** the teaching is constant in its principles and criteria of judgment, ever new in adapting them to changed conditions: the corpus's own statement of [principle and application](#principle-and-application) ([1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md), 6.2).
- **§15** suppressing the right of economic initiative kills creative subjectivity, as experience shows (an empirical premise: [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)).
- **§19** international debt (`theology-of-debt` 6.2's, not taught here).
- **§§20–22** the East–West blocs: two imperfect concepts of development, liberal capitalism and Marxist collectivism, whose rivalry held the South back (6.2 T7).
- **§28** [superdevelopment](#superdevelopment); having and being. **§32** the duty to development binds nations ([the duty of rich nations](#the-duty-of-rich-nations)).
- **§§36–37** a world of blocs and imperialisms is subject to [structures of sin](#structures-of-sin), rooted in personal sin and concrete acts ([3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md), 6.2).
- **§§38–40** [solidarity](#solidarity) as a virtue, a determined and lasting commitment to the good of all and of each; **§39** applies it by analogy between nations, grounded on the goods of creation being meant for all ([3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md), [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)).
- **§41** the Church has no technical solutions for underdevelopment as such; her doctrine is no "third way" between liberal capitalism and Marxist collectivism but a category of its own, belonging to moral theology ([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md); [the doctrine's disclaimers](#the-doctrines-disclaimers)).
- **§42** the option or love of preference for the poor, the universal destination of goods and the [social mortgage](#social-mortgage) in one paragraph ([3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md), [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-work-and-the-worker-laborem-exercens.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md), 6.4).
- **§43** four reforms (the trade system, the monetary and financial system, technology, the international organizations, whose review is wanted "in the opinion of many"), with the rider that each local situation will show which are most urgent. **§§44–45** [agents of their own development](#agents-of-their-own-development).

- **Level.** The principles are **rung 3**, owed religious submission; none is defined (can. 749 §3). The category of §36 is **rung 3** as [`moral-theology` 3.5](../moral-theology/lessons/03-05-social-sin-and-cooperation-in-the-sins-of-others.md) fixed it. §43's reforms and the 1987 verdict on the blocs are **applications**. The Catechism locates it (CCC 2442 cites SRS 47 and 42) and adds no weight.
- **Text note.** The vatican.va English of §41 says the Church has no technical "revolutions" to offer; the Latin reads *solutiones technicae*, technical solutions. Paraphrase it as "technical solutions"; never quote that English.

**Principle or application:** SRS marks the split itself (§3; §43's rider). Its principles bind; its programme of reforms and its reading of the 1987 world ask serious, respectful consideration.

- **Trap:** "SRS 41 says the Church has no competence in economics, so its verdicts are opinions". It disclaims technical solutions and any system, and in the same section claims moral judgment, as moral theology.
- **Trap:** reading SRS 20–22's criticism of both blocs as a third system the Church prefers. SRS 41 denies exactly that.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md)

### Distributism

**Spread productive property among as many owners as possible: Belloc's and Chesterton's lay remedy for both capitalism (property in few hands) and collectivism (property in the state's).** Hilaire Belloc, *The Servile State* (T. N. Foulis, 1912), and G. K. Chesterton, *The Outline of Sanity* (Methuen, 1926; quoted from the Dodd, Mead edition, New York, 1927), essays from *G. K.'s Weekly*; both public domain in the US. Chesterton names a policy of "small distributed property" against "the two extremes of Capitalism and Communism" (Part I, ch. 1): the family shop and farm, local trade over big shops, and, where a business cannot be small, profit-sharing or guild ownership. His book is an outline admitting "many varieties" (I, ch. 4), to be achieved "by people", not "to people", step by step (Part VI). 6.3's Source (I, ch. 1) runs diagnosis, remedy, rival: the cure for centralization is decentralization; capital too much in the hand of the few should be restored to the many; the Socialist would put it in even fewer hands; and "what we call Capitalism ought to be called Proletarianism". The Distributist League followed in 1926. Belloc's forecast is [the servile state](#the-servile-state).

- **Level: no magisterial level.** A lay program of lay writers and a lay movement. It applies a **rung-3** principle, [widening ownership](#widening-ownership): law should "induce as many as possible of the humbler class to become owners" (RN 46, Benziger 1903), restated in QA 59–63 ([redemption of the proletariat](#redemption-of-the-proletariat)), MM 113 and GS 71. As an application it is owed serious, respectful consideration, not religious submission. Whether it is the teaching's natural implementation is **rung 5**.
- **Same direction, not the program** (6.3's thesis). The documents want many owners. They also hold the wage contract not unjust in itself (QA 64), allow property with too great a power to be reserved to the State (QA 114) and public property generally (GS 71), commend partnership and shares in medium and large firms (QA 65; MM 115), count ownership of the means of production, private or public, legitimate as it serves labour (LE 14) and useful work (CA 43), extend Leo's reasons to every form of property, know-how included (CA 6, 32), and accept a market economy within [CA 42](#centesimus-annus-42)'s conditions. The Church has no models to present (CA 43) and no "third way" (SRS 41).
- **On 6.4's map** (T5): a fourth position beside the three readings. It shares the progressive's and the integralist's judgment of the market, proposes widely spread small property as the remedy, and as a programme is silent on the state's duty toward religion.
