**Principle or application:** application of a rung-3 principle, with empirical premises of its own: Belloc's instability thesis and forecast; whether small property stays small (Chesterton reports Bernard Shaw denying it); every claim about worker-owned firms' efficiency. A Catholic who weighs distributism and prefers employee shares dissents from nothing; he may not deny that ownership should be spread, treat the papal proposals as weightless, or claim the Church's authority for his own scheme (GS 43).

- **Trap (inflation):** "distributism is the Church's own economics", or "RN 46 makes distributism doctrine". RN 46 teaches a direction at rung 3 and leaves the means; preferring small property to large, and Belloc's "no third course", are lay proposals with no magisterial level.
- **Trap (demotion):** "nostalgia for three acres and a cow, with no standing in Catholic teaching", or "RN 46 is a Victorian aside". It demotes a principle that QA 59–63, MM 113 and GS 71 restate.
- **Trap:** "Chesterton's 'Capitalism' is what CA 42 accepts". He means wage-dependence on a small owning class, and says that if capitalism means private property he is a capitalist. CA 42 accepts a market economy only within a strong juridical framework serving a freedom whose core is ethical and religious. Same word, different referents.
- **Trap:** quoting "too much capitalism does not mean too many capitalists, but too few capitalists" from Chesterton. It is not in *The Outline of Sanity*, which says "too much capitalism in the one sense or too little capitalism in the other" (I, ch. 1). Treat the tag as a later summary of uncertain source.
- **Bridges:** Rawls's property-owning democracy, taken from James Meade, is widened ownership argued secularly ([`political-philosophy` 6.4](../political-philosophy/lessons/06-04-welfare-property-and-subsidiarity.md)); Paine's endowment and the Jubilee's limit on permanent dispossession are cousins ([`philosophy-of-debt` 5.4](../philosophy-of-debt/lessons/05-04-jubilee-as-a-moral-principle.md)); the left's case against a smallholder society, Marx's peasants "like potatoes in a sack", is [`history-of-political-thought` 6.6](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/06-06-marx-the-state-revolution-and-after.md).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### The servile state

**Belloc's name for the stable society he predicted unstable capitalism would become: the propertyless bound by positive law to work for owners, in return for security.** *The Servile State* (Foulis, 1912). The definition (Section I) concerns a society in which so many "are constrained by positive law to labour for the advantage of other families" that such labour marks the whole community; servility, for Belloc, is compulsion by positive law, not labour from need or love of gain. His thesis in outline, as 6.3 T1 and its map give it: capitalism, ownership by a few joined to freedom for all, cannot last (Section V); a reformer who keeps freedom must give property to many or to none: "The first model we call *Socialism* or the Collectivist State; the second we call the Proprietary or Distributive State" (VI). Collectivist reform, he predicts, will yield the servile state instead (VII–VIII), and he counted the Insurance Act (the UK National Insurance Act of 1911) among its first signs (IX). Belloc counts three stable arrangements (Distributive, Collectivist, Servile); 6.3's map adds the unstable capitalist state as a fourth node.

- **Level: no magisterial level.** The thesis and forecast are Belloc's **empirical premises**; his reading of social insurance is his own **application**.
- **Where the documents part from him:** they list insurance for old age and accidents at work among workers' rights (LE 19) and social insurance for old age and unemployment among goals still valid (CA 34). The principle of [widening ownership](#widening-ownership) wants many owners; it does not adopt his "no third course", since the documents combine wages, partnership, public and private property ([distributism](#distributism)).

**Principle or application:** neither: a lay writer's thesis, forecast and applications, to be weighed on evidence and on their merits.

- **Trap:** "Belloc's verdict on the Insurance Act is Catholic teaching". The documents do the opposite (LE 19; CA 34).
- **Trap:** "the servile-state thesis is a doctrine, so doubting the forecast is disloyal". It is an empirical forecast with no magisterial level.
- **Reserved for Boss 6(b).** Close-reading Belloc's Introduction (from "That our free modern society" to "beneath which they shall not fall"), reconstructing its argument in numbered premises, and saying which premises RN 46 or *Quadragesimo Anno* supports and which are Belloc's own, is the Boss's. 6.3 quotes only the Section VI sentence and a fragment of Section I.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md)

### Redemption of the proletariat

**Pius XI's name for Leo XIII's goal: that the propertyless workers be raised, by wages that allow saving, to owning some modest property.** *Quadragesimo Anno* (15 May 1931, paraphrased). The Latin heading is *Redemptio proletariorum* (QA 59); vatican.va's English renders it the redemption of the "non-owning workers". QA 59: this is the goal Leo set. QA 61–63: the fruits of production should reach workers in ample sufficiency, so that by thrift they can increase their property, carry family burdens securely and provide for those they leave; without some little property for the non-owning workers, Leo's principles cannot be put into practice; hence the wage question. Around it: the wage contract is not unjust in itself (QA 64, **rung 3**, [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md)); in the present condition of society, and so far as possible, a partnership contract should modify it, giving workers a share in ownership, management or profits (QA 65); property carrying too great a power may rightly be reserved to the State (QA 114).

- **Level.** The goal is **rung 3**, principle, restating RN 46 ([widening ownership](#widening-ownership)); the repetition adds weight within the rung (LG 25), not a definition. QA 65's partnership contract is an **application**, tied by its own words to present conditions and to what is possible. Which property QA 114 would reserve to the State is an application ([4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md)).

**Principle or application:** principle that the propertyless should become owners; the scheme by which they do (partnership, shares, small property, savings plans) is prudence.

- **Trap:** "QA 65 requires co-ownership". It drops QA 65's two conditions, the present state of society and what is possible; [worker participation](#worker-participation) is the same story in LE 14.
- **Trap:** "Pius XI made small property binding on every Catholic". The goal binds as a goal; QA itself favours partnership in large firms (65), and MM 115 names shares in medium and large firms.
- **Copyright:** paraphrase QA; do not quote the vatican.va English.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md)

### Cooperatives and worker ownership

**Firms owned and governed by those who work in them, and schemes that give employees shares in their employer: widened ownership with institutions.** As 6.3 T7 gives them: pupils of the Basque priest José María Arizmendiarrieta founded Mondragón's first cooperative, Ulgor, in 1956; defenders cite Mondragón's seven decades, critics the 2013 bankruptcy of Fagor Electrodomésticos, Ulgor's descendant. Louis Kelso designed the first US employee stock ownership plan (1956), given statutory form in 1974. Chiara Lubich's [economy of communion](#economy-of-communion) (1991) is a cousin. *Magnifica Humanitas* 155 (2026) credits cooperatives, with associations and unions, with a decisive part in labour law and the protection of the vulnerable, but judges them no longer sufficient by themselves. The documents behind them: LE 14 (joint ownership of the means of work, a share in management or profits, shareholding by labour, each worker a part-owner of the great workbench: proposals whose concrete applicability LE leaves open); QA 65 (partnership); MM 115 (shares in medium and large firms); CA 43 (workers' wider participation in the firm). See [worker participation](#worker-participation).

- **Level.** Every institution here has **no magisterial level**. The principle behind them (widened ownership; ownership legitimate as it serves labour) is **rung 3**. Papal praise or criticism of a form (CiV 46's naming; MH 155's verdict) is an **application**.
- **Empirical premises, reported, not ruled on:** scale (some goods are cheapest from large plants: returns to scale, [`micro-refresher` 3.1](../micro-refresher/lessons/03-01-technology-production.md)); capital (worker-owned firms struggle to raise equity without ceding control); risk (members hold wages and savings in one firm). Evidence runs both ways (Mondragón; Fagor). How to test a claim about why cooperatives survive is [`social-theory` 1.4](../social-theory/lessons/01-04-testing-a-social-explanation.md).

**Principle or application:** application of a rung-3 principle. Which forms to foster, how and at what cost is prudence for owners, workers and legislators, informed by economists.

- **In use:** 6.3 Example 1, the invented Ambleford's rate relief for firms at least half employee-owned. The aim is commended (RN 46; MM 115); the 30 percent, the threshold and the funding are applications; whether relief creates employee ownership or only rewards firms that have it is empirical; the limit is RN 47, since a rise that falls on small shops drains the owners §46 favours. A councillor preferring a buy-out loan fund dissents from nothing.
- **In use:** 6.3 Example 2, the Larkspur Mills cooperative facing a cheaper automated rival. Widely held ownership favours saving it (RN 46; LE 14), but the wage answers to the firm's condition and to employment (QA 72, 74), and a large firm's ownership is legitimate if it serves useful work (CA 43). Nothing says the mill must survive at any price, or that the market's verdict settles its worth; the members judge first, the council any loan, on evidence.
- **Trap:** "QA 65 or LE 14 requires co-ownership". QA 65 ties partnership to present conditions and to what is possible; LE 14 leaves open whether each proposal can be applied.
- **Sibling cases, not re-set:** 4.3's Maresh (compulsory conversion of firms over 50 workers: proportionate or not is prudential), and 3.3's village water cooperatives.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md)

### Economy of communion

**A Focolare initiative of businesses that keep the profit motive but put part of their profits at the service of the poor and of a culture of giving.** Launched by Chiara Lubich in 1991 (6.3 T7). *Caritas in Veritate* 46 (2009, paraphrased) names it, beside the "civil economy", within a broad intermediate area between profit and non-profit enterprise: a mixed reality that allows profit but uses it for human and social purposes; whether such firms distribute dividends is secondary; it hopes they find fitting juridical and fiscal forms; and it holds that a plurality of institutional forms makes the market more civilized and more competitive ([civil economy](#civil-economy), [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md)).

- **Level.** The initiative itself: **no magisterial level**. CiV 46's naming and hope: an **application**, owed serious, respectful consideration. That markets grow more competitive with more forms of enterprise is an **empirical premise** (4.4). The principle beneath, that gratuitousness belongs inside economic life, is **rung 3** ([economy of gift](#economy-of-gift), 4.4's).

**Principle or application:** application. A Catholic owner may weigh it and organize his firm otherwise without dissent.

- **Trap (inflation):** "CiV obliges Catholic owners to join the economy of communion", or to become non-profits, so that expecting a dividend is dissent. CiV 46 calls dividends secondary and binds no one to a form (4.4).
- **Trap (demotion):** dismissing CiV's chapter on gift as pious decoration. Its principle is rung 3.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md)

### Progressive reading

**The reading that takes the corpus through the option for the poor, read as a structural critique, with Francis as recovering its centre: the poor are the key, and the corpus has been learning to read itself by them.** As [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) T4 gives it:

- **Its centre:** sin takes shape in [structures](#structures-of-sin) (SRS 36), and an economy is judged by those it excludes (*Evangelii Gaudium* 53, its no to an economy of exclusion and inequality).
- **Its historians:** Charles Curran (*Catholic Social Teaching, 1891–Present*, 2002) traces a shift from a classicist to a historically conscious method, with DH its best conciliar instance; Donal Dorr (*Option for the Poor*, 1983, revised 1992) traces a century of Vatican teaching moving toward the poor.
- **Its Francis:** the option is a theological category (EG 198), and [the cry of the earth and the cry of the poor](#the-cry-of-the-earth-and-the-cry-of-the-poor) are heard together (LS 49). It reads the corpus chiefly through *Sollicitudo Rei Socialis* and Francis.
- **Its neighbours** in 6.4: the [Catholic liberal reading](#catholic-liberal-reading), [political integralism](#integralism), and [distributism](#distributism) as a fourth position.

- **Level.** **No magisterial level**. Its distinctive premise, that the option is the key to the whole corpus, is **rung 5**. What it builds on is the floor: the [preferential option for the poor](#preferential-option-for-the-poor), never exclusive, is **rung 3** (SRS 42; CA 57), and so is the category of structures of sin (as `moral-theology` 3.5 fixes it). Its programme, a structural agenda, is an **application**, which a Catholic may weigh and reject without dissent (GS 43). Leo XIV's address to the popular movements (23 October 2025: land, housing and work as sacred rights), which this reading quotes, is **rung 3 lightest** at most, a text it appeals to, not the Pope taking its side.

**Principle or application:** neither a principle nor an application of the Magisterium: a theologians' reading, whose key premise is rung 5 and whose programme is an application.

- **Trap (inflation):** "the option is the whole of it", the inflation 6.4 names for this reading.
- **Trap (demotion, the mirror):** "the option is a slogan".
- **Trap:** "Francis replaced John Paul II", or "*Centesimus Annus* 42's yes has lapsed". No act withdraws CA 42; *Magnifica Humanitas* 39 (2026) restates its conditional acceptance of the market, and MH 45's harmonious but not always linear development takes no reading's side.
- **Background:** what the 1984 and 1986 instructions affirmed and criticized in liberation theology is [3.6](lessons/03-06-the-preferential-option-for-the-poor.md)'s ([liberation theology](#liberation-theology)).
- **Secular frame:** the structural critique meets Young's structural injustice in [`political-philosophy` 4.4](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-04-feminist-political-philosophy.md).

**Reserved for Boss 6(c).** Stating the three readings as their best defenders would, and naming the single premise on which each pair disagrees, is the Boss's; 6.4 T6 only locates where the readings part.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### John Courtney Murray

**The American Jesuit whose reading of religious liberty, and of the American settlement, anchors the Catholic liberal reading.** From [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md): silenced by his superiors in 1955, a conciliar *peritus* from 1963, he shaped the text of *Dignitatis Humanae*, which passed 2,308 to 70 ([`church-history` 9.2](../church-history/lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)). For Murray, DH teaches a juridical principle, government's incompetence in religion, and Leo XIII answered conditions now gone. From [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) T2: *We Hold These Truths* (1960) reads the American religion clauses as "articles of peace", not articles of faith; reads Leo XIII's confessional teaching as shaped by the paternal states and anticlerical liberalism of his century; and argues that limited constitutional government has no competence to judge religious truth. In 6.4 Example 2, the liberal, with Murray, doubts a government's competence to honour religious truth.

- **Level: no magisterial level.** He shaped the drafting; the act is the Council's, and his account of what it declares is a theologian's reading.

**Principle or application:** neither: a reading of the documents, weighed on the texts.

- **Trap:** treating Murray's reading as what DH itself teaches because he helped write it. The declaration's words and levels are fixed by the act ([Dignitatis Humanae](#dignitatis-humanae)), not by its drafter.
- **Bridges:** the "articles of peace" meet the religion clauses in [`constitutional-law`](../constitutional-law/syllabus.md) Module 7; whether accepting religious liberty is principled or a truce while outnumbered is the modus vivendi question of [`political-philosophy` 4.1](../political-philosophy/lessons/04-01-political-liberalism.md).

**Reserved for Boss 5(c) and 6(c).** Stating Murray's reading and Pink's at full strength, the crux between them about whose authority to coerce DH addresses, and which reading better fits the text, are Boss 5(c)'s; stating the Catholic liberal reading as its defenders would is Boss 6(c)'s.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Thomas Pink

**The philosopher whose narrow reading of *Dignitatis Humanae* is the integralist's main textual account of the declaration: DH addresses the state's coercive authority as a civil power, and changed policy, not doctrine.** From [5.2](lessons/05-02-religious-liberty-dignitatis-humanae.md): Pink (*Nova et Vetera*, 2013, replying to Martin Rhonheimer's hermeneutic-of-reform reading of 2011) reads DH as addressing the state's coercive authority as a civil authority, not the Church's authority over the baptized (Trent, session VII, on baptism, can. 14), which states once exercised on her behalf; DH 1's clause then means what it says. From [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) T3 ("Conscience and Coercion", *First Things*, 2012): DH denies coercive authority in religion to the state as a civil power, and so changed the Church's policy, not her doctrine. DH is read by integralists either so, narrowly, or as rupture.

- **Level: no magisterial level.**
- **Not a rejection of DH.** Pink's reading claims to keep DH's teaching by reading its scope; a reading that refuses DH 2 any assent is a different position, and demotes a **rung-3** act (6.4 Watch out).

**Principle or application:** neither: a reading of the documents, weighed on the texts.

- **Trap:** "Pink's reading rejects the Council". It claims to keep DH and argues about what DH addresses.
- **Trap (the mirror):** "DH condemned integralism". DH 1 affirms the moral duty of societies toward the true religion (**at least rung 3**) and DH 6 permits special civil recognition on its conditions ([duty of societies toward the true religion](#duty-of-societies-toward-the-true-religion); [confessional state](#confessional-state)).

**Reserved for Boss 5(c) and 6(c).** Stating Pink's reading against Murray's at full strength, naming the crux between them, and arguing which better fits DH's text, are Boss 5(c)'s; stating the integralist reading as its defenders would is Boss 6(c)'s.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

### Common-good constitutionalism

**Adrian Vermeule's theory of how judges in a secular constitutional order should read law: in the classical legal tradition, toward peace, justice and abundance.** *Common Good Constitutionalism* (2022), as 6.4 T3 gives it. It is a neighbour of [political integralism](#integralism), not the same thing: a theory of adjudication, which does not rest on the two powers or on the subordination of the temporal power to the spiritual. On 6.4's map it sits in a dashed box beside the integralist.

- **Level: no magisterial level** (a jurisprudential theory).

**Principle or application:** neither: a theory of law, outside the Magisterium's acts.

- **Trap:** filing it under integralism. It answers how judges should read the written law, not what the temporal power owes the spiritual.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md)

## Terms

Technical vocabulary in first-appearance order across all 28 lessons (problems included; Connections and Flashbacks not counted). Where a term first appears before the lesson that teaches it, both are given. Positions with a full entry are under Positions and distinctions; this table is the one-line gloss. Post-1930 documents are paraphrased; a few words in quotation marks are terms of art.

| Term | Means | First used |
|---|---|---|
| the social question | how to fit the tradition's principles to industrial wage labour, the nineteenth century's question | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| competence in the moral order | the Church's claim to judge what social arrangements do to persons, not how to run them (GS 76) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| the disclaimers | the corpus's own statements that it offers no technical solutions, model, system, third way or party (SRS 41; CA 43; GS 42, 76) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| "third way" | the description SRS 41 refuses: social doctrine is not a middle system between liberal capitalism and Marxist collectivism but a category of its own | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| the corpus | papal, conciliar and curial teaching on social questions, 1891 to the present, defined by subject and issuer, not by a canon | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| the social encyclicals | a scholars' list of the major documents; a convention, not a promulgated canon, and not all of them encyclicals | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| social doctrine of the Church; Catholic social teaching | the Church's moral teaching on life in society; both names are in use, and MH 29 dates the title, not the words (QA 87's Latin has *doctrina socialis christiana*) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| *Compendium* | the Pontifical Council for Justice and Peace's compilation (Presentation dated 2 April 2004): a map of the corpus with no magisterial level of its own | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| shared discernment | MH 27's name for the doctrine at work: not a handbook of norms awaiting application, but discernment where the Gospel meets history's questions | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| evangelizing mission | teaching social doctrine is part of evangelization, since it draws out the Gospel's consequences for social life (CA 5, 54; CiV 15) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| the lay faithful's direct duty | the concrete ordering of society belongs properly, though not exclusively, to lay Catholics (GS 43; DCE 29); pastors teach the principles | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md) |
| civilization of love | Paul VI's phrase; CA 10 counts it among the older names of solidarity, and the *Compendium* closes on it | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| pastoral constitution | *Gaudium et Spes*'s genre: doctrine brought to bear on present conditions; its first note says it rests on doctrinal principles | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| grade within a rung | LG 25's signs (character of the document, frequency, manner of speaking) make a teaching weigh more or less without moving it to another rung | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md) |
| permanent principle | a moral truth that holds whatever the facts, for every time, economy and regime; owed religious submission at its rung (usually rung 3) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| religious submission of mind and will | the assent a principle taught authentically (rung 3) is owed, graded by genre, frequency and manner (LG 25) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| *res novae* | "new things": Leo XIII's word for the industrial upheaval, taken up by Leo XIV for digitalization, AI and robotics (MH 4) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [4.5](lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md) |
| empirical premise | a claim about how the world is, taken from the sciences; it has no doctrinal rung and stands or falls with the evidence | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| prudential judgment | a judgment about what to do here and now, joining principle to facts; owed serious consideration, open to sincere disagreement | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| inflation; demotion | the two symmetrical errors: making an application a binding principle; making a principle a preference | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| common good | the sum of the conditions of social life that let groups and their members reach their fulfilment more fully and readily (GS 26; MM 65) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md) |
| solidarity | SRS 38: a firm will committed to the good of all and of each; a virtue taught at rung 3, not a feeling | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| intergenerational justice | LS 159: intergenerational solidarity is a matter of justice, since the world we received belongs also to those who follow | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [6.1](lessons/06-01-integral-ecology-laudato-si.md) |
| serious, respectful consideration | the assent an application asks: weigh it loyally; one may then judge it otherwise without dissenting | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-social-doctrine-sources-genre-and-claim.md); taught [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| contingent application | a principle joined to empirical premises in a judgment about present conditions: every figure, institution, policy and reading of the times | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| frugal and well-behaved wage-earner | RN 45's measure of the wage floor (Benziger 1903): pay owed in natural justice, whatever was agreed | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md) |
| boards (RN 45) | Leo's preferred means for settling hours and wages, with the State as backstop "should circumstances require"; the means are application | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| elements of permanent and transitory value | GS's first note: Part Two mixes the permanent with the transitory; both parts rest on doctrine | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| non-combatant immunity | no direct attack on those not engaged in fighting; an absolute limit on conduct in war | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [5.3](lessons/05-03-war-and-peace.md) |
| "no first use" | *The Challenge of Peace*'s example of an application people of good will may read differently | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [5.4](lessons/05-04-deterrence-and-the-challenge-to-just-war.md) |
| *recognitio* | the Holy See's review a conference doctrinal declaration needs when not passed unanimously (*Apostolos Suos* 22) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| five principles | MH 46's list, to be held together: the common good, the universal destination of goods, subsidiarity, solidarity, social justice | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| universal destination of goods | God destined the earth and its goods for every person; the first principle of the social order of property (GS 69; LE 19) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| subsidiarity | a higher body must not absorb what a lesser one can do, and must help it do it (QA 79-80; CA 48) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| social justice | the justice that brings distribution back under the common good and asks of each what it requires (QA 57-58; DR 51) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md) |
| particular teaching | a bishops' conference teaches authentically in its own territory, never universally, and cannot move a universal level | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| the standing question | 1.2's working test: would the sentence still hold if the facts it mentions changed? If yes, a principle; if not, an application or a premise | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md) |
| directive (MM 239) | a practical decision of the Hierarchy on carrying out principles; binds conduct as discipline, not assent to its truth | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| family allowance | social measures supplementing pay for family needs, the alternative LE 19 allows | [1.2](lessons/01-02-permanent-principles-and-contingent-applications.md); taught [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md) |
| see, judge, act | the three-stage method (Cardijn; MM 236): facts, then principles, then action; a method commended, with no rung | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| JOC | Jeunesse Ouvrière Chrétienne (Young Christian Workers): Cardijn's movement, named 1924, blessed by Pius XI in 1925 | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| Catholic Action | the organized lay apostolate under the hierarchy, of which the JOC was a specialized movement | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| signs of the times | the events and movements of an age, which the Church must examine and read in the light of the Gospel (GS 4) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| rightful autonomy of earthly affairs | created things and societies have their own laws, which each science uncovers by its own methods; never independence from the Creator (GS 36) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| natural law as common language | the ground on which PT addresses all men of good will: an order written into human nature, from which rights and duties flow | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| men of good will | *Pacem in Terris*'s addressees, beyond the faithful (PT title; MH 33) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md); taught [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| rights and duties | PT's pairing: every natural right carries a duty, in the one who holds it and in others (PT 28-30) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md); taught [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| instability objection | the charge that an inductive method makes the teaching unstable; the reply: stable principles, revisable judgments | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md) |
| just wage | the wage owed in justice: a floor (RN 45), three measures (QA 71-74) and the family measure (LE 19) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-see-judge-act.md); taught [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md) |
| surplus; superfluous goods | what remains over after one's needs; alms from it are a precept, not an option (q.32 a.5; RN 22; GS 69) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| liberal political economy | the view that a wage is a price, just when freely agreed, and that the state should abstain | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| private property | a natural right and the ordinary means by which the universal destination is secured, its use held as common (RN 6; CA 30) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md); taught [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| power to procure and dispense; use | Aquinas's two relations to external goods: managing them may be private; their use is held "as common" (ST II-II q.66 a.2) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| "as common" | in use: ready to share with others in their need; not co-ownership (q.66 a.2) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| addition devised by human reason | q.66 a.2 ad 1: the division of possessions is not against natural law but a human addition to it | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| "a debt, not a gift" | Ambrose (quoted PP 23): giving to the poor returns what is theirs | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| extreme necessity | urgent need with no other remedy, in which taking is not theft (q.66 a.7; GS 69); its conditions are `history-of-political-thought` 2.4's | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md); taught [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| workers' circles | de Mun and La Tour du Pin's Catholic workers' circles, after 1871 | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| Fribourg Union | the study group Mermillod gathered from 1884; one source among several for RN | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| judicial precepts | Israel's civil law (the same-day wage, the Jubilee): no longer binding as such | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md) |
| community of goods | common ownership administered by the State or municipal bodies, socialism's "main tenet" (RN 4, 15) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md); taught [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| natural right | a right a person has by nature, prior to the State, which the State must protect (RN 6, 51) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-before-1891-the-roots-and-the-social-question.md); taught [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| private and public society | RN 51: civil society exists for the common good; societies formed within it for their members' advantage are private | [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| "absolutely, and as such" | RN 51: the State may not ban association as a kind, only a particular society for evident cause (RN 52) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| widening ownership | RN 46: the law should induce as many as possible of the humbler class to become owners | [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| "a general and lasting law" | RN 57's own marker of a principle for associations, against forms left to "circumstances of fact and of time" (RN 56) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-rerum-novarum.md) |
| corporative state | the interwar regimes (Portugal 1933, Austria 1934) whose makers claimed QA's proposal; QA 91-95 describes such syndicates with fears | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md) |
| Industries and Professions | the vatican.va English for QA's *ordines* (QA 81-87); "vocational orders" is the course's name, not QA's English | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md) |
| commutative justice | justice between two parties: it protects what is owned (QA 47) and governs this employer's contract with this worker | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-just-wage.md) |
| social charity | QA 88: the soul of the juridical and social order that social justice builds | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md) |
| partnership contract | QA 65: wage contracts mixed with workers' sharing in ownership, management or profits; advisable in present conditions | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [6.3](lessons/06-03-distributism-and-the-alternative-proposals.md) |
| subsidiary function | QA 80's name for subsidiarity | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| social function of property | ownership's service of the common good (GS 71's social quality), which MH 66 calls Church doctrine in so many words | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| economic dictatorship | QA 105-109: concentrated power of those who control credit, which replaced free competition | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) |
| "internationalism of finance" | QA 109 (vatican.va English), beside economic nationalism; not PP 26's "international imperialism of money" | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) |
| moderate socialism | QA 111-113's socialism that renounces violence; its just demands are not peculiar to it, but while it remains truly socialism it cannot be reconciled with Catholic teaching | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) |
| intermediate bodies | families, associations, unions, cooperatives and communities between person and State, which subsidiarity protects (MM 65; CA 48-49) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| "truly socialism" | QA 117's condition: the judgment holds of socialism as doctrine, historical fact or movement while it remains truly socialism | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) |
| twofold character of ownership | ownership has an individual and a social side (QA 45); deny either and you wreck on individualism or collectivism (QA 46) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-quadragesimo-anno-and-the-interwar-proposals.md); taught [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| world-wide public authority | PT 137: the universal common good needs an authority of world-wide reach, set up by consent, bound by subsidiarity | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md); taught [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md) |
| error and the person who errs | PT 158: the error is condemned, the person keeps his dignity; a false philosophy differs from the historical movements it inspired (PT 159) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| body politic and state | Maritain: the state is an instrument of the body politic, not the whole | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md); taught [6.4](lessons/06-04-three-readings-catholic-liberal-integralist-progressive.md) |
| socialization | MM 59-67: the multiplying of social ties and associations; an ambivalent fact to be guided, not nationalization | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| new colonialism | MM 171-173: aid must never be a disguised colonialism | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| just reasons | PT 25's condition on the right to emigrate | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md); taught [5.5](lessons/05-05-migration.md) |
| the four orders | PT's four parts: among persons, between persons and authority, among states, in the world community | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md) |
| universal common good | the common good of the whole human family (PT 132-145; GS 26) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md); taught [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md) |
| "intrinsically wrong" | DR 58's judgment on communism (1937); rung 3 | [2.3](lessons/02-03-mater-et-magistra-and-pacem-in-terris.md); taught [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) |
| "constitutive dimension" | *Justice in the World*'s hedged sentence: action for justice "appears" to it a constitutive dimension of preaching the Gospel | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| integral human development | the development of each person and of the whole person, not growth alone (PP 14; MH 82) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md); taught [6.2](lessons/06-02-integral-human-development-and-the-international-order.md) |
| "the new name for peace" | PP's heading over 76-87, said outright in §87; CA 52 has the sentence form | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| expropriation | taking land or goods for the common good, with equitable compensation, "sometimes" and "according to the different cases" (PP 24; GS 71) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md); taught [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| aspiration, movement, ideology | OA 31's three levels of socialism, to be distinguished without forgetting their link | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| integral part, essential part | the two readings of "constitutive" (Torrella against Alfaro); freely disputed | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| manifest, long-standing tyranny | PP 31's narrow exception to its warning against revolution (LC 79; CCC 2243) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-gaudium-et-spes-to-justice-in-the-world.md) |
| human dignity | the worth a person has by existing, created and loved by God; the root of rights | [3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md) |
| ontological, moral, social, existential dignity | *Dignitas Infinita* 7-8's four senses: what a person is (never lost); how she uses her freedom; her living conditions; how she experiences her life | [3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md) |
| inalienable | no one can sign it away; "no man has in this matter power over himself" (RN 40) | [3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md) |
| analytic tool | a distinction a document uses (DI's four senses) that carries no separate assent; assent goes to what it serves | [3.1](lessons/03-01-human-dignity.md) |
| legal (general) justice | Aquinas's justice that directs every virtue's acts to the common good, chiefly in rulers (ST II-II q.58 aa.5-6) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md) |
| three elements of the common good | respect for the person and his rights; social well-being and development; peace, the security of a just order (CCC 1907-1909) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md) |
| "plus" | MH 61: the common good is more than a total of interests, a surplus no adding of individual goods explains | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md) |
| molecule | CA 13: socialism's anthropological error makes the person a molecule of the social organism | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md); taught [4.3](lessons/04-03-socialism-and-liberal-capitalism-each-judged.md) |
| moral force | authority acting within the moral order for the common good, appealing to freedom, not acting despotically (GS 74; PT 48) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md); taught [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-political-community-and-the-limits-of-the-state.md) |
| interdependence | the fact of mutual dependence, which SRS 38 turns into a moral category | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md); taught [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| differs in kind | q.58 a.7 ad 2: the common and the individual good differ formally, as whole and part, not only as many and few | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-common-good.md) |
| the two halves | subsidiarity's restraint (do not absorb) and help (support, coordinate, supply), owed by the same body | [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| substitute function | CA 48: a higher body may stand in for a lesser one that cannot act, as briefly as possible | [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| Social Assistance State | the name the welfare state's critics gave it, which CA 48 takes up in criticizing its excesses | [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md); taught [4.4](lessons/04-04-enterprise-consumption-and-the-economy-of-gift.md) |
| decentralization theorem | Oates: provision tailored to local demand beats a uniform central level unless spillovers or scale economies say otherwise | [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| crowding out | public provision displacing private giving; an economic premise (`public-economics` 1.2), never the principle | [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| TEU art. 5(3) | the EU's subsidiarity rule: two tiers and no duty of help; a secular borrowing | [3.3](lessons/03-03-subsidiarity.md) |
| in solidum | Roman law's joint obligation in which each co-debtor answers for the whole; the root of "solidarity" | [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| structures of sin | personal sins hardened into customs and institutions, which condition others' conduct; sin only by analogy (SRS 36-37; `moral-theology` 3.5) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| social privatism; paternalist social assistance | CiV 58: what subsidiarity becomes without solidarity, and solidarity without subsidiarity | [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| social friendship | FT 99: friendship extended to the whole society; with political charity (FT 180) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-solidarity.md) |
| secondary natural right | FT 120's phrase: private property is a natural right derived from the universal destination of goods | [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| social mortgage | private property carries a social mortgage: an inherent social function (John Paul II's Puebla address, 1979; SRS 42) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
| "twin rocks of shipwreck" | QA 46: individualism and collectivism, the errors on each side of ownership | [3.5](lessons/03-05-the-universal-destination-of-goods-and-private-property.md) |
